#5126. Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Europe's freezers have gone rogue, with supermarkets in nine more countries reporting refrigeration systems that heated up overnight on Monday.

Stores in Belgium, Estonia, Finland, Ireland, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Portugal and Sweden reported problems, and Estonian units were pushed to 50°C (122 °F).

No group has claimed responsibility, so the only ones taking the heat are the freezers.

The outbreak has a patient zero in France, where Finance Minister Roland Lescure told Parliament on Oct. 13 that about 700 of the country's 10,000 supermarkets were affected.

According to France's CERT-FR, hackers forced a continuous defrost cycle, which spoiled food and sparked fires at some stores. CERT-FR said it was aware of numerous compromises of remote control modules made by Danfoss and Copeland.

Groupement Mousquetaires, which owns Intermarché and Netto, counted 300 stores affected, while Cooperative U said 80 of its 2,000 shops were hit. Lescure said food supplies were not at risk but acknowledged the attack "touches the heart of our food security." Some Mousquetaires display cases were still out of service that Tuesday.

Monday's wave hit several chains in Belgium, where Carrefour reported disruption at about 15 of its 700 stores. Sabcobel, a Belgian refrigeration contractor, said evaporators had been switched into defrost mode, and federal prosecutors opened an investigation. Four Delhaize outlets in Luxembourg also reported problems.

Italy's Radenza Group, which runs Coop-branded stores in Sicily, said the attack began at 4 a.m local time. and targeted the remote-control system for frozen food sections. Ireland's Musgrave counted 13 SuperValu stores affected, and Mosqueteiros confirmed incidents at seven Intermarché stores in Portugal.

At 50°C (122 °F), a freezer is well past hot-tub territory and turns basically into a low-powered steam oven or, worse, a bacteria incubator where germs grow quickly if food is left there too long.

In Sweden, Coop said six of its roughly 800 stores were hit and that they ran on 4G connections, making them "more exposed." Ica said a limited number of stores had problems and that the affected shops used an external Danfoss control system. Neither chain has confirmed a cyberattack, and Finland's Kesko says network interference has not been ruled out.

EU Agriculture Commissioner Christophe Hansen called the incidents a "deliberate attempt" to disrupt the food supply chain. Investigators have not said whether Monday's wave is linked to the French attack.

Estonian refrigeration firm Kitman Coldtech said the affected units were older Danfoss models similar to those attacked in France. CERT-FR warns that leaving an end-of-life device without security patches exposed to the internet carries a very high risk of compromise. Researchers at Claroty published flaws in Danfoss AK-SM 800A controllers in August, including an authentication bypass.

Claroty found 2,765 of those devices exposed online, and Danfoss fixed the flaws in firmware R4.3.1. It remains unclear which weaknesses the attackers used, and CERT-FR says the campaigns are probably still ongoing, while Italy's government was set to hold a cybersecurity meeting on Tuesday.

For now, CERT-FR advises operators to take the controllers off the public internet, and Sabcobel's CEO says many older units need physical modifications that require investment. Until those arrive, there is no real fix for the oldest units, which makes your 1998 chest freezer the most secure device on the continent.

#5125. Prediction markets bet on Democratic sweep of House, Senate in midterm elections

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Tuesday, Polymarket users gave Democrats a 76% chance of securing the Senate, and the House a 94% chance.

Ahead of polls closing in the U.S. midterm elections, bettors on prediction market platforms are increasingly pricing in Democrats taking both the House and Senate.

As of early Tuesday afternoon, Polymarket users gave Democrats a 76% chance of securing the Senate, and the House a 94% chance. Similarly, on Kalshi, bettors said Democrats had a 94% chance of controlling the House and a 75% chance for the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election, as well as 35 of the 100 Senate seats.

Prediction markets surged in popularity during the 2024 presidential election, emerging as an alternative barometer of public opinion and drawing comparisons to national polling. Since then, platforms like Polymarket and Kalshi have generated significant revenue and reached multibillion-dollar valuations.

But interest in election-related trading has cooled considerably since 2024, according to a Bloomberg report published Tuesday. Politics accounted for just 1% of trading volume on both Kalshi and Polymarket in October, down from more than 60% during the 2024 election, Bloomberg reported.

A shift in parties is pivotal since Democrats are likely to then lead key committees, driving the agenda. For crypto, that could mean that Democrats such as Elizabeth Warren and Sherrod Brown could lead panels overseeing the digital asset industry.

Experts who spoke with The Block last month said that would mean more oversight over key issues, including President Donald Trump's crypto wealth through his family's World Liberty Financial, and less of an emphasis on crypto legislation.

As for key crypto races, such as Ohio, where incumbent Jon Husted will face off against crypto critic and former Sen. Brown, bettors on Kalshi say Brown has a 71% chance of beating Husted. For other races, including lawmakers focused on crypto legislation, Sen. Cory Booker, D.-N.J. has a 98% chance on Kalshi.

#5124. 8 Things That Should Always Be in Your Field Bag

Game day is only one part of an athlete’s schedule. These versatile picks from Brooks are made for the rest of your routine.

If you’re a regular at your local soccer league, you probably have your game-day uniform down to a science: cleats, socks, shorts, jersey, done. But you also know that training doesn’t end when you leave the field. There are warmups before practice, conditioning sessions, gym workouts, recovery afterwards, and, of course, the inevitable post-game team hangouts while you’re still wearing half your soccer gear. The right off-field pieces can make all of that a little more comfortable.

Brooks has plenty of options that can slot into those in-between moments—from cushioned running shoes for off-field training to lightweight layers for chilly practices to comfortable sweats for the ride home.

Below are eight of our favorite picks for the training, travel, and downtime surrounding the “beautiful game.”

The Glycerin 23 is arguably Brooks’ most versatile off-pitch training shoe. It’s perfect for road-running and training on hard surfaces thanks to plush cushioning and a smooth, stable ride that’s ideal for conditioning runs, or other training away from the field. The breathable upper and roomy fit also make it a comfortable option when you’re on your feet all day or logging miles without a ball at your feet.

For early-morning practices and chilly fall training sessions, lightweight layers that you can easily move in become essential. #5123. France postpones 2027 budget vote, crypto exit-tax proposals scrapped

The National Assembly postponed the vote on the budget’s revenue section for the second time, while the Nov. 17 deadline for the first reading examination nears.

The French National Assembly will continue debating the revenue section of the 2027 state budget on Tuesday instead of holding a planned vote.

According to the Assembly’s end-of-session notice following Monday’s sitting, Tuesday’s agenda includes questions to the government and continued debate on the budget’s revenue section with 758 amendments still pending examination.

This marks the vote’s second delay, after the original Oct. 20 vote was postponed with more than 2,000 amendments awaiting debate, according to French newspaper Le Monde.

The National Assembly has until midnight on Nov. 17 to complete its first-reading examination. If the revenue section is rejected, the government’s original budget proposal will move to the Senate without the amendments adopted by MPs.

Related: EU investors rank crypto above retirement plans for lasting wealth creation: OKX survey

The National Assembly rejected two proposals on Friday that would have applied exit taxes to unrealized crypto gains when taxpayers transferred their tax residence abroad.

Lawmakers rejected the first proposal with 27 votes in favor, 55 against and one abstention, according to the Assembly’s official voting record. The proposal sought to include crypto assets in the country’s existing exit tax regime.

A separate proposal was rejected with 31 votes in favor and 60 against. It would have taxed unrealized gains on household crypto holdings worth more than 800,000 euros ($897,000) when taxpayers transferred their tax residence outside the country, provided they had been tax-resident in France for at least six of the preceding 10 years.

Other crypto-related proposals await debate, including a requirement to report self-custodied crypto holdings worth a combined value of at least 100,000 euros ($112,000) to tax authorities, based on their value at year-end.

Another proposal would exempt gains on crypto payments for goods and services from tax if the total value of those payments doesn’t exceed 1,000 euros ($1,120) during the tax year.

Lawmakers approved a proposal on Oct. 16 to tax gains when crypto assets are converted into fiat-pegged stablecoins. The measure has not become law.

#5122. CZ calls for crypto security alliance as AI-driven attacks accelerate after Binance hack

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Changpeng Zhao says companies need AI-based defenses and an industry alliance to share lessons and freeze stolen funds faster, in his first interview since the Oct. 29 hack.

Binance cofounder and former CEO Changpeng "CZ" Zhao said AI is accelerating cyberattacks and argued companies should invest in AI-based defenses, security personnel, and partnerships with AI security providers.

In his first public interview since the Oct. 29 hack of Binance, Zhao told the Talking Tokens podcast that the threat extends beyond individual companies to the broader crypto industry and global financial system, given crypto's connections to stablecoins, tokenized stocks, real-world assets, and traditional finance.

"The speed of hacking is going to pick up because of AI capabilities [being] so strong now. At the same time, we've got to use more AI to defend. The only way to fight a monster is to kind of become that monster."

The comments come amid a string of security incidents across the crypto industry, including a separate AI-assisted breach at blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis and a $46 million security incident at Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin.

Zhao said he learned of the incident around midnight in the UAE and focused on communicating with the community while Binance's team worked to contain the attack and protect users. He attributed relatively calm market conditions and subsequent net inflows to the company's response, financial capacity and user trust. He described security incidents as preventable with sufficient effort and attention, while warning that organizations can become complacent.

Earlier Tuesday, Binance said hackers used a fake job interview to compromise an employee's computer before leveraging an internal AI tool in the attack. The exchange also said it detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026.

Zhao called for an industry alliance to share lessons and best practices, conduct cross-audits and help freeze stolen funds more quickly. He views security as an ongoing process rather than a milestone that must be reached before adoption.

"As an industry, I encourage [...] forming some kind of industry alliance on security to help each other learn from each other's mistakes, share the best practices, cross audits, help each other freeze funds very quickly, etc."

Zhao said he had not spoken with President Donald Trump or other U.S. officials about the incident, but that Binance and the broader crypto industry would welcome government assistance. Trump has said the U.S. was "looking into it."

"What's bad for crypto on this scale has real economic impact elsewhere," Zhao said. "So you cannot just say, 'Look, this is one specific problem for one specific sector or for one specific company.' It impacts the company, impacts the industry, impacts global finance. It's all interconnected, and I think, given the size of impact, it's definitely at the national security level as well."

Zhao expressed optimism that AI could help identify software vulnerabilities and strengthen defenses, while blockchain could support faster transactions facilitated by AI.

#5120. Build Strong Bones in Just Four Weeks With This Plan: Download the PDF Today

Bulletproof your bones with this easy-to-follow workout plan.

With aging comes a number of changes—from shifts in muscle mass and hormones to changes in sleep and bone health. Bone density naturally begins to decline around age 35, but there’s plenty you can do to support strong, healthy bones at every stage of life.

Introducing the WH+ 4 Weeks to Stronger Bones workout plan. This comprehensive program gives you the tools to build strength, support bone density, and keep your body resilient at any age.

Each week includes three strength workouts, two bone-health-focused cardio circuits (one optional), one active recovery day, and one rest day. The typical schedule will look something like this:

Saturday: Bone-Strength Circuit 2 (optional)

Each workout is designed to challenge your major muscle groups and boost your bone health in the process. All workouts are outlined in the PDF, including:

Download the PDF below, and save it to your computer or phone for easy access. Then, use it to follow your workouts, log your numbers, and watch your strength build over time.

The only way to truly build bone strength is to load your bones consistently. Repetition is what helps them become stronger over time, so it’s important for you to find a workout schedule that you know you’ll stick with. Although each workout is assigned to a specific day, feel free to adjust the schedule to fit your life. What matters most is choosing workout days that are realistic for you and following through.

Your body—and bones!—need the right fuel to thrive. Bones are made up of a mix of calcium and other nutrients (in fact, 99 percent of your body’s calcium is stored in your bones!). That makes a nutrient-rich diet a vital part of supporting bone health. Use the nutrition cheat sheet included in this program to help you choose calcium-rich foods and build bone-friendly meals.

Fill in your workout charts as you complete each session. As the weeks go by, pay attention to how the exercises feel, and choose more challenging weights and exercises when you’re ready—and watch your strength, balance, and power all improve. Each gain is a sign your hard work is paying off, and that you’re making meaningful steps toward stronger bones.

#5119. Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Hackers from North Korea are notoriously effective at stealing crypto, with one estimate attributing 60% of all losses last year to DPRK-linked groups. These hackers enlist several different vectors of attack, making it all the more difficult to spot them in the act.

Last week, it took just 20 minutes for alleged North Korean hackers to drain $6.3 billion from Binance, in what is the biggest crypto hack on record. The attackers compromised a Binance engineer's work computer through a fake job interview, used an internal AI tool to gain control of other engineers' computers, and then used stolen credentials to hijack an internal wallet signing service, the exchange said.

In the past, North Korean hackers have been known to get hired at crypto companies under fake identities, attack through third-party infrastructure, and poison widely used code libraries. More recently, they've started using AI to make all of it more convincing.

Here are some examples of the methods that North Korean hackers are known to use in crypto attacks.

Binance chief security officer Jimmy Su told Decrypt in August 2025 that the exchange throws out suspected North Korean resumes every day, and that "almost all the large DPRK hacks," at the time, had involved a fake employee helping facilitate the attack.

Su said there were tell-tale signs in resumes that indicate the applicant was from North Korea, but the exchange was not willing to reveal the specifics on the red flags. Sometimes, though, malicious actors get through to the interview stage, where the attackers use deepfakes to change their face and voice.

"The only real good detection is that they almost always have a slow internet connection," he told Decrypt. "What's happening is that the translation and the voice changer are working during the call … that's why they are always delayed."

Those who do get hired tend to be suspiciously good at the job. Su said North Korean workers are often among a company's top performers, likely because several people may be sharing one role across time zones.

The threat has not let up. Binance said its security team detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threat cases in 2026, using techniques ranging from fraudulent job interviews to fake video conferencing apps.

Attackers also flip the scam around and pose as recruiters to target people who already work at high-profile crypto companies. Often, the attacker will claim there's a video or audio issue during the interview and send the victim a link to update Zoom, which infects their device with malware

"The DPRK group will [also] try to schedule calls with the external-facing employees," Su told Decrypt. "Either as a DeFi project or investment firm. Worst yet, they'll be recruiting them for a high-level job, paying twice, three times as much, just to get them onto an interview."

This is how the Binance attack began, according to the exchange. In September, a Binance software engineer was approached through a legitimate job-hunting platform by someone posing as a project director, who set up two interviews. In the first, a purported HR representative built trust over a live video call by sharing a social media account full of personal photos and videos, an approach Binance called "more advanced and deceptive than previously seen."

In the second, held on a chat app, the engineer was told to download an "assignment" onto his work computer. The code repository contained previously unknown malware that ran during setup, swapped a legitimate developer tool for a malicious version and connected to an attacker-controlled server, evading Binance's security tools.

One North Korea-linked group infected at least 30,000 devices in more than 100 countries between roughly December 2025 and July 2026 using similar methods. It took funds or credentials from more than 7,000 crypto wallets and moved around $10.71 million to North Korea.

The group, known as WaterPlum or Contagious Interview, posed as AI, crypto, or NFT companies and set candidates a technical interview or coding task that required downloading malware-filled files. Some used AI face-swapping software during interviews before cutting their video and asking the candidate to do the same, blaming a bad connection.

Binance's Su also told Decrypt that North Korean hackers poison public code libraries, ranking it as the second most common method of attack—behind fake employees.

Node Package Manager (NPM) packages are collections of reusable, open-source code that developers frequently use. Attackers can clone these packages and slip in a small line of malicious code while keeping its original function. That means that a victim could use this poisoned code library and not notice, with the malicious code embedding itself deeper over time as more is built on top of it.

In September 2025, software security firm ReversingLabs found two NPM packages that used Ethereum smart contracts to download malware, which the firm said it hadn't seen before. The packages were tied to a wider network of GitHub repositories, mostly branded as crypto trading bots, with thousands of commits and stars that ReversingLabs thinks were mostly faked.

The tactic formed part of a “sophisticated campaign” of malicious actors attempting to hack users via poisoned blockchain-related public code libraries, a vector of attack that Binance has previously attributed to North Korean hackers.

To counter the threat, Su said, Binance makes employees go through NPM libraries with a fine-tooth comb.

Tampered developer tools also played a part in the Binance hack. In June, a member of Binance's wallet DevOps team downloaded a tampered installation of Homebrew, a popular software installer, while trying to install an AI coding tool he had found through a search engine, the exchange said. Its security team caught the problem, replaced the device and rotated his credentials, but the incident exposed the engineer and his colleagues on the wallet infrastructure team to the attackers.

Before Binance, the unwanted record for the largest crypto hack belonged to Bybit, which lost $1.4 billion in February 2025. Cybersecurity firms revealed that North Korean hackers pulled off the attack by planting malicious code into the infrastructure of Safe—a crypto wallet provider used by the exchange.

The security firms said malicious JavaScript code was directly injected into Safe's online infrastructure. Though, it was unclear how the attackers managed to do this.

Bybit's infrastructure "was not compromised," the exchange told Decrypt, and the hack came purely because a third party was exploited.

The Binance attack targeted the exchange's own systems. On October 29, the attacker used stolen credentials to deploy a container and gain unauthorized access to an internal wallet signing service, which let it initiate transactions outside Binance's normal withdrawal process. On Ethereum, it used the signing service to exploit EIP-7702, a mechanism that lets an account delegate execution rights to a smart contract, giving an attacker-controlled contract partial control over a Binance hot wallet.

Those on-chain authorizations kept working even after Binance suspended withdrawals. Because the hot wallet used multi-party computation, there was no single private key Binance could use to revoke them quickly, the exchange said.

Every single one of the above mentioned attack vectors can be turbo-charged with artificial intelligence tools.

Early this year, Google warned of an AI-powered malware campaign targeting crypto companies coming from North Korea. Similarly, Binance's Su also mentioned that AI tools can be used to increase the effectiveness of attacks.

Concerns around AI-powered hacks have risen in recent months, with Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin saying in October that it's "plausible" AI could break encryption within the next two years. It came amid an explosion in AI-driven maths, with OpenAI claiming to have cracked a 90-year-old maths problem in a matter of days. Experts urged the industry to prepare for AI-powered attacks on encryption.

There are no confirmed reports of North Korean hackers using AI to break encryption. But given how quickly they've adopted AI elsewhere, the industry may have to prepare for that next.

#5117. SEC division views Canton Coin as digital commodity in no-action letter

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission's Division of Corporation Finance said it views Canton Coin (CC) as a digital commodity and will not recommend enforcement action over its programmatic issuance under certain conditions.

In a no-action letter published Tuesday, the division said Canton Coin derives its value from the programmatic operation of the Canton Network and supply-and-demand dynamics, rather than expectations of profit from the managerial efforts of others. It also cited the network's lack of a central party.

"Based on the facts presented, the Division will not recommend enforcement action to the Commission if, in reliance on your opinion as counsel, Canton Coin is minted programmatically by Super Validators, Validators and application providers, in the manner and under the circumstances described in your letter, without registration under Section 5 of the Securities Act or Section 12(g) of the Exchange Act," Deputy Chief Counsel Jonathan Ingram wrote.

The letter reflects the Division of Corporation Finance's enforcement position based on the specific facts and representations submitted by Canton and does not express a legal conclusion on the issues presented, including whether Canton Coin is a security.

In May, 21Shares became the first U.S. ETF issuer to offer direct exposure to CC, the network's native utility token.

In June, Digital Asset raised $355 million in a funding round led by Andreessen Horowitz's a16z crypto fund to expand the Canton ecosystem and accelerate institutional adoption of its blockchain infrastructure.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

"Incredible. @CantonNetwork just received no action relief from the SEC for its network token," Jennings wrote Tuesday on X. "The @SECGov's token taxonomy from March and follow up FAQs have now created a clear pathway out of securities laws—Network tokens are digital commodities."

#5116. Binance’s net inflows turn positive at $2.3B as withdrawals expand

Binance led exchanges in 24-hour net inflows, reversing Monday’s heavy outflows as it restored access to funds after its $6.3 billion hack.

Binance recorded $2.3 billion in net inflows over the past 24 hours as it expanded withdrawals following last week’s $6.3 billion hack.

The exchange led centralized exchanges tracked by DefiLlama in 24-hour net inflows. Bitfinex recorded about $280 million and Bitget about $127 million over the same period.

“By 03:00 UTC, we are seeing hourly deposits exceed withdrawals, reflecting improving user activity and stabilizing flows,” Binance said Tuesday on X.

The inflows mark a reversal from Monday, when Binance recorded more than $7 billion in 24-hour net outflows. OKX led exchanges with about $445 million in net inflows at that time.

Binance suspended withdrawals on Friday after Thursday’s $6.3 billion hack while it investigated the breach and conducted security checks. It began restoring withdrawals at 06:00 UTC on Sunday and reopened withdrawals for some fiat currencies on Monday, as outflows started to slow down.

In a Sunday recovery update, Binance said it had replaced approximately 95% of the affected assets. The remaining 5% consisted of less liquid tokens that took longer to acquire through market and over-the-counter purchases.

The exchange has pledged to cover the losses using company resources and protection funds without passing them on to customers.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation attributed the attack to North Korea on Saturday. North Korea denied responsibility Tuesday, with Kim Yo Jong, the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, dismissing the allegation as “absurd rhetoric.”

#5113. Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Two of Elon Musk’s companies are now keeping their shares off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Tesla and SpaceX are among six new objecting issuers listed in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Monday. Amazon, Netflix, Lam Research, and SoFi Technologies are the others. The additions bring the total to 56 companies.

The list stood at 50 in the venue’s Oct. 30 notice, when Nvidia, Costco, and Nike led the biggest batch yet. That followed 38 on Oct. 28, after Walmart joined, and nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

The new names deepen the roster of megacap holdouts. Amazon, Tesla, and Netflix join Microsoft, Alphabet, and Nvidia, while SoFi, a fintech company that offers crypto trading, adds a company with its own stake in digital assets. SpaceX, which trades under the ticker SPCX, rounds out the batch.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party.

If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. The objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC.

To trade, users must pass identity and sanctions checks and hold a nontransferable “soulbound” token that the venue’s smart contracts verify before every transaction.

Apple is now one of the few megacap names still missing from the list. And while objections bar OKXICE from listing third-party tokenized versions of a company’s stock, they don’t close every door.

The notice says the venue can also list shares tokenized by the issuers themselves, though it doesn’t identify any, and that tokenized stocks it offers aren’t restricted to trading on OKXICE alone.

#5112. NEAR token sinks 7% as proposal to cut annual token issuance passes

The proposed issuance cut would prevent 11.8 million NEAR from entering circulation over two years, worth about $45 million at today’s price.

Near Protocol’s NEAR token fell by roughly 7% on Tuesday, despite community approval of a proposal to gradually reduce the maximum annual issuance rate from 2.5% to 1.6% over 24 months.

NEAR traded at $3.82 as of 11:06 am UTC, extending its weekly decline to roughly 13%, according to CoinMarketCap.

The latest drop followed Monday’s approval of HSP-029, which received support from 99.9% of participating voting power. About 3.28 million vote-escrowed NEAR (veNEAR) tokens were cast in favor, according to the NEAR House of Stake governance platform.

The proposal would reduce long-term token dilution while lowering projected gross staking yields from 5.2% to 3.3% by the end of the 24-month period. It estimates that issuance would fall by 11.8 million NEAR over two years and 66.1 million NEAR over six years.

The proposal remains subject to the Security Council’s 14-day veto window. If not vetoed, it will be ratified, followed by a 90-day grace period before the issuance reduction begins. Implementation also requires validators to adopt the necessary technical upgrade.

#5109. South Korea’s ruling party formally proposes deferral of crypto income tax

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

South Korea's ruling party leader has officially proposed that the government further delay the crypto tax.

Democratic Party leader Kim Min-seok said at a party supreme council meeting at the National Assembly that the decision followed consultations through the Strategy and Finance Committee, government-party talks, and meetings with crypto asset businesses, local news outlet News1 reported on Wednesday.

Kim stated that party members agreed that the country should first enact the Digital Asset Basic Act, a comprehensive legal framework for crypto, and give companies and investors time to adapt to new rules.

The tax plan, currently scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027, would impose a 22% tax (including local tax) on annual gains from the transfer or lending of crypto assets above a basic deduction of 2.5 million won ($1,868.7). If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

The ruling party leader acknowledged past deferrals of the tax and said that this time the party would set a clear deferral period while thoroughly preparing prerequisites, including use of overseas transaction information, IT system verification and taxpayer guidance.

Last week, South Korea's presidential office was reportedly discussing postponement of the levy until 2029 to align with the U.S. timeline for joining the OECD's Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework.

#5107. Brazil's Biggest Bitcoin Exchange Says It Will Refund Users After a $40 Million Hack

Brazil's Biggest Bitcoin Exchange Says It Will Refund Users After a $40 Million Hack

Brazil's largest crypto exchange says its users won't pay for its hack. Mercado Bitcoin, which lost tens of millions of dollars on Monday, says the losses are its problem, not its customers'.

An exchange holds your coins the way a bank holds your cash, and when one gets robbed, the customers are often the ones left waiting to see what they get back.

The exchange spotted a breach early on November 2 and froze deposits and withdrawals. “We identified and quickly contained a security incident in one of our systems early this morning,” Mercado Bitcoin said in a Portuguese-language statement on X. “As a preventive protection measure, deposits and withdrawals are temporarily suspended.”

It said no customer lost money and no data leaked. It has not said how the attackers got in.

How much was taken depends on who is counting. Security firm Hypernative flagged more than $40 million in suspicious transfers across at least 10 blockchains, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana, and Tron.

Ontem (2/11), identificamos um incidente de segurança. Acionamos na hora nossos protocolos de segurança e governança e informamos as autoridades.…

— Mercado Bitcoin (@MercadoBitcoin) November 3, 2026

The exchange's own number is lower. “Right now the value of the assets involved is about $38 million,” Mercado Bitcoin said in a Portuguese-language update on X. “After a quick action with the crypto community and strategic partners, including Tether, Circle, Paxos, BRL1 and Chainalysis, we have already blocked $11 million.”

Others put it higher. Blockchain analysts at Arkham traced at least $46 million, while Chainalysis says about $49 million, with nearly $10 million frozen. The exchange has not said whether its $38 million includes the frozen amount.

Tether, which issues the USDT stablecoin (a token pegged to the dollar), froze more than $4.5 million in USDT tied to the incident, CEO Paolo Ardoino said on X.

A freeze only stops the tokens from moving. It doesn't return them to their owner, which is why the exchange's promise to cover losses carries the weight here.

On October 29, hackers drained more than $6 billion from Binance, the world's biggest exchange, and the FBI blamed North Korea's TraderTraitor group. Mercado Bitcoin has not linked its incident to that attack.

Binance made the same pledge as Mercado Bitcoin. “Binance will absorb the financial impact of the stolen funds, not our users,” CEO Richard Teng wrote on X, adding that about 95% of the affected assets had been replenished.

Neither promise can be checked from the outside. A guarantee to cover losses is only as good as the money behind it, and exchanges don't have to show customers their books.

For now, Mercado Bitcoin deposits and withdrawals remain suspended with no restart date. The exchange says it will post updates on X and resume service once full security is restored.

#5105. Moscow Exchange to launch Bitcoin, Ether and USDT trading in December

Moscow Exchange plans to launch crypto trading on Dec. 1 under Russia’s new law, with annual purchase limits for non-qualified investors.

Moscow Exchange plans to launch trading in Bitcoin, Ether and Tether’s USDT on Dec. 1, opening what it describes as Russia’s first organized digital currency market.

The exchange said Monday that pilot trades would begin as participants join the platform. Trades will use its existing stock-market infrastructure and be cleared through the National Clearing Center (NCC), with trading hours from 6:50 am to 11:50 pm Moscow time.

The venue will initially support same-day and next-day settlement, as well as swap transactions. Non-qualified investors must pass a test before purchasing crypto and will be limited to purchases of 300,000 rubles (about $3,700) annually per broker, according to the exchange.

The launch follows a Russian crypto law whose main provisions took effect Sept. 1. The law permits regulated trading but maintains a ban on cryptocurrency payments for goods and services in Russia.

Moscow Exchange and the NCC have been under US sanctions since June 2024, when the UK also sanctioned the exchange. The European Union added it to its sanctions list in July 2026.

Sergey Mendeleev, founder of Russian cross-border payments platform Exved, told Cointelegraph he doubted the proposed market would attract significant demand.

“Neither the global nor the local market will notice anything at all,” he said, questioning who would trade on a sanctioned exchange.

Mendeleev also predicted uncompetitive trading conditions, comparing the prospective offering to precious metals accounts at Russian lender Sberbank, where customers trade account balances without taking possession of physical metal.

Related: Russia to ban direct peer-to-peer crypto trades starting July 2027

He warned that investors could face “absolutely outrageous spreads,” referring to the difference between buying and selling prices. “For ordinary non-qualified investors, this is an entirely unattractive and unprofitable proposition,” he said.

Mendeleev saw potential for the platform to support cross-border payments. He said a venue where importers and exporters could buy and sell crypto directly with one another would be welcome, but questioned whether adding another intermediary would be competitive in a market where traders negotiate over price differences as small as 0.05%.

“I hope this will mark a genuine breakthrough for foreign trade,” he said.

Block raised its full-year gross profit forecast to $12.63 billion, which would represent a 22% year-on-year growth.

Jack Dorsey-led fintech firm Block Inc. Block, Inc. (XYZ) saw its shares jump 6.2% in after-hours trading on Tuesday after reporting stronger third-quarter earnings and raising its full-year profit outlook.

Block posted a net income of $566 million in the third quarter of this year, up from $462 million a year earlier, according to its latest quarterly report released Tuesday. It generated $6.72 billion in total revenue, up from $6.12 billion in the same quarter last year.

#5102. Women’s Heart Health Just Got a Major Update. Here’s What Doctors Want You to Know

This is a much-needed and overdue change.

Big news in female heart health: Four major health organizations have come together to create a shared framework and clinical guidance for women's heart health across several different life stages.

The American Heart Association, American Academy of Pediatrics, American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG), and Society for Maternal-Fetal Medicine came together to outline the “female life stage" framework to protect heart health from puberty through menopause and beyond. The framework (which essentially acts as a classification system for medical providers) was just published in the journal Circulation.

This is a big deal for many reasons, including acknowledging that female heart health and risk factors change over a woman's life. "This has been a long time coming," says Nicole Weinberg, MD, a cardiologist at Pacific Heart Institute’s Women’s Heart Center in Santa Monica, California.

According to the American Heart Association, cardiovascular disease is the leading cause of death in women, claiming more lives than breast cancer, lung cancer, and chronic lung disease combined. But only four in 10 women are aware that cardiovascular disease is the biggest threat to their health.

This new framework separates cardiovascular risk factors, along with care needs, throughout the different stages of a woman's life. Here's what you need to know.

Meet the experts: Sadiya Khan, MD, chair of the collaborative writing group and professor of Cardiovascular Epidemiology at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine; Jennifer Haythe, MD, co-director of the Women’s Heart Center at NewYork-Presbyterian/Columbia University Irving Medical Center; and Nicole Weinberg, MD, cardiologist at Pacific Heart Institute’s Women’s Heart Center in Santa Monica, California

The Female Life Stage framework is defined by hormone changes specific to girls and women at different stages of life, hence the name. The framework also recognizes pregnancy as an event that can impact cardiovascular health at any or several life stages. Here's a breakdown of each:

Adopting this new way of classifying female life stages “offers a transformative opportunity to equitably improve cardiovascular health for all girls and women," the researchers wrote in the review article.

This has never been done before. “For the first time, four major medical groups agree that a woman’s heart health is shaped by every stage of her life, from her first period to menopause," says Jennifer Haythe, MD, co-director of the Women’s Heart Center at NewYork-Presbyterian/Columbia University Irving Medical Center.

This helps address the "fragmentation of care" that can happen between ob-gyns, cardiologists, and other specialists who care for women, says Sadiya Khan, MD, chair of the collaborative writing group and professor of Cardiovascular Epidemiology at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine. "We're all speaking different languages and we're all speaking different levels of care, yet they're all connected," she says.

The framework is a "shared playbook" that doctors from different specialties can use to guide patient care, along with a language that's understandable and easy to communicate for everyone, Dr. Khan says. "It's really connecting those aspects of health that we think are disconnected," she says.

This new system helps to bridge the gap between viewing cardiovascular care as something completely separate from women's health. "This is meant to reframe cardiovascular health as part of a woman's care," says Dr. Khan. "Any of these things may influence the other."

Weinberg says the framework will create "a more unified view" of women's health. "By creating a road map that is shared by pediatricians, cardiologists, ob-gyns, and maternal-fetal medicine doctors, things like PMOS, irregular periods, pregnancy complications, and metabolic shifts in perimenopause will be treated as real heart health signals, so early warning signs aren’t missed," Haythe says.

Carina Hsieh, MPH, is the deputy features editor of Women’s Health. She has more than a decade’s worth of experience working in media and has covered everything from beauty, fashion, travel, lifestyle, pets, to health.

She began her career as an intern in the fashion closet at Cosmopolitan where she worked her way up to Senior Sex & Relationships Editor. While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

#5101. What’s at stake for crypto as the US votes in the 2026 midterms?

Crypto groups spent about $176 million ahead of the US midterms, with control of Congress and the future of crypto legislation at stake.

US voters head to the polls in midterm elections on Tuesday that could shift control of Congress and shape the future of cryptocurrency regulation.

Crypto industry groups have spent roughly $176 million across more than 100 races this election cycle, according to an Oct. 27 Reuters report.

Coinbase, Ripple and Andreessen Horowitz are among the main financiers of the Fairshake political network.

The results could reshape the prospects for the stalled CLARITY Act and determine which lawmakers lead committees overseeing the industry.

Republicans enter Tuesday’s midterms controlling both chambers of Congress, with 219 seats in the House of Representatives and 53 in the Senate, according to the Blockchain Association’s election dashboard.

Democrats are seeking to overturn those majorities, with prediction markets favoring the party to win the House and Senate, according to Kalshi data at the time of publishing.

Voters will decide all 435 House seats and 35 Senate races, determining which party controls Congress for the final two years of President Donald Trump’s second term.

Democratic control could bring more congressional scrutiny of Trump-linked crypto businesses and regulators’ decisions. Control of the Senate would also affect confirmation of future presidential nominees to regulatory posts.

Ohio’s Senate race between Republican Jon Husted and Democrat Sherrod Brown is a major test of crypto’s political influence. Industry groups have reportedly spent more than $50 million opposing Brown, a longtime crypto critic who could return to a senior Senate committee role.

In Nebraska, Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts faces independent Dan Osborn. Ricketts, a Senate Banking Committee member who has supported crypto-friendly legislation, has benefited from nearly $11 million in spending by Fairshake, according to the Washington Examiner.

Texas is another race to watch, with Republican Ken Paxton facing Democrat James Talarico in a closely contested Senate election. As Cointelegraph previously reported, the Winklevoss-backed Digital Freedom Fund has spent money supporting Paxton.

A shift in congressional control could delay the CLARITY Act, although the crypto market structure bill has already struggled under Republican leadership.

The legislation would divide oversight of crypto markets between the Securities and Exchange Commission and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, as well as establish rules for intermediaries. The Senate failed to advance it in a 49–50 procedural vote on Sept. 15, short of the 60 votes required.

Democratic Sen. Mark Warner cited insufficient safeguards against Trump profiting from his administration’s crypto policies. Republican sponsors said their final draft incorporated substantial ethics concessions sought by Democrats.

“A change in control of Congress would most likely reset the timetable for the CLARITY Act,” Christian Davies, group head of innovation and digital capital at Relm Insurance, told Cointelegraph.

Related: Crypto PAC spending before midterms possibly obscured after reported FEC hack

Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, agreed that the challenges extend beyond party control.

“The CLARITY Act was already struggling to get through the Senate with Republicans in control, so clearly the problem goes beyond which party holds the majority,” Kirkley said.

The industry has already secured a federal stablecoin framework through the GENIUS Act, which became law in July 2025.

Bitcoin’s (BTC) price history around US midterms has attracted attention on social media, but analysts caution that past declines offer little guidance on what could happen after Tuesday’s vote.

Bitcoin fell below earlier yearly lows after the 2014, 2018 and 2022 midterms. Source: ManGana

“I’d be careful reading much into Bitcoin’s record after past midterms. There haven’t been enough cycles to call it a pattern, and the market is built differently now: ETF flows, institutional portfolios and global liquidity move the price far more than they used to,” Michael Heinrich, CEO and co-founder of 0G Labs, told Cointelegraph.

Related: From Uptober to Hacktober: Are Bitcoin’s seasonal patterns becoming obsolete?

GSN’s Kirkley said institutional flows, interest rate expectations and market liquidity were more immediate factors affecting Bitcoin.

“Bitcoin doesn’t need a particular election result to succeed. It needs capital, confidence and a macro environment that gives investors a reason to take risk again,” Kirkley said.

#5100. CFTC Lets Traders Bet on Stocks With Crypto-Style Perpetual Futures

CFTC Lets Traders Bet on Stocks With Crypto-Style Perpetual Futures

The U.S. derivatives regulator has opened the door to perpetual futures on individual stocks, importing one of crypto’s most popular trading instruments into traditional equity markets for the first time.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission said Tuesday that “equity perpetual futures,” or perpetual contracts tied to individual stocks, can now begin trading on CFTC-registered exchanges.

Perpetual futures, long a staple of crypto markets, are derivatives that track an asset’s price but carry no expiration date, letting traders hold leveraged positions indefinitely as long as they meet margin and funding requirements. Until now, they’ve been largely off-limits to U.S. traders.

Chairman Michael Selig framed the move as the latest step in a campaign to bring perpetual products onshore.

“In my inaugural remarks, I directed the CFTC to use the tools at its disposal to onshore perpetual derivative products so that they can flourish in the U.S., subject to appropriate safeguards,” he said, calling the stock contracts an extension of the agency’s earlier approval of the first true Bitcoin perpetual futures on a regulated U.S. exchange.

That milestone, as Decrypt reported, saw the CFTC clear Bitcoin perpetual futures for Kalshi earlier this year.

Selig returned to a familiar argument: that regulated U.S. venues are safer than the offshore platforms traders have otherwise had to use.

Bringing these products into regulated markets “ensures that American investors and traders can access these products within the protections of the CFTC’s proven regulatory framework, rather than being forced to turn to offshore, and often unregulated, venues,” he said.

It’s a theme he pressed just last week when he used the record Binance hack to argue offshore exchanges need U.S. oversight.

The equity perps were deemed approved as security futures products under the Commodity Futures Modernization Act of 2000, placing them under the joint oversight of the CFTC and the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Selig credited a historic memorandum of understanding the two agencies signed in March for ending the jurisdictional limbo that had, in SEC Chairman Paul Atkins’s words, stranded such products in a regulatory “no man’s land.”

The approval arrives amid a rush to bring perps onshore. The CFTC has floated plans to bring crypto exchanges under federal oversight, dismissed a CME lawsuit challenging crypto perpetual futures, and cleared the way for Coinbase, which recently filed to list single-stock perps on names like Apple, Tesla and Nvidia.

For Selig, the goal is explicit: ensuring “the new frontier of finance is built in the United States of America.”

#5098. Former Sen. Sherrod Brown defeats Jon Husted, dealing a blow to crypto PAC Fairshake

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Earlier this year, Fairshake threw millions of dollars into the Ohio race, positioning itself against former Sen. Sherrod Brown.

Former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown is taking back his spot in the Senate, leaving crypto political action committee Fairshake with the bag as he beats out incumbent Republican Jon Husted.

On Tuesday night, Brown defeated Husted in the race for Ohio's Senate seat, dealing a setback to Fairshake, a top crypto political action committee that committed tens of millions of dollars to keep Brown out of Washington.

Earlier this year, Fairshake threw $30 million into the Ohio race, positioning itself against Brown. The crypto PAC and the broader crypto industry played a significant role in the 2024 elections, spending millions to influence political outcomes.

This is not Fairshake's first time spending against Brown. He was previously the chair of the Senate Banking Committee, where he set the agenda for crypto and called for a crackdown on the use of cryptocurrency to fund terrorism and evade sanctions.

Then in 2024, Fairshake spent $12 million to back crypto-friendly Republican Bernie Moreno against Brown, defeating him. Since Democrats regained control of the Senate, Brown could become chair of the Senate Agriculture Committee, which has jurisdiction over digital assets and prediction markets, among other things. If he is chair, sources previously told The Block that Brown would be unlikely to introduce any crypto legislation.

Sen. Husted, who is endorsed by crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, has voted yes for a few crypto bills, including the broad crypto legislation called the Clarity Act. Husted has an A rating from the group and gave Brown an F.

Other candidates backed by Stand With Crypto such as Democrat Chris Pappas in New Hampshire won their race.

Other pivotal Senate wins include Democratic Sens. Cory Booker and Mark Warner, both of whom have been a part of the negotiations for crypto legislation. Senate Democratic candidate in Texas, James Talarico, received an A rating from crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto for his previous pro-crypto statements and his vote to create a strategic bitcoin reserve in Texas. Talarico also won his race on Tuesday against Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton.

#5096. Reigning Champ Hellen Obiri Didn’t Change Much About This Year’s Prep for the NYC Marathon

Her secret weapon to a successful training block? A lot of naps and even more tea.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Speaking from personal experience, the closer you get to marathon day, the louder the worries become. Can I really make it to the start line when I’m so sore and tired all the time? Are my long runs and weekly mileage on par with everyone else’s? Do I even want to be doing this anymore? That is, unless you’re the reigning champion, Hellen Obiri.

This weekend, she’ll be making her return to the New York City Marathon—which she won last year in a course record of 2 hours, 19 minutes, 51 seconds. When I chatted with the On athlete a few weeks out from the race, she had just wrapped up a morning session of 12.4 miles and still had another five on deck later that day. It seems like a lot, but “the evening session is always very easy,” she assured me.

Obiri, 36, has won four marathons (NYC and Boston, two times each), and though she knows everyone will be watching to see if she’ll do it again, she doesn’t seem affected by the pressure. “When you're the reigning champion, everybody's looking at you and wondering what you can do,” she says. “You have that pressure of, ‘How can I win back to back?’”

But Obiri knows that in a marathon, anything can happen. It’s a long distance, and she’s competing against the best of the best—so she’s not counting a single person out. “For me, [the key] is to keep training well, and to keep that in my mind: I'm the defending champion,” Obiri says. “The most important thing is to stay safe and to stay healthy, and then everything's possible.”

Here’s what she’s doing to prepare for race day—and her advice to those heading to a starting line soon.

Because, well, it worked so well in 2025. “Sometimes we do study who is in the race,” she admits, though she didn’t name drop any one contender she has her eye on this year.

Each week, she logs over 100 miles, and gives herself two to three days to recover after a long run or speed session. (Recovery days still often consist of easy miles, though.) Her longest run heading into November 1? Twenty-four miles. Even the best of the best don’t run the full distance until race day.

She also preps—both physically and mentally—for pushing through particularly hard moments. Will she still have enough in the tank to keep pushing the pace with her speedy competitors come mile 20? What about in those last 100 meters if she needs to dig deep for a sprint finish?

It’s her second year working with On Athletic Club coach Laura Thweatt, who also boasts an impressive history with the NYC Marathon, placing eighth in 2021. Having a coach who has also raced New York has made a huge difference, Obiri says, because she knows what it’s like to train for this specific race and also what it takes to do well. Plus, Thweatt is able to join her on runs (though she won’t be running the race herself). “She’s my training partner,” Obiri says.

Thweatt is also Obiri’s first-ever female coach, and that’s not to be underestimated, either. “To have a female coach, you can't hide something from her,” Obiri says. “She already knows everything, what you're going through in your mind, in your body.”

Beyond running, Obiri does strength work three times per week, focusing on lower-body muscles like her calves and glutes, but surprisingly, she’s pretty focused on her arms, too. At her level, everything counts, including how strong your arms are when you’re swinging them during a race—an area that needs some improvement, she says.

She naps. Often. After her morning session, Obiri takes a snooze. Then, she heads to lunch. After lunch? She’s napping again. “I sleep a lot,” she says. Obiri estimates that she spends anywhere from five to six hours a day napping.

For Obiri, the basics—sleeping, eating, and hydrating—are the most important things for feeling her best. And when it comes to hydration, Obiri’s not getting caught up in the electrolyte craze or downing gallons of water. Instead, she relies on tea as her main source for fluids. She drinks all different kinds, but will make it in a big batch, complete with milk and three spoonfuls of sugar per cup.

“I’m addicted to tea with sugar,” she says. She has about seven cups per day, including before a run. It’s an easy way to get some extra carbs in, for sure. Along with her tea, before her 6:45 a.m. run, Obiri will have two slices of bread and a banana. “I can’t go on my runs with an empty stomach,” she says.

“The most important thing is to listen to your body,” she says. You might be feeling the excitement right off the bat or trying to keep up with the crowd, but remember to pay attention to how you feel and not go too hard too soon. “Don't go crazy because it's a marathon—it's a long journey—and needs patience,” she says. Make sure you’re keeping on top of fluids and fuel, too.

At this point, you may be deep into training and feeling drained—that’s completely normal, but does require a mindset shift. When Obiri is lacking motivation, she just tells herself she has to do it, or at least start it. “If I start, I will finish it,” she says. And, when a run gets hard? No matter how far she is from the end, she tells herself, “I’m almost there.”

It’s possible your For You Page (like mine) is filled with people freaking out about where they’re at in training—and it can be pretty easy to compare yourself to them. Maybe you have friends crushing long runs on Strava and are starting to wonder if it says anything about your progress.

Whatever it is, stay focused on your own program. “Everybody trains differently, so just know you have trained well and focus on your body, and trust me, you’ll be at the finish line and run well,” Obiri says.

Ultimately, your marathon is about you and what you did to get there. “Trust yourself, trust what you have been training for, and be ready to do what you have been training for six months or longer,” Obiri says.

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.

#5094. Crypto Docs Host GitBook Hit by AI Agent Security Breach, Urges Users to Rotate Keys

Crypto Docs Host GitBook Hit by AI Agent Security Breach, Urges Users to Rotate Keys

GitBook, a documentation platform widely used by cryptocurrency projects, has disclosed a security breach involving AI agents that allowed an attacker to gain access to some of its infrastructure credentials, forcing the company to shut down services and revoke customer access tokens.

In a security disclosure published today, the company described the incident as “a sophisticated, highly targeted attack using AI agents.” The attacker gained unauthorized access at 7 p.m. Central European Time on November 2, according to GitBook, which said it detected the intrusion immediately and moved to contain it.

The reference to AI agents is significant. Unlike traditional chatbots that simply respond to questions, AI agents can carry out sequences of actions, interact with software, and perform tasks with limited human supervision, making them potentially useful to attackers trying to automate parts of a cyberattack. Some are even capable of acting autonomously and even against their guidelines, as OpenAI’s and Anthropic’s experiments have proven.

GitBook has not explained exactly how the attacker used these tools, which AI systems were involved, or how the credentials were obtained. Its disclosure also does not establish that GitBook's own AI products were responsible for the intrusion.

The company took its application and documentation sites requiring authentication offline while securing its infrastructure. It also kept publicly available documentation accessible through cached copies for as long as possible, allowing visitors to continue reading previously saved versions of pages even while the underlying systems were unavailable.

A separate status notice acknowledged that GitBook was experiencing downtime affecting its published documentation sites and editor. By Tuesday afternoon, GitBook said its application, API, and published documentation were accessible again, although some services could remain degraded during recovery.

GitBook is effectively an online instruction manual for software products. Cryptocurrency projects use platforms like it to publish technical explanations, developer instructions, and guides that help users understand everything from connecting software to interacting with decentralized financial applications.

The documents themselves are often public, but the systems that maintain them can connect to private accounts and external services. Those connections depend on credentials that are supposed to remain secret, and GitBook says some of them may have been exposed.

Its investigation identified several potentially affected categories, including integration tokens, Git Sync secrets used to connect documentation with code repositories, and API tokens that allow software to communicate with GitBook automatically.

The incident has already prompted a response from the cryptocurrency industry, where documentation websites often serve as an important source of official instructions.

Decentralized exchange PancakeSwap temporarily disabled its documentation following news of the incident, although it said its own systems had not been compromised.

In a public statement, PancakeSwap said its “docs are not compromised and [the] PancakeSwap app is not affected.” The exchange nevertheless took its documentation offline as a precaution and redirected visitors to its homepage.

PancakeSwap reminded users that its documentation does not require them to perform wallet transactions, warning that “docs never ask you to connect your wallet or approve tokens.”

PancakeSwap subsequently announced that its documentation was back online after being moved from GitBook to infrastructure under its own control, with existing documentation links continuing to work.

GitBook says it blocked the attacker, addressed the vulnerabilities involved, replaced its own infrastructure credentials, and invalidated customer API tokens. But customers using GitBook alongside other services still have work to do.

The company's instructions begin with “rotate any existing GitBook API tokens,” meaning customers should replace their old digital access keys with newly generated ones.

GitBook also instructed customers to replace third-party API tokens and secrets used to connect external tools to its platform, including credentials associated with published integrations. Users of its Model Context Protocol, or MCP, server must authenticate again; MCP is a system that allows AI assistants and other software tools to connect to services such as GitBook.

#5093. French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa exploited for over $133 million, ZachXBT says

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

ZachXBT said he has a 'high degree of confidence' that the attacker is not associated with North Korea's Lazarus Group.

Onchain investigator ZachXBT has identified French cooperative bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the alleged victim of a theft estimated at more than $133 million.

ZachXBT first reported on Nov. 2 that an unnamed EU entity had lost over $106 million from multiple centralized exchange accounts the previous day, with funds withdrawn as USDC and USDT.

A day later, he raised the total stolen amount to above $133 million after tracing an additional $27 million in withdrawals, some of which occurred after his initial disclosure. He speculated that a large financial institution in Western Europe was exploited and currently being covered up.

On the evening of Nov. 3, he stated that the institution is allegedly Crédit Mutuel Arkéa, a French bank with approximately $235 billion in assets. He noted that the attack spanned multiple days and suggested the actual losses could be higher when accounting for possible over-the-counter activity.

"Over the past 24 hours the illicit actor has been aggressively swapping USDC for ETH, transferring to instant exchanges (WellWallet, Kyrrex, etc), converting to XMR via Wagyu, and bridging across multiple chains," ZachXBT wrote in his Nov. 2 message in his Telegram group, adding that he has a "high degree of confidence" that the attacker is not associated with North Korea's Lazarus Group.

He has criticized the institution for allegedly allowing more than $30 million to be laundered, noting that proper incident disclosure to EU law enforcement could have frozen the funds.

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa has not issued any public statement confirming or denying the incident. The Block has reached out to the bank but has not heard back. The claims remain based on ZachXBT's own analysis and have not been independently verified by the bank or authorities.

The bank has more than 5.6 million customers, with total assets of 211.5 billion euros (around $237 billion).

#5090. Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Hackers exploited compromised employee computers and stolen credentials to breach Binance’s wallet infrastructure, according to the exchange’s investigation.

A coding assignment from a prospective employer seemed like an ordinary part of a job interview. Instead, it was one step in a months-long cyberattack that ended with $6.3 billion stolen from Binance, raising questions about how exchanges protect themselves against threats that target their employees.

The attack was part of a coordinated campaign that exploited both human vulnerabilities and internal security systems, according to a postmortem released by Binance on Tuesday. The exchange’s findings detailed how hackers gradually gained access to its wallet infrastructure before the Oct. 29 theft.

According to Binance’s security report, the attack campaign began in June, when an engineer downloaded a tampered software package while setting up an AI coding tool. Although Binance detected the malware and replaced the affected device, the attackers had already gathered information about the exchange’s wallet team.

In September, attackers posing as recruiters targeted another software engineer and tricked the employee into downloading a malicious file disguised as a coding assignment. The malware compromised the engineer’s work computer and evaded the exchange’s security protections.

On Oct. 20, the attackers used the compromised computer and an internal AI tool to gain access to two additional engineers’s systems, obtaining deployment credentials that allowed them to install malicious software within Binance’s wallet infrastructure on Oct. 29.

The attackers then gained access to the exchange’s internal wallet-signing service, which authorizes cryptocurrency transactions. This allowed them to move funds outside the normal withdrawal process.

“This wasn’t one mistake or one breach — it was a months-long campaign,” Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said in a Tuesday post on X.

Binance said the attackers also exploited Ethereum’s EIP-7702 mechanism, which allows wallets to delegate transaction permissions to smart contracts, enabling unauthorized transfers to continue even after the exchange suspended withdrawals. Its multiparty computation (MPC) wallet system, which distributes control over transaction signing, complicated efforts to revoke those permissions.

The breach affected approximately $6.3 billion in digital assets, with Binance reporting that $2.85 billion had been frozen or intercepted, according to its latest accounting. The exchange said it would absorb the losses rather than pass them on to customers and had replenished 98% of the affected token assets as of Oct. 31.

The attack, the largest cryptocurrency hack in history, has been attributed to North Korean hackers by the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI). On Tuesday, Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, denied the country’s involvement, saying the FBI’s attribution was politically motivated and intended to damage North Korea’s international standing.

Binance co-founder Yi He said Tuesday that the exchange’s security team had detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026, including fraudulent job interviews, malicious software packages and social engineering attempts aimed at stealing credentials or installing malware.

“We intercepted many of them, but the cost of failing to stop even one was severe—and this should never have happened,” she said.

#5088. Justin Sun Accuses Trump-Backed World Liberty of Using UAE Ties to Escape Federal Court

Justin Sun Accuses Trump-Backed World Liberty of Using UAE Ties to Escape Federal Court

Justin Sun’s legal fight with World Liberty Financial now hinges on a question of nationality.

The Tron founder said Tuesday on X that his lawyers have asked a federal court in California to sanction the Trump family-backed crypto venture over how long it took to disclose that its ownership chain gives it citizenship in the United Arab Emirates.

Sun says he is one of World Liberty’s earliest and largest investors. He agreed in November 2024 to buy $30 million of its WLFI tokens and later raised his total investment to $45 million.

In April, he sued the company, alleging it misled him into investing and then unlawfully froze his tokens. World Liberty has fired back in court, alleging that Sun shorted WLFI and drove down its price.

The current dispute turns on a technical point. Sun chose to sue in federal court, which can hear cases between parties from different places but generally can’t hear a foreign plaintiff’s case against a foreign defendant.

As Sun explained it, a company like World Liberty takes on the citizenship of each of its owners, so a single foreign owner makes the whole company foreign for those purposes.

According to Sun, World Liberty’s initial court disclosure listed only itself, with Florida as its sole citizenship. A judge signaled at an August hearing that some of Sun’s claims should stay in open court rather than go to arbitration.

Less than a week later, Sun says, the company amended the disclosure to list 22 members in its ownership chain, with citizenship in Florida, Delaware, Puerto Rico, and the UAE. World Liberty now argues that the federal court lacks jurisdiction over the case, according to Sun.

Sun argues the late disclosure was a tactic to move the case out of federal court, and his motion seeks to make World Liberty and its lawyers bear the cost. He contrasted the company’s position with its own marketing, posting excerpts from World Liberty’s “gold paper” that describe it as “an American, tax-paying, corporation.”

Once the sanctions motion is resolved, Sun said, he will refile his lawsuit in California state court. “I will keep fighting for the transparency this community deserves,” he wrote.

The feud has played out publicly for months. Sun has criticized how World Liberty treats its investors and alleged that the company threatened to burn his WLFI tokens.

#5087. Morgan Stanley among first banks to tokenize US equities, Treasurys through DTCC’s service

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The bank is among the first to use DTCC's new tokenization service as it separately prepares to support tokenized equities in Q1 2027.

Morgan Stanley has begun using the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation's new tokenization infrastructure to tokenize real-world assets, joining a group of financial institutions that accessed the service after its launch last week.

The bank said it has tokenized U.S. equities and Treasurys through the service, making it among the first to do so, according to an X post.

"The hands-on experience of integrating tokenized securities with established market infrastructure will inform the continued development of the firm's digital asset capabilities as the market evolves," said Amy Oldenburg, Morgan Stanley's head of digital assets strategy.

DTC, DTCC's depository subsidiary, which holds $114 trillion in assets under custody, signed 20 participants to access the service at launch, including Bank of America, Barclays, and BNP Paribas. Citi, Fidelity Investments, JPMorgan, State Street, and UBS were also listed, with plans to onboard future users on a rolling basis put in place.

DTCC said at the time the service was developed with input from more than 150 firms and initially supported Canton and its private Besu network.

The development comes as Morgan Stanley expects to support tokenized equities on its alternative trading system in the first quarter of 2027, later than its previous H2 2026 target. Oldenburg told The Block last month that the bank was building the necessary infrastructure for the initiative.

Meanwhile, Morgan Stanley is not alone in the tokenization race. The New York Stock Exchange and its parent, Intercontinental Exchange, are also advancing plans for a tokenized trading platform. Broadridge, Computershare, Equiniti, and Superstate have signed agreements to serve as transfer agents.
