#5068. She Had a Bad Day and Consulted a Psychic. It Destroyed Her Life.

Intuitive practices have brought comfort to millions—but for some, it has opened the door to predators who weaponize belief, hope, and the search for healing.

IN EARLY 2024, a young Los Angeles musician who was down on her luck met with the FBI. She had a story that, at first, seemed unbelievable.

Nine months earlier, her career faltering and her morale low, she was approached one night by a psychic. The psychic said she could see that something was bothering her and offered to do a palm reading. The reading showed spiritual blockages hanging over the musician’s life, and the psychic said they would need to work together closely to clear them.

The musician gave the psychic thousands of dollars, the amounts increasing over time, and even bought cars for her and her family. At the psychic’s instruction, the musician lied to her own family, extracting what she could from them. When that wasn’t enough, the psychic put her to work. But now, confused and desperate, and overwhelmed by how she had even ended up in this situation in the first place, the musician needed to tell someone what had happened to her.

When the FBI ran the psychic’s name in their system, they discovered a familiar narrative of victimization had played out at least once before. Gina Rita Russell—psychic hustler, skilled manipulator, and pill-popping madame—was already on the FBI’s radar. And the story that put her there, beginning more than a decade earlier, was wilder than anyone could have imagined.

ONE NIGHT IN the fall of 2009, Jill Smith* was walking home through the streets of New York City, her self-esteem at an all-time low. Smith, 24, had just lost her job and gone through a devastating breakup. She felt vulnerable and rudderless, unable to launch her adult life. She’d been crying herself to sleep every night. When she passed a psychic storefront in the West Village, Gina Rita Russell was sitting outside at a small table. “Come for a reading,” Russell called out warmly. Smith declined politely, but Russell was persistent. So Smith took a seat and handed over $40.

Russell described herself as a “spiritual guider.” She peered into Smith’s palm, holding it in her own plump hands, and said she could see why Smith’s life was uncomfortably stalled. Even worse, she could see more bad things down the road. Smith needed Russell’s help, and fast. They would have to do “the work” to clear the mystical clouds hanging over Smith’s future, “driving the demons out” together. For the first time in a while, Smith’s despair lightened. She felt relieved that Russell wanted to help her.

What began as a cautious friendship at a rough time in a young woman’s life soon morphed into a nightmare. Over the next eight years, Russell would push Smith to abandon relationships, walk away from her budding career, and engage in degrading acts to clear what she was told was a malignant hex. And then, when the spell was finally broken, Smith would face a new uphill battle common to victims of trafficking: trying to persuade the courts that she wasn’t a coconspirator but a victim of forces that even she struggled to explain.

GROWING INTEREST IN “spiritual wellness” is driving the purchase of shiny little crystals and the borrowing of chants from Eastern traditions, plus plenty of yoga-and-gratitude retreats in places all over the world. But as part of this trend, some women are also leaning on psychics and other intuitive practitioners.

According to recent Pew research, 30 percent of Americans now consult astrology, tarot cards, or fortune tellers at least once a year. For women under 50, 43 percent “believe in astrology,” while only 27 percent of women 50 and over do. Eight percent of women say they rely at least a little on astrology, tarot cards, or fortune tellers to make big life decisions. Psychics are now ubiquitous, from small storefronts in one-stoplight towns to psychic influencers on Instagram with hundreds of thousands of followers. In November, Oprah had a psychic medium on her podcast discussing how to communicate with dead relatives. It can all be in good fun and perhaps even offer a bit of clarity. But at other times, it can veer into a form of exploitation.

In Jenkintown, Pennsylvania, a man-woman team with multiple fortune-telling charges against them allegedly fleeced a woman of more than $600,000, convincing her that they needed the money to lift a curse (their court cases are pending). A mother and daughter in Australia were recently arrested for allegedly running a $46 million psychic scam in which they told victims to sign over loans so they could use their powers to make the victims rich. And a woman in Toronto was allegedly scammed out of almost $8,000 by a Texas psychic she met on TikTok following the death of her stepfather. She was eager for guidance, and the cost of readings escalated quickly. “She would say I need more money to get rid of all this negativity—like, I need to fix you,” the woman said in describing the psychic to a reporter.

In some rare cases, exploitation can go far beyond theft, forcing victims into previously unimaginable acts. These relationships often start with grooming—the victim being offered kindness and attention—and then quickly shift into one-sided demands. The dynamics of control and manipulation depend on a power imbalance, and when the potential perpetrator is in a nontraditional form of authority with fewer guardrails in place, such as psychics and gurus, there may be a higher risk factor. Soon, victims may find themselves isolated, their resources depleted and their sense of reality in serious question.

“The exploiter is very intentional and thoughtful about identifying what that person needs the most and offering it to them, training them to trust them, to believe in them,” says Korin Hoffman Arkin, director of survivor care and engagement for Polaris, a national anti-trafficking organization. “So then when [an exploiter] starts asking for things that are maybe not so wonderful, it’s confusing. It starts out small and starts to build.”

While these stories might seem far-fetched, experts say that anyone can succumb to sophisticated manipulation tactics and the power of a trauma bond between victims and their tormentors. A false sense of security, of believing that one is invulnerable, creates its own risks, explains Laura Dryjanska, an associate professor at Rosemead School of Psychology of Biola University. “All of a sudden, you’re in much more danger because you’re not street smart,” she says. “People don’t realize that those psychological bonds can be much stronger than someone physically chained to a bed.”

SMITH WASN’T SOMEONE who seemed poised to veer off the tracks. She had been raised by supportive parents in upper-middle-class comfort in Long Island, New York, and earned a 3.49 GPA in theater production and design at Ithaca College. She didn’t smoke and only drank occasionally; her friends in college called her “Mom” because, mature beyond her years, she took care of everyone. After graduating from college in 2006, Smith looked for work as a designer, stylist, and wardrobe supervisor. She was thrilled when she landed a job on the soap opera Guiding Light. Smith, who has long chestnut hair and a toothy smile, was finding her feet in the big city, dealing with the exhilarating victories and demoralizing setbacks of early adulthood, impatient to live up to her promise. And then she met Russell.

Russell was just 24 when she beckoned Smith, and her background couldn’t have been more different. Her parents were Roma, a traditionally nomadic ethnic group from Eastern Europe, and she never attended school and is functionally illiterate. (As an adult, she would communicate by text using audio commands.) According to Russell, she and her siblings were forced at a young age by their parents, who were frequently violent, to support the family by selling trinkets on the streets and performing bogus psychic readings. By 15, already getting drunk and smoking marijuana, she says she was forced by her father into an informal marriage with her third cousin, Robert. Russell gave birth to the first of their four children at 16—though she eventually lost custody to Robert’s parents as she slid into multiple addictions.

The world had been kind to Smith, and she was brimming with trust. Russell had been primed for survival, trusting no one and empathizing with few. When Russell and Smith crossed paths, Russell wasn’t looking to help her; she needed a mark. But she framed her involvement in Smith’s life as an emergency intervention born of her concern. Did Smith have a way to get her hands on some money, fast? When Smith divulged her job loss, Russell encouraged her to apply for more jobs, stroking her ego like an old friend. Though Russell and Smith were roughly the same age, there was something maternal in her round face and soft, dark features. Russell had been groomed by her parents to understand how to win someone’s confidence, the gradual coaxing of attachment with the express purpose of betrayal. As the weeks grew into months, Russell turned herself into someone, maybe the only person, Smith could confide in.

Soon, with Russell’s encouragement, Smith got a new job, and they celebrated together. But it wasn’t enough money to save Smith, spiritually speaking. What started out as a $40 reading soon became $500, and then $1,500, to continue “the work.”

About three months into her relationship with Russell, Smith tentatively volunteered that her father had some money, and they came up with a plan to coax out some cash: Smith would tell her father that she was trying to improve her life, to get back on the right track. She would lie about needing help with tuition, therapy, and expensive sleep studies. Her family and friends had grown worried—Smith didn’t seem like herself—and her father agreed at first to help.

Smith had never lied to her father before, but Russell explained that if she didn’t do “the work,” then Smith’s father would get sick and die. And Smith loved her father, right? Smith nodded vigorously, the worst-case scenario never far from her mind. She felt lucky to have someone like Russell helping her.

Smith’s father started sending cash but was increasingly reluctant as Smith’s self-help bills mounted. Emboldened, Russell instructed Smith to tell her father that she needed $500,000 for a cutting-edge therapy that would erase her memories of an early-life trauma. Smith even sent her father a treatment plan she had assembled. Smith’s father demanded to speak with the therapist, so Russell had a relative play the part in a call that lasted more than an hour. Smith was also on the line, and when Russell didn’t think she sounded sufficiently distraught, Russell kicked her hard under the table to make her cry.

BY 2011, TWO years into their relationship, it was hard for Smith to keep up with the demands and expense of Russell’s spiritual program, despite the money she was taking from her father. Smith was now working on the soap opera One Life to Live. Crediting Russell with helping her land the gig, she handed over almost her entire paycheck. Smith had grown emotionally dependent on Russell; she had mostly stopped hanging out with her friends and kept her coworkers at arm’s length with a hollow smile. But Smith’s relationship with Russell was becoming increasingly fraught. At first, it had felt like Russell wanted to help her, that they were solving a problem together. But now, Russell was pushier and more explicit about all the terrible things that might happen to Smith and the people she loved if she didn’t unquestioningly cooperate.

As part of “the work,” Smith was told she had to clean Russell’s home and babysit her kids. Smith soon morphed into Russell’s personal assistant—booking vacations, arranging transportation, and running endless errands. She might be sent suddenly in the middle of the night to buy a TV or air conditioner, carrying the appliance on her back up the stairs to Russell’s apartment. Smith claimed Russell’s addiction to painkillers, muscle relaxers, and antianxiety meds inspired her to dispatch Smith to the city’s emergency rooms with a slew of made-up ailments—one time even telling her to pretend she’d been in a car accident—in order to get their hands on prescriptions.

Russell was surrounded by what seemed like a tight-knit extended family. In addition to her own parents and siblings, there was her common-law husband, Robert; Robert’s brothers, Tony John and Corry Blue; and the brothers’ parents, Archie and Candy. By Smith’s account, the dynamic was far from wholesome: There were fistfights, destroyed TVs, thrown furniture, and smashed bottles. Smith claims she saw Russell’s mother use a Taser on Russell’s sister. Over time, the violence was turned on Smith if she questioned or disobeyed instructions. Russell would insist that the “devil” was in Smith and that she had to be “cleansed” through beatings. One time, Smith says she was forced naked into a freezing-cold bathtub while Russell lit various objects and then shook and hit her as if it were an exorcism.

By this point, Russell had made two predictions that had (sort of) come true, and Smith was convinced of her psychic powers. Russell said that Smith’s father would have her incarcerated—a foretelling that was fulfilled when Smith went home and her father had her committed on a 72-hour psychiatric hold. When Smith was released from the hospital, she fled back to Russell, shaken by the prophecy.

In the midst of all of this, Smith found work in the wardrobe department of the TV series Madam Secretary. It was a dream job, but working full-time and often staying up until the middle of the night to meet Russell’s demands, Smith developed sleep-deprived vertigo. She was also drowning in debt. Smith says that Russell told her to rack up charges on her credit cards and even had Smith sign leases and rental agreements on the family’s behalf. She was told to buy a Bentley and pay for an apartment in Marina del Rey, California. The family took trips to Hawaii, Las Vegas, and Austin, attending Formula One races and a Stevie Wonder concert. Unable to keep up with payments, Smith saw her credit destroyed. But the family kept spending. Smith says Russell developed a taste for Louis Vuitton handbags and bought herself a $150,000 diamond ring. Her family posted their vacations on social media while Smith was back at home, paying for all of it.

All the anxiety of Smith’s early years in the city, when she was trying to gain a foothold in life professionally and personally, had been supplanted by the relatively straightforward but almost impossible aim of keeping one psychic satisfied. Smith was constantly doing cleanup on her relationship with Russell and her family. Russell’s mother and brother-in-law stole keys to Smith’s apartment, ensuring she had nowhere to hide. Smith says that, once, trying to track her down, they showed up and threatened Smith’s neighbor. It wasn’t just terrifying; it was embarrassing.

Smith soon realized it was futile to try to have any kind of life beyond Russell’s needs. She quit her job, despite feeling trapped between desperately wanting out of the relationship, shame at handing over so much control and stealing from her father, and terror at what might happen if she tried to leave.

BY 2013, SMITH’S father had forked over more than $1 million. His relationship with his daughter badly frayed, he was disappointed and done. But Russell told Smith “the work” still wasn’t finished. Smith had quit her job, so Russell came up with a new way for her to make money.

“Sex sells,” Russell told Smith, as if it were a demand of the universe and out of her hands. Russell instructed Smith to pack a suitcase and sent her to Washington, D.C., where she didn’t know anyone. She was instructed to start listing ads for sensual massage services—and the suggestion of more—on websites like Backpage.com. Soon, Smith was moving from hotel room to hotel room, seeing as many as 17 clients a day. Russell had become even more caustic and demanding, now frequently threatening violence. Smith claims that one night, Russell told her that if she didn’t come up with $10,000 quickly, Russell would infect her with HIV. Smith, estranged from her family and penniless, believed her. She hustled to make the payment.

Smith was in a perpetual state of fear. Her body was broken and didn’t even feel like hers anymore, and she was still chronically sleep deprived, with Russell and her family tag-teaming with nearly 24-hour demands. Soon, Russell told Smith that the money she was bringing in from “massage services” still wasn’t enough to keep the demons at bay. She would have to work harder, finagling jewelry and other expensive goods from her clients. She instructed Smith to tell them that she was in debt to people who wanted to hurt her. She should give her clients an opportunity to save her. Russell cracked open a window into her own grooming techniques, the manipulation tactics that had been passed down in her own family like a silver tea set.

After successfully conning a few men for extra cash, Smith met Daniel Zancan. He was married and had two kids and was the chief financial officer of his wife’s family business. Zancan, 34, with glasses and short brown hair, was, like Smith, relatively sheltered. He had graduated from a private high school, went to a good college, earned his accounting license, and made six figures. But despite his circumstances, he was looking for more—which led him to the racy ads on Backpage.com.

Zancan quickly became one of Smith’s regulars and, despite himself, fell in love. To him, Smith was wounded and delicate, someone who needed taking care of. To Russell, Zancan was another gullible mark, someone whose dissatisfaction could be easily exploited. She told Smith that her powers “saw money” around Zancan.

Smith was ordered to sell Zancan a tragic fictional narrative: that she had been abused by her father, who was now suing her. The court costs were bleeding her dry, and she had borrowed money from the wrong people—dangerous people, loan sharks. They wanted it back and sometimes held her against her will for days at a time, assaulting her. They threatened to do even worse.

Zancan jumped at the chance to be her knight in shining armor. But he had to be careful. He didn’t have a lot of money, and he couldn’t tip off his wife or in-laws. He started funneling small amounts to Smith—$2,000 here, $2,500 there. But in January 2017, Smith told him that she needed a lot more—$100,000. Russell had Smith tell Zancan that because she didn’t have the money, she could pay off the debt with a pair of expensive Rolex watches instead. Smith and Zancan made their way to a high-end jewelry store in northern Virginia, where Zancan filled out a credit application for a $10,000 line of credit—which wasn’t enough to buy even one of the watches.

Smith’s phone buzzed with texts from Russell’s family posing as the angry loan sharks, and Zancan was terrified about what the men might do to her. (Some of the calls and texts were coming from Florida, where Russell and her family were vacationing. Smith had booked their trip.) In a state of desperation, an idea was floated: What if he tapped into his employer’s ample resources? As the CFO, he had access to their bank accounts.

On January 11, 2017, Zancan embezzled $110,000 via a wire transfer he sent to Smith’s personal account. The next day, Smith converted the stolen funds into a cashier’s check, which she took to a check-cashing place in New York. Russell texted Smith to stay calm as she presented the large sum to the teller. “Don’t panic this is negativity it’s the devil trying to attack you and we will not except [sic] it rebuke it in the name of Jesus your powers are coming through,” dictated Russell. When the check was successfully cashed, Smith dutifully handed over every cent.

Privately, Smith was fighting her instincts. But she knew better than to cross Russell. On more than one occasion, Smith says she tried to walk out of Russell’s apartment, only to have Russell grab her by the hair and drag her back in, leaving bruises and threatening to kill her. Once, Russell issued a vague threat that her own young son would be harmed if Smith left. So Smith stuck with the plan, Russell telling her it would take just a little more money, that they were doing all of this to help Smith, and that things would get better.

THE RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN Smith and Russell, while dysfunctional and violent, was also strikingly affectionate. The now-yearslong spell was an incredibly potent mixture of fear and dependence. Smith didn’t just take Russell’s instructions; she clung to the tiniest morsels of care and actively sought Russell’s counsel. She spoke fondly of her to Zancan and other clients, telling them that the only bright spot in her life was her good friend, Gina. Isolated and unable to articulate how powerful Russell had become, she was intent on preserving a bond that seemed essential. The two were completely enmeshed, Smith doing Russell’s bidding for scraps of approval. It had to feel a bit like love when nothing else in Smith’s life did.

But Russell was firmly in control. Smith says that when it came to Zancan, Russell was clear: “Make sure he loves you enough that he would do anything for you.” Russell and her extended family continued spending lavishly, buying Louis Vuitton luggage, Tiffany and Cartier jewelry, and Patek Philippe and Audemars Piguet watches. Smith was forced to turn up the pressure on Zancan, telling him she needed $500,000 in cash.

One night, while Zancan was driving home from work, Smith called and said a loan shark named “Tony” (impersonated by Gina’s brother-in-law, Tony John) wanted to speak with him. Hammering home the threat, he said he knew where Zancan lived and worked. “Do I need to tell you where your kids go to school?” asked “Tony.” Zancan, panicking, agreed to steal the money and make the payment. Later, when he was en route to deliver the cash, one of Robert’s brothers took a picture of his license plate while he was in transit, which the family had Smith text to him. They wanted him to know that he was being watched.

Inspired by their new tactic, Russell and her family created additional characters to shake down Zancan. “Sammie” and “Santino” were even more sadistic; they gleefully told Zancan that they planned to beat Smith up—and worse. Zancan was breaking under the pressure. On February 5, 2017, Smith texted Russell in the middle of the night to tell her that Zancan was sobbing in their hotel room. “Tell him not to cry tell him that you love him very much,” Russell texted back. A few doors away, in their own room at the same hotel, Russell and Robert were taking videos of their young daughter posing with fat stacks of Zancan’s cash.

Later, Russell texted Smith to ask how Zancan was doing. Better, responded Smith. “We did good,” texted Smith. “Love you.” Russell texted back three crying-laughing emojis. “Love you,” she added. Russell then told Smith to delete her messages and get a burner phone. Shortly after, Tony John, impersonating a mobster in a call with Smith and Zancan, convinced Zancan to produce $1.6 million in gold bars.

Zancan was terrified but committed to a future with Smith. They would get through this mess and live happily ever after. On February 27, 2017, Smith texted Russell a picture. It was her left hand, her ring finger now heavy with a massive diamond engagement ring. Russell asked how many carats it was, and Smith guessed two, including the diamond-studded band. “Remember don’t fall in love and get too emotionally attached he only is doing this because you are emotionally supporting him,” texted Russell. Smith promised she was only “playing the part,” that she knew the difference between real and fake love.

Zancan had put aside $80,000 in gold bars that he hoped to use to start a new life with Smith. But soon after he put it in his car, Smith called to tell him a turf war had broken out between the gangsters. Santino had shot Tony. Santino wanted another $50,000 or Smith wouldn’t be allowed to leave. He threatened to rape her. Zancan liquidated the gold bars and handed $50,000 over to Smith at a Starbucks near Times Square. She gave it to Russell. Zancan and Smith drove to a hotel in Baltimore to spend the night, but not long after they checked in, the family started texting with more demands.

Zancan couldn’t take it anymore. He left Baltimore and started driving toward Texas. He was planning on collecting fake IDs there that he and Smith could use to escape for good. But when Zancan didn’t show up for work, his colleagues started digging around in his records and discovered the missing money. On April 6, 2017, Zancan was arrested. He had stolen more than $4 million, and he told the FBI everything. In a letter to the court, his parents suggested that Zancan’s vulnerability was being unusually trusting. “It is a good quality until it isn’t,” they wrote.

The FBI wanted to interview Russell and Smith, and Russell’s family hustled to come up with a cover story. They also decided that Russell, now 28, and Smith would get married, creating spousal privilege and ensuring that Smith couldn’t be compelled to testify against Russell. Smith was told to get burner phones to create “fake” conversations with Russell, crafting the impression that they were in love. They married at City Hall in New York.

But that wasn’t enough. According to Russell’s defense team, Robert’s mother, Candy, coerced Smith and her daughter-in-law to sign a notarized confession implicating themselves and absolving herself, her husband, and her sons of any illegal acts. Before Smith, now 32, went to her FBI interview in late April, the family threatened that she would spend the rest of her life in prison if she didn’t do exactly what she was told. Candy handed her two Valiums and told her it would help her “appear relaxed” during the interrogation. The Evans family (Robert’s brother) also made Smith sign a notarized confession and film a statement that absolved Russell and her family of any responsibility. But it wasn’t enough to assuage the family’s concerns that Smith might turn on them or that Zancan might successfully implicate them.

ON OCTOBER 19, 2017, a federal grand jury returned an indictment against Smith; she was arrested six days later. After eight years of terror, Smith felt a wave of relief as she was taken into custody. She was assigned to a maximum-security section of the D.C. Jail, where she bunked with a convicted murderer, and her stress-related chronic cough almost immediately ceased.

With so many details to disentangle, it would take another six months for a grand jury to return a 13-count indictment of fraud and extortion charges against Russell and her family. Russell and Robert split up, and the unified front fractured. Robert, his brothers, and his parents pleaded guilty to a litany of charges (Robert Evans and his brother Tony John Evans pleaded guilty to interference with interstate commerce by extortion; Corry Blue Evans pleaded guilty to bank fraud; Archie Kaslov pleaded guilty to conspiracy to commit wire fraud; Candy Evans pleaded guilty to tampering with a witness by corrupt persuasion or misleading conduct) and accepted sentences that ranged from one to five years.”

Russell eventually agreed to plead guilty to one count of interference with interstate commerce by extortion. She convinced prosecutors to propose a limited prison sentence, in light of her dramatic upbringing and her promise to stay out of trouble. She received permission to live in Los Angeles, supposedly to help an ailing family member. But in 2024, Russell was arrested in connection to the young musician’s case. Prosecutors immediately recognized a pattern in the musician’s account: promising spiritual healing for steep fees, encouraging the theft of almost $200,000 from the musician’s wealthy father, and eventually driving her into sex work before her instincts had a chance to catch up. Russell’s L.A. arrest violated the conditions of her relatively lax sentencing deal, and it was revoked by the court. On July 18, 2024, she was sentenced to more than 10 years and five months in prison, which she is presently serving in Oklahoma. She was also ordered to pay $4,217,542.86 in restitution.

As Russell’s court battle played out, Smith was able to convince prosecutors that while she’d participated in extortion schemes, she’d also been a victim who had handed all her money and power to a woman who had threatened and exploited her. (The marriage between the pair was annulled in 2019.)

On September 9, 2024, Smith pleaded to one count of conspiracy to commit bank and wire fraud and was sentenced to time served and 36 months of supervised release. “Coercion is a complex thing,” noted Judge Tanya S. Chutkan, at Smith’s sentencing. “But what I have learned…is that under the right combination of stress, pressure, and psychological circumstances, seemingly normal people will do very abnormal things.”

Since her release from Russell’s grip, Smith has become an anti-trafficking advocate and now works for 3Strands, an organization dedicated to combating trafficking through education and reintegration programs. Last year, Smith provided testimony to a congressional task force in support of The Trafficking Survivors Relief Act, which was passed into law in January 2026 and allows victims of sex trafficking to have their arrest/conviction records vacated and expunged—Smith seems to have availed herself of this law as her criminal history is no longer publicly available. (The court records in her case were accessed and saved for this article before public access was removed.) Smith declined to comment for this article; instead, the above is based on the voluminous court records filed by both prosecutors and defendants in the Russell, Smith, and Zancan prosecutions.

In January 2026, Smith was pardoned by President Donald Trump.

It’s also worth noting that though Smith identifies as a victim of human trafficking, none of the people involved in her odyssey were charged or convicted of human or sex trafficking.

“There is no black and white survivor story,” she wrote in her testimony, alluding to the various definitions—both legal and informal—of the word. “There are all types of trafficking in this world.... I am determined to use my lived experience from these horrific years to bring meaningful change and protect others.”

*This story uses a pseudonym at the request of the victim’s lawyer.

#5067. SpaceX blocks tokenized stock listing on OKXICE despite offshore trading boom

SpaceX joined several other issuers objecting to OKXICE tokenized trading, months after crypto platforms offered tokens tied to its shares.

Just months after tokenized versions of SpaceX shares became one of the biggest drivers of onchain stock trading, the aerospace company is pushing back against plans to bring similar trading to US investors.

According to a Nov. 2 public notice from OKXICE, SpaceX has objected to the proposed listing of tokenized versions of its shares on the trading venue, a joint venture between crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange (ICE). The company now joins Tesla and Amazon among 56 companies objecting to the proposed listings.

Under the Securities and Exchange Commission’s five-year Innovation Exemption introduced in September, platforms such as OKXICE can offer trading in tokenized versions of US stocks without registering as national securities exchanges. But the framework also gives companies a say in the process: They have 30 days to object to the proposed trading of their shares tokenized by third parties, effectively keeping those tokens off the venue.

Tokenized stocks are blockchain-based representations of company shares that allow investors to gain exposure to traditional equities onchain, often around the clock. Depending on the structure, they can be backed by actual shares, although the rights granted to tokenholders may differ from those of traditional shareholders.

OKXICE co-chair and former New York Governor Andrew Cuomo told Cointelegraph on Oct. 20 that the venture expected more objections as companies avoided being the first to test the model.

SpaceX’s objection comes despite tokenized versions of its shares already trading on platforms outside the US, where they have attracted significant trading activity since the company’s June public offering.

Ahead of the SpaceX listing, Backpack Securities, a stock brokerage and tokenization platform, and crypto exchange Kraken, through its xStocks offering, both prepared tokenized versions of the aerospace company’s stock, each designed to be backed by actual shares. Backpack’s SPCX token went on to record $1.08 billion in onchain trading volume in June, while Kraken’s SPCXx token recorded $852 million.

Unlike Backpack and Kraken, OKXICE would open tokenized stock trading to US investors under the SEC’s new regulatory framework, potentially tapping into the world’s largest equity market by capitalization and expanding the investor base for tokenized securities.

Nasdaq warned issuers in September that they “must be cautious about their participation” in the venture, arguing that tokenized stock trading could fragment liquidity and undermine long-term shareholder value.

Despite the growing opposition, Cuomo said during an Oct. 30 press conference that as people see the benefits of tokenization, “you’ll see more and more companies participate.”

#5066. Where Does Brock Purdy Live? Exploring the Homes of the San Francisco 49ers Quarterback

He spends plenty of time at Levi Stadium as the quarterback of the San Francisco 49ers, but where does Brock Purdy live when he’s off the field? The football player was born and raised in Queen Creek, Arizona, and played football at Perry High School in nearby Gilbert. While Purdy left Arizona in 2018 to play at Iowa State University, he’s still fond of his home state. “I love the state of Arizona and everyone that’s been a part of my life,” he told reporters ahead of the 2024 Super Bowl. “And obviously, I’m trying to represent them as best as I can for as long as my career goes.”

At Iowa State, Purdy was a three-time All-Big 12 quarterback and was the winningest quarterback in the school’s history. The University’s Midwest environment suited Purdy. “My favorite part is the people, just because of how down-to-earth and real everyone is,” he told the Iowa Stater in 2024. “Everyone wants to help each other out. There’s no selfishness. I don’t know what it is about the culture in Iowa, but everybody just loves one another authentically. And when you go back, you feel that every time.” Purdy also met his future wife, Jenna, his freshman year at Iowa State. The couple got engaged in 2023 and tied the knot back in Iowa in 2024.

In 2022, the San Francisco 49ers selected Purdy as the final pick of the NFL Draft. This pick typically earns the harsh nickname Mr. Irrelevant, but Purdy quickly proved the moniker wrong, becoming starting quarterback in his rookie season. Now in his fifth season with the 49ers, Purdy is also building a successful life off the field. Read on to learn about Brock Purdy’s homes over the years.

Teammates—and onetime roommates—Alfredo Gutierrez, Nick Zakelj, and Purdy in the locker room in 2022

At the beginning of his NFL career, Purdy lived more like a recent college grad than a pro football player. During his rookie year, Purdy shared a house with offensive linemen Alfredo Gutierrez and Nick Zakelj. The trio even gave out a Christmas card from the household to teammates. In an interview with NBC Sports Bay Area, Gutierrez revealed that Purdy did most of the cooking out of the three and that they spent downtime watching football, movies, and playing Mario Super Slugger.

By 2023, Purdy had only one roommate: Zakelj. “So he and I are still splitting rent,” Purdy told the TODAY Show. “I still drive my Toyota Sequoia. Other than that, it’s pretty simple.”

Purdy with his wife, Jenna, and daughter, Millie Joleen, in 2025. They now have a second child on the way.

Purdy left his shared home in 2024 when he married his wife, Jenna. They now live in a rental home in the Bay Area with their daughter, Millie Joleen, who was born in July 2025. The couple announced in July 2026 that they would soon be welcoming a second child.

The couple focused on making the home family-friendly, adding plenty of storage for their daughter’s toys, and durable furniture for hosting. “Becoming new parents, we really wanted this house to feel like a home where our family is going to continue to grow,” Jenna said in a video unveiling the couple’s living room and bedroom makeovers, which they worked on with Pottery Barn.

In May 2025, Purdy signed a five-year, $265 million deal with the 49ers, making him the fifth-highest-paid quarterback in the league. The hefty pay raise led Purdy to start his real estate portfolio. His teammate, 49ers tight end George Kittle, revealed on a podcast in June of that year that Purdy had purchased a home in Nashville.

Purdy with teammate George Kittle in 2025

“He did purchase a house in Nashville, Tennessee,” Kittle said. “He will be kind of a neighbor to me at some point. That will be pretty fun, because I’ll have a quarterback that can throw to me all the time, that’s actually on my team.” That summer, Purdy was seen training at Kittle’s football compound in Nashville.

#5065. RUNE rallies 15% as THORChain restarts most swaps after halt

THORChain has restored trading on Bitcoin, Ethereum and several other networks following a refund bug, while three chains remain paused.

THORChain’s RUNE token rose about 15% over the past 24 hours as the cross-chain swap protocol restored trading on most networks following a weekend halt triggered by an Ethereum block reorganization.

THORChain (RUNE) was trading at around $0.64 on Wednesday, up approximately 15% over the previous day, according to CoinMarketCap.

THORChain’s network status page showed trading and transaction signing marked “OK” on 10 of its 13 listed external networks. BNB Smart Chain, Cosmos Hub and Solana continued to show warnings for both functions.

Bitcoin, Ethereum, Bitcoin Cash, Dogecoin, Litecoin, Tron and Zcash were among the networks showing trading was re-enabled. Avalanche, Base and XRP were already operating when Cointelegraph reported on the halt on Monday.

The disruption began on Saturday when a one-block Ethereum reorganization removed a 10 Ether deposit that THORChain had already credited, according to the protocol’s account.

After the swap failed to meet its price limit, THORChain issued a refund against funds its vault had never received. The sender’s actual deposit was also refunded, resulting in an extra 10 ETH payout and triggering an automatic pause.

Node operators subsequently paused trading across multiple networks while developers investigated.

The halt also disrupted attempts to move funds stolen in the $6.3 billion Binance hack, according to blockchain analytics company Chainalysis, which said the pause had inadvertently delayed laundering by North Korean hackers.

The partial restart restores access to several cross-chain swap routes.

#5064. OpenAI Wants to Rebrand AI Data Centers, Use Them to 'Protect the Grid'

OpenAI Wants to Rebrand AI Data Centers, Use Them to 'Protect the Grid'

OpenAI wants its massive AI data centers to help keep the lights on when electricity grids come under pressure. And, apparently, it would also prefer that people stop calling them AI data centers.

The ChatGPT developer announced its Grid Protectors Program on Monday, a national initiative designed to let future AI facilities reduce electricity consumption during emergencies and periods of high demand, potentially lowering costs for other customers.

Under the plan, OpenAI would coordinate with utility companies to determine how much electricity its facilities could temporarily stop using, how quickly they could respond, and for how long. The company is also exploring on-site batteries and power generation to help manage demand.

“Families and small businesses should not be asked to subsidize a compute campus’s electricity needs,” OpenAI said in its announcement.

The program addresses one of the biggest complaints surrounding the AI infrastructure boom: companies are building increasingly power-hungry facilities while residents worry about paying higher—and water—electricity bills to support them.

OpenAI is using a planned facility in Effingham County, Georgia, as its first example. The company has signed a 25-year agreement with Georgia Power covering approximately 3,200 megawatts of electricity demand, with up to 1,000 megawatts available for temporary reductions during periods of high demand.

Under the approved agreement, OpenAI must cover the full infrastructure costs required to serve its facility, rather than passing those expenses on to existing customers.

Georgia Power also projects approximately $950 million in annual customer savings beginning in 2029, equivalent to at least $15 per month for a typical residential customer. However, those estimates cover the utility's broader portfolio of large electricity customers and expected growth, not just OpenAI's project.

The Georgia facility is still under development, and the savings remain projections. OpenAI has not yet disclosed binding demand-reduction commitments for other facilities under its national program.

OpenAI's electricity initiative also arrives after reports of a massive PR campaign to change what people call the facilities in the first place to erase from collective memory the negative sentiment towards AI data centers.

According to Axios, OpenAI is preparing a post-midterm policy and PR campaign that includes rebranding data centers as "AI compute campuses," an effort to make the infrastructure sound more appealing to communities, effectively trying to solve a problem by changing the problem’s name.

The company has already started using the terminology. Its Grid Protectors announcement repeatedly refers to future facilities as "compute campuses," although it occasionally uses the more familiar term "data centers."

An OpenAI spokesperson confirmed the naming strategy to Business Insider, arguing that the term better reflects the facilities' broader role in computing, energy infrastructure, and skilled employment.

The reported rebranding comes as opposition to AI infrastructure projects has become a political issue across the United States. Pennsylvania introduced new restrictions on large data centers in August, including provisions designed to protect residents from higher electricity costs. Other states are following.

OpenAI and six other major technology companies also signed a White House pledge in March to pay for the electricity and grid upgrades their AI projects require.

The new program goes further by proposing that AI facilities could become useful to utilities rather than simply adding pressure to their systems. But the national framework is still largely an ambition, with individual agreements to be negotiated.

OpenAI's Georgia project will provide an early test of that approach. Electricity delivery to the planned 3.2-gigawatt facility is scheduled to begin in 2028 and expand in phases through 2032.

#5062. How to Kill a Tree Stump, According to Experts—And Why It’s Important for Your Yard’s Health

Removing a tree from your property can be daunting, especially considering you can’t just shout “Timber!” and move on: You might need to know how to kill a tree stump as well. Just as an ailing or overgrown tree may put your roof, patio, or parked cars at risk if limbs start dropping, an untended tree stump may well cause underground damage to pipes or the foundation of your home as its roots keep spreading.

So your upcoming arboreal project may start with tree removal, but it’s important for the health of your lawn and your home to learn how to kill a tree stump in the aftermath. Even if you don’t plan to use the space where the tree once stood, according to the arborists we consulted, a tree stump left just sitting around can cause a host of new problems. While there’s more than one way to remove a tree stump, the consensus is universal: To consider tree removal truly complete, you’ll need to kill a tree stump, too.

“Old stumps attract pests, harbor fungi, and become tripping hazards,” says Melissa Rolston, chief strategy officer at Paramount Landscaping. “Some even try to regrow. Removing them protects your soil, prevents infestation, and keeps your yard design intentional.”

Rolston adds that the pest problem an unattended rotting tree stump causes can spread. Insects like ants and termites that might be drawn to a tempting tree stump can multiply and affect an entire property, potentially damaging healthy plant life directly and/or by spreading fungi or pathogens to nearby trees and plants. That “dead” tree stump may well become the launchpad for invaders that are very much alive—and very harmful to your yard.

Now that we’ve established a few of the reasons why you might need to kill a tree stump, read on to learn about a few methods you can use to get rid of a tree stump for good. Note that if the first approach fails to work on a truly tough tree stump, you can then move on to the second or third technique. If time isn’t a factor (as in, you’re not in a tree-stump removal rush to build a new patio or solarium), then the fourth approach is the least invasive in terms of impact on your property’s ecosystem.

Before you decide which tree stump removal method you’re going with to remove the stump in question, you should figure out how big of a job it is—literally. Stumps of different sizes call for different removal approaches, says Sara Gottlieb, founder and CEO of Liminal Gardens.

“Start by measuring the diameter of the flat, cut surface at its widest point, while accounting for any large bulges or irregular shapes,” says Gottlieb. “You’ll also want to consider the ground-level root flare, surface roots, and the height of the stump above the ground. Together, these measurements help give you a better sense of the stump’s overall size. A stump under 12 inches in diameter would generally be considered small, while one measuring more than 30 inches would be considered large. Surface roots and stump height should also factor into your assessment.”

Small stumps can be ground out in a matter of minutes, whereas massive stumps may be too big for practical grinding. Chemical or fire stump removal can cause enough damage to large stumps that they are indeed “killed” and might not need full removal; the roots will stop growing if the stump is effectively killed off. For medium-sized stumps, it’s more a matter of your budget, patience, and DIY know-how when it comes to which method you choose from the options below.

Almost every expert we reached out to for tree-stump removal tips recommended using a chemical herbicide to quickly and effectively vanquish unwanted tree stumps. For many of our pros, it was the top suggestion. Benjamin Hanley of Hanley Landscape Design tells AD that “targeted herbicides like glyphosate (potassium nitrate) or triclopyr can be applied directly to a fresh cut or in drilled holes in the stump to kill the root system.” This can be a highly effective way to completely kill off a stump, but be warned: Those chemicals can be dangerous. It’s crucial to use proper protection—and to keep kids and pets at bay—if you’re using this tree-stump removal method.

· Herbicide (potassium nitrate or triclopyr)

Herbicide has been applied into the drilled holes of this tree stump.

Use a chainsaw to cut the stump down as short as you safely can. Next, drill multiple holes down into the stump, with the goal of sinking them at least eight to 10 inches down.

Generously pour your herbicide of choice down into each of the holes you drilled, taking extra care not to get any of the powder or liquid on exposed skin or anywhere near your face. Then pour about a cup of water into each hole to activate the chemicals.

Cover the treated tree stump with a tarp. Secure the tarp with bungees, ropes, rocks, or a combination of items heavy enough to keep it down tightly. Within a matter of days, that stump should be effectively dead, but the process may stretch out a bit depending on the stump’s resilience. (See below FAQ on how long it takes to kill a tree stump.)

“The tarp keeps rain out and helps retain heat and moisture, speeding up decomposition,” says Gabe Boersma of Top It or Drop It Tree Service. He advises checking on the treated stump every few weeks, and “within a few months, the stump should be soft enough to break apart with an axe or shovel.”

For smaller tree stumps, you may be able to simply rip the stump and its root system out of the ground using a shovel, a pick, and some elbow grease. (Ask me about the seven or eight hours I spent battling the root structure of a rather small Japanese maple tree in the summer of 2017.) For larger stumps, “our preferred method of removal is to grind the stump,” says Hanley. “A grinder can be used to remove it below grade with minimal site disturbance. We often grind the root system as well. Homeowners can rent a compact stump grinder from a store like Home Depot or Lowe’s, or call your local tree company to grind it.”

Be warned that a stump grinder can be a serious expense. “Renting [a stump grinder] typically costs between $300 and $500, while purchasing one outright can cost approximately $1,500 to $2,000,” says Gottlieb. Before choosing the stump-grinding approach, she advises you to make sure you have adequate access to and space around the stump for the grinding equipment. “Different types of grinders are suited to different stump sizes, and you’ll need enough room to maneuver the machine, along with an electrical hookup to power it.”

Whether you rent or buy a stump grinder or hire professionals to come in and use one for you, this is the most expensive way to remove a tree stump. But it might be worth the money: Stump grinding is by far and away the fastest approach; it can be the most effective, as well.

If you have a chainsaw and feel safe using it, cut the stump as low to the ground as you can. Then clear away as much debris as possible, as the chainsawing process will almost certainly kick up sawdust, fallen twigs, leaves, and loose rocks. Do not let your chainsaw hit the ground! Dirt not only dulls the chain instantly, but it can also cause dangerous kickback, send rocks and other debris flying, and generally be very, very bad. In fact, if there is visible dirt on any part of the stump you’ll be cutting, including roots, rinse off thoroughly with water, then let the stump dry fully before any sawing.

Set the stump grinder in place with its cutting wheel over the stump, then fire it up and slowly lower it down. Once the cutting wheel begins to make contact with the stump, move it from side to side and forward and back as needed to slowly grind away the tree stump.

Use the stump grinder to sever and grind large tree roots you see exposed in the grind-down process; when you’re done grinding both the stump and the root system, be sure to fill the hole you have created with soil, packing it until it’s leveled off. Consider using the ground stump bits as mulch.

It’s important to address the whole root system when killing a tree stump.

If you plan to use fire to kill a tree stump, please, put safety first, second, and third! Make sure there are no overhanging limbs that could combust; cut back and remove nearby grasses, brambles, and underbrush; and have a fire extinguisher ready to deal with any embers that float away.

Despite the many hazards one must be aware of before choosing this tree-stump removal method, there are good arguments to be made for using fire to eliminate your tree stump. “The burning method deserves special attention because it does not require uprooting the stump by hand or using heavy equipment, which can be a challenge if the stump is in a hard-to-reach area,” says Josh Heston, co-owner of ALC Property Maintenance. “The process does not take much time but does require attention to safety.”

Ideally you would do this using a doorknob drill bit or one similar to it, making a large hole in the center of the stump and drilling down as far as you can. Then, with a smaller bit (think a half-inch), drill into the sides of the stump until you reach the main hole. (Take note: This approach will not work with extra-wide stumps, though you may be able to use it by drilling a series of holes down into the top of the stump and connecting to each hole from the side. It’s a process, to be sure, but this approach can be very effective and may be worth trying even for larger tree stumps.)

Taking great care (and wearing gloves), soak the strip or strips of cotton in gas or lighter fluid, then insert all but a few inches of the fabric into the center hole. Switch gloves for safety (be aware of gasoline residue) and then light the fabric. Make sure you are leaning well back from the hole as you apply flame, and remember to wear protective eyewear during this stage; no need for you to end up as a comical internet meme without eyebrows as a result of this process.

“In order for the stump to start burning from the inside, you need to provide an oxygen supply,” says Heston. “For this, periodically blow air through the side holes. Also, from time to time, toss in some dry branches into the central hole to maintain the flame inside.” And do not squirt in gas or lighter fluid; that’s riskier than it’s worth.

It may take several hours for a stump to burn up enough that it can be removed easily with shovels and picks. Even if you don’t plan to fully remove the stump—you just needed to kill it—the burning technique will work and is relatively low-impact.

You don’t need to use fire, harsh chemicals, or heavy machinery to kill a tree stump; you can opt for a much more “natural” approach—but it will take time. Essentially, for the “natural method,” per Boersma, you’ll use “the same equipment as with the chemical [approach], but swap out chemicals for epsom salt or rock salt.”

Epsom salt or rock salt can be used to kill a tree stump.

If you have one, use a chainsaw and cut the stump as low as you safely can. (Again take heed of the notes above on chainsaw stump-removal safety, avoiding the blade from touching dirt or rocks and so on.) Then, drill several wide holes into the stump.

Pour in enough rock or epsom salt to fill each hole you drilled, then pour over enough water to fully saturate the salt. If the salt seems to all wash down into the hole, repeat the process one or two more times, adding more salt, then more water. If a stump is particularly porous (rotting, for example) then you may need to add salt and water two or more times.

Wrap the stump in a durable tarp and secure it in place. Then it’s time to play the waiting game. “This process is slow, taking 6 to 12 months, but is nontoxic and requires very little labor,” says Michael Clarke, the founder of Yardwork. Eventually, the stump will be so broken down that the ground will reclaim it or you can remove it with a shovel.

Our experts stressed the need for caution around chemicals, fire, chainsaws, and the heavy machinery (like stump grinders) one might make use of to kill a tree stump, but safe stump removal also involves really knowing what’s going on under the ground, says Sal Bracale, president of Moon Valley Nurseries. “Always call utility companies before you dig; this is the best advice I would give. Safety is always a concern, so wear proper PPE [personal protective equipment].”

And don’t ever use a brute-force approach to tree-stump removal, Bracale says. “[Killing a tree stump] is a lot of work and effort, but using unsafe techniques like a truck and a chain can cause serious injury if the chain comes off and backlashes toward a home or the truck.” You may well damage or ruin your vehicle. When in doubt, Bracale adds, “Hire a professional who is educated, experienced, licensed, and insured.”

The most common household products you can use to kill tree stumps include epsom salts and rock salts, as noted above, but there are other things you likely have around the house that can be used as well. Like the salts (and stronger herbicidal chemicals), both of the following products should be applied by pouring them down holes you drill into the stump.

You can use undiluted bleach (be careful not to breathe in the fumes) or white vinegar, and make sure to wrap the stump tightly in an airtight tarp after pouring in either of the DIY stump killer fluid. Do not—repeat—do not ever use bleach and vinegar at the same time for anything. Ever. Mixing these two substances can create a dangerous and even deadly gas.

While it could be considered the most labor-intensive and costliest way to fully destroy and remove a tree stump, grinding the stump is the fastest method of removal by far. You can remove a tree stump via grinding in an hour or two, as opposed to the weeks or months it can take to kill off then dig up a stump with chemicals or other products. “Especially for large stumps that need to be removed quickly, it’s best to use a stump grinder,” says Clarke. Larger stumps may resist salts or chemicals, and may continue to resprout new shoots or harbor pests and fungi for a long while.

With a stump grinder, you can kill a tree stump in an hour or two, give or take the preparation and cleanup time. On the far extreme, David Brown, owner of Reliable Tree Service, notes that it can take “six months to two years” to kill off a tree stump via “natural decomposition, [which is] the best approach for eco-conscious or low-budget approaches if you have the time to wait.”

The stump itself won’t damage buried water lines, per se, but the roots can be ruthless with pipes. Emily Demirdonder, cofounder and director of operations and marketing at Proximity Plumbing, says, “Homeowners usually focus on what’s sitting aboveground, but the real risk is what the roots are doing underneath.”

Tree (or stump) roots can spread two to three times the width of the tree’s canopy underground; they grow toward water and nutrients, and clay or PVC pipes are a direct source of both. “Drainage lines are essentially a target,” Demirdonder says. “The stump isn’t safe just because the tree is gone; live roots keep growing for months after removal, and in my work, I’ve tracked root systems traveling over four meters from the original stump during drain repairs. The tree above is dead, but the root network below is still very much active.... I believe if the stump sits within three meters of a drain line, full removal is the right call. Leaving it creates a slow root-intrusion problem that rarely shows surface signs until it’s already serious. The roots infiltrate through tiny joints or cracks, expand from inside, and the pipe collapses gradually. You won’t see it coming until there’s a slow drain or a wet patch in the yard, and by then the damage is usually well underway.”

The most basic reason to kill off a tree stump is to reclaim the space; maybe it’s in the middle of your backyard and the kids want to play there, or it would make for less hassle for you and your lawn mower. Maybe it’s in the middle of your garden and you want more planting space. The list could go on.

But there are other valid reasons that you may want to kill a tree stump—reasons that can protect your home and can protect other trees and plants nearby. “Fungal decay [from a stump] can spread to nearby plants, and the rotting wood often attracts pests,” says Hanley. Adding to that, Boersma explains that “dead stumps can attract pests like termites, carpenter ants, or fungi that can spread to nearby healthy trees or even your home’s structure.”

Yes, a tree can sometimes grow back from a stump, but it depends mainly on the species of the tree and the condition of the roots left behind. When a tree is cut down and its root system remains alive, some species are able to send up new shoots from the stump itself or from the roots, in a natural process called coppicing. Tree species such as oak, willow, maple, poplar, birch, and eucalyptus are especially good at this and can regrow surprisingly quickly, particularly if the tree was relatively young and healthy when it was cut down. On the other hand, most conifers (evergreens like pine, spruce, fir, and cedar) generally do not regrow from stumps because they lack the ability to produce new shoots from old wood, so once they are cut down, the stumps typically die. Other factors also influence whether regrowth happens, including the age of the tree (again, younger trees respond better and tend to grow back), the overall health of the root system, how high the stump was cut, and even the season in which the tree was removed.

As long as that tree stump isn’t putting buried pipes at risk, or acting as a hotbed for pests (and isn’t going to be a pain to mow around), it might be best left right where it is—and that’s true even if it’s rather prominent on your property.

“Removal isn’t the only option; a tree stump can also become part of your landscape design,” says Gottlieb. “Building up a mound of plantings around it offers a way to incorporate the stump into the garden and build on previous iterations of the landscape. The stump itself can serve as a bench, or it can be carved to create an artistic focal point. Leaving it in place can also reduce the disruption to the property that full root removal would cause. Planting around and on the stump, working into the crevices between surface roots, and layering moss and flowering perennials can add depth and visual interest to the garden.”

#5061. Walking This Many Steps a Day Could Help Lower Your Risk of Alzheimers—It’s Less Than You Think

You've probably heard by now that exercising regularly is good for your brain and may even help lower your risk of Alzheimer's disease. But new research suggests that you don't have to kill yourself at the gym to harness these perks.

The study, which was published in Nature Medicine last year but is making the rounds now, found that taking just 3,000 steps a day is linked to significantly lower rates of cognitive decline, along with fewer markers of Alzheimer's disease.

It's worth pointing out that 3,000 steps isn't a ton (we're talking about walking about 1.5 miles in a day). That makes this a very doable goal to aim for. But how accurate is this, and what does it suggest about the 10,000-step goal everyone throws around? Doctors weigh in.

Meet the experts: Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center; Nikhil Satchidanand, PhD, a research assistant professor of medicine in the Jacobs School of Medicine and Biomedical Sciences at the University at Buffalo; Liron Sinvani, MD, director of research and innovation for the Institute for Healthy Aging at Northwell Health and the Feinstein Institutes for Medical Research; Anna Hohler, MD, regional director of Neurology for Westchester, Northwell Health, and chair of Neurology for Northern Westchester and Phelps hospitals; and Shannel Elhelou, PsyD, a neuropsychologist and founder of Brain Centric Neuropsychology in Westlake Village, California.

For the observational study, researchers analyzed data from 296 people between the ages of 50 and 90 who participated in the Harvard Aging Brain Study, a long-running study that tracks brain changes in older adults. None of the participants had dementia or signs of cognitive decline at the start of the study.

Participants wore waistband pedometers to track their physical activity and underwent PET brain scans to measure the amount of amyloid-beta plaques and tau tangles (both are hallmarks of Alzheimer’s disease). Participants also underwent cognitive assessment about 9.3 years later.

The researchers found that people who had higher step counts also had lower rates of cognitive decline. Higher step counts were also linked with a slower buildup of tau. Not only that, but walking 3,000 to 5,000 steps daily seemed to slow progression of Alzheimer's disease in people who were at a higher risk of developing it.

Specifically, those considered high risk for Alzheimer's disease who walked 3,000 to 5,000 steps a day had a three-year delay in developing cognitive decline. People who walked 5,000 to 7,500 steps daily saw a seven-year delay in their cognitive decline.

Those who were more sedentary saw a buildup of tau proteins in the brain, along with faster rates of decline in their cognition and daily functioning.

There are probably a few things behind this, according to neurologists. A big one is that physical activity is simply good for the brain. "It improves blood flow, supports cardiovascular and metabolic health, reduces systemic inflammation, and may help protect brain cells from aging-related damage," says Nikhil Satchidanand, PhD, research assistant professor of medicine in the Jacobs School of Medicine and Biomedical Sciences at the University at Buffalo.

Working out also helps stimulate the release of protective molecules like irisin and brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF), which support the health and connectivity of brain cells, says Liron Sinvani, MD, director of research and innovation for the Institute for Healthy Aging at Northwell Health and the Feinstein Institutes for Medical Research. "Exercise also improves cardiovascular fitness, and what is good for the heart is good for the brain," she adds. Daily walks can support your cardiovascular system and may lower the risk of vascular or multi-infarct dementia, according to Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center.

Neurologists applaud how approachable the findings are. "Importantly, 3,000 steps is an achievable goal for many people, making regular physical activity more sustainable over time," says Anna Hohler, MD, regional director of Neurology for Westchester, Northwell Health, and chair of Neurology for Northern Westchester and Phelps hospitals. "Consistent movement, even at lower levels, appears to contribute to better cognitive resilience and may help reduce the risk of developing Alzheimer's disease."

But Dr. Satchidanand says that "there's nothing magical" about 3,000 steps. "The brain doesn't suddenly start benefiting from physical activity when someone reaches a particular number of daily steps," he says. "The relationship between physical activity and health exists along a continuum."

Step count also only reveals one dimension of a person's physical activity, Dr. Satchidanand says. "They don't tell us much about walking intensity, cardiovascular fitness, strength training, or other forms of movement that may also influence brain health," he says.

Dr. Satchidanand says it's also important to consider that people who develop Alzheimer's disease may move less, which could also influence findings. "Early Alzheimer's-related changes in the brain could themselves lead people to become less physically active, even before noticeable cognitive symptoms develop," he points out.

The idea that you only need to log 3,000 steps a day to slow down cognitive decline is definitely appealing—and far less than the 10,000-step milestone that gets thrown around a lot. So where does that magical 10,000 standard come from?

Turns out, it’s actually an arbitrary number from a marketing campaign for a Japanese step counter. Since then, research has actually suggested that 10,000 steps is more than most people need to be healthy. So, if you want to walk that many steps a day, more power to you—but just know you don’t actually need to walk that far to reap health benefits.

Movement in general benefits the brain, so it’s hard to go wrong if you find something you love.

"My advice is to find activities you enjoy and can realistically sustain," says Shannel Elhelou, PsyD, neuropsychologist and founder of Brain Centric Neuropsychology in Westlake Village, California. "Consistency matters, and an achievable routine can grow over time."

This can include brisk walking, swimming, dancing, or cycling, depending on your abilities and preferences, Elhelou says. "I also encourage people to consider what makes movement meaningful to them," she continues.

Dr. Satchidanand also notes that people shouldn't feel boxed in by a specific step count. "Moving more, particularly among people who are currently inactive, is beneficial for overall health and may help support healthier brain aging," he says.

Carina Hsieh, MPH, is the deputy features editor of Women’s Health. She has more than a decade’s worth of experience working in media and has covered everything from beauty, fashion, travel, lifestyle, pets, to health.

She began her career as an intern in the fashion closet at Cosmopolitan where she worked her way up to Senior Sex & Relationships Editor. While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

#5060. 1inch lawyer sees agencies driving US crypto regulation as midterms unfold

Crypto executives expect US regulators to drive crypto policymaking regardless of the midterm election outcome.

With voting underway in the US midterm elections and control of Congress at stake, Maylea Ma, deputy general counsel at 1inch, said she expects federal agencies to drive the next phase of crypto regulation.

The election could reshape the path forward for the stalled CLARITY Act, but Ma said “real movement” in US digital-asset regulation over the next few years will come from the US Securities and Exchange Commission, Commodity Futures Trading Commission and Treasury, rather than Congress.

Ma said those agencies were increasingly engaging with crypto projects. However, agency engagement does not provide the stability the industry had hoped to secure through legislation.

“The long-term goal, of course, remains a statute that cannot be reversed every time a government changes,” said Ma.

That uncertainty also matters to institutional investors, according to Michael Ho, co-founder of D3, the team behind Doma Protocol.

“With the market structure bill stalled, US crypto policy continues to rest on agency guidance that a future administration can reverse, and that keeps serious capital waiting,” Ho said.

“Whatever the result, clear rules written into law are what will bring institutional capital into tokenized real-world assets.”

In August, the SEC proposed a framework for crypto fundraising, including exemptions for certain token offerings and a safe harbor from treatment as investment contracts. In September, it also updated staff guidance covering token buybacks, crypto networks and staking receipt tokens.

The CFTC, meanwhile, submitted a crypto market regulatory plan for White House review on Sept. 17, two days after the Senate failed to advance the CLARITY Act.

#5059. Judge Orders Bitcoin Wallet Returned to Family of Convicted Canadian Killer

Judge Orders Bitcoin Wallet Returned to Family of Convicted Canadian Killer

A Canadian judge has ordered that a laptop containing a Bitcoin wallet be returned to the family of William Sandeson, who is serving a life sentence for the 2015 murder of fellow university student Taylor Samson during a drugs deal, Global News reported.

Justice John Bodurtha of the Nova Scotia Supreme Court ruled on Tuesday that prosecutors had not proved the cryptocurrency was linked to the killing. Sandeson, who appeared virtually at the hearing in Halifax, had filed a motion in April seeking the return of personal items seized when police searched his apartment after his arrest in 2015.

"The Crown has not met its burden to demonstrate the cryptocurrency was used as part of the drug transaction, proceeds of crime, or otherwise tainted by criminality," Bodurtha said, adding that its potential use in the deal was "hypothetical."

The judge did rule that about $3,600 (C$5,000) in cash found in Sandeson's apartment after the murder was "offence-related property," meaning it will not be returned.

Sandeson has said the Bitcoin was worth between about $5,800 and $7,200 (C$8,000 to C$10,000), according to Global News. A lawyer representing Samson's family in a separate wrongful death lawsuit, in which the cryptocurrency had been at the center, has said it could now be worth more than $144,000 (C$200,000). Global News did not report how much Bitcoin the wallet holds.

Sandeson, then 23 and about to start medical school at Dalhousie University, arranged for Samson, 22, to come to his apartment on August 15, 2015, to sell him cannabis that Sandeson had arranged to buy for C$40,000, according to prosecutors at his 2023 trial. Sandeson shot Samson, kept the drugs and the money, and later admitted to dumping his body in a river that feeds the Bay of Fundy. Samson's remains have never been found.

Sandeson was convicted of first-degree murder in 2017, but an appeals court overturned the verdict in 2020. At a second trial in 2023, he admitted to shooting Samson but said he acted in self-defense. A jury found him guilty of second-degree murder, which carries an automatic life sentence. He is serving his sentence in Quebec and will be eligible for parole in 2030.

#5057. ZachXBT identifies French bank as alleged victim of $133M crypto theft

The French bank has not confirmed its alleged involvement in the crypto theft, which ZachXBT said could have resulted in losses exceeding $133 million.

Onchain investigator ZachXBT has identified French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the alleged victim of a crypto theft exceeding $133 million, warning that total losses could be higher.

On Wednesday, the pseudonymous crypto sleuth said the institution, which he described as having $235 billion in assets, was allegedly exploited over multiple days. He also suggested that additional losses may have occurred through over-the-counter (OTC) transactions that were not included in earlier estimates.

“I suspect the amount stolen is larger when accounting for OTC activity,” ZachXBT said.

The bank’s alleged involvement has not been independently verified, and ZachXBT has not disclosed how the attackers gained access to the funds.

Cointelegraph contacted Crédit Mutuel Arkéa for comment but had not received a response by publication.

The disclosure follows ZachXBT’s initial report on Monday, when he identified over $106 million in suspicious withdrawals from centralized exchanges.

On Tuesday, he raised the estimated losses to over $133 million after discovering another $27 million in withdrawals, some of which occurred after his initial warning.

Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery previously traced withdrawals from accounts at Kraken, Crypto.com and OKX. It found that approximately $63 million in stablecoins had been converted into Ether (ETH) within four hours.

Kraken and Crypto.com subsequently told Cointelegraph they had restricted accounts or blocked funds associated with the suspected theft. Both exchanges said their platforms remained secure.
