#5027. How This New Orleans Hotel Went From Former Haunted House to Chic Destination

The Greek Revival mansion that sits at the corner of Prytania Street and Jackson Avenue in New Orleans’ Garden District wouldn’t know “dull” if it came up and rang the doorbell. The 1857 building’s first owner was a cotton merchant who died of yellow fever. Another owner was fatally struck by lightning. Fast-forward to the 21st century, when a Tina Turner impersonator bought the property and turned it into Magnolia Mansion, a B&B with suites with names like Vampire Lovers’ Lair and Moulin Rouge. A former professional boxer and local politics kingmaker took over, but things fell apart around the time of the FBI’s raid on the property.

Oh, and the estate is said to be haunted.

The Whit's moody mahogany-paneled library

Named in its B&B era by USA Today as one of the top places in the nation “to sleep with a ghost” the magisterial Harris-Maginnis House earned a spot on the city’s ghost-tour circuit with an ever-mounting pile of lore. According to a spooky tour-operator website, guests at the B&B reported various eerie happenings over the years, such as the sensation of somebody tucking them in at night or a presence watching them while they slept. There were also rumblings of mysterious footsteps and slamming doors. Ghosts or no ghosts, the new team taking over the place found gothic-coded wall murals and dark purple velvet hanging at every turn.

Who you gonna call to give an old haunt a new design vernacular? Gretchen Trauth, CEO of the New Orleans investment firm behind the New Orleans hotel, rang up Jensen Logan and Katie Leblanc Killen of Logan Killen, a local design firm known for infusing spaces with a beguiling mix of polish and patina. “The goal was a hotel where nobody can quite tell whether a designer has touched it,” says Logan.

Breakfast room with Pierre Frey wallpaper

“We wanted it to feel very storied, like it’s been someone’s house for a long time,” adds Killen. “There are these layered histories imbued in the furnishings and the art and accessories.”

Named for poet Walt Whitman, who spent three months in New Orleans around the time of the home’s construction, the designers have reimagined the property with a romantic, lived-in, and curio-studded aesthetic that tempts guests to sit down and stay awhile. Architect Seamus McGuire of New Orleans firm Cicada helped navigate historical codes and redraw the map to capture some of the glamour that had drained away in recent years. His blueprint called for converting a trio of bedrooms into lounges. A mahogany-paneled library is now a gallery of antique dog portraits and a repository of volumes from local independent Faulkner House Books. “It’s dark and moody, and where you might want to have a cocktail or a whiskey,” Logan says of the clubby room. Bookworms might also gravitate to the sunroom’s banquette seating, one of the wicker chairs on the wraparound porch, or the breakfast room, a delightfully matchy-matchy enclave covered in Pierre Frey Les Colonnes wallpaper and curtains.

The hardest room to get right, say the designers, was the main lounge. “It’s a giant space that isn’t always in use, and can be tricky to give life to when it’s empty,” says Killen. Intended to serve as a great room by day and an atmospheric cocktail bar come evening, the team layered in elements such as blue paint that reads ethereal by day and warm by night, a custom grid carpet to add a sense of tailoring to the expansive area, and an intimate salon-style wall of works by Louisiana artists, including Robert Moreland and Mallory Page.

Another tightrope walk was the second-floor bar. Landmark rules forbid faking history a space never had, so the team went openly Deco instead. “What we don’t want is to put a contemporary bar into a very historic property,” says McGuire. “You can’t give a false sense of history to a space.”

It will probably come as little surprise that the bedrooms resist uniformity. In addition to the classically designed units, a quartet of cozy garden rooms has low ceilings and glossy oxblood walls; three cabana rooms feel airy, with bamboo beds and high ceilings. “It’s not meant to feel like everything was changed by the same person at the same time,” Killen says of the narrative that she and Logan have introduced to the New Orleans hotel.

As for the supposed ghosts, nobody’s heard boo.

#5025. Morning Minute: CFTC Clears Stock Perps, Gets Sued the Same Day

Morning Minute: CFTC Clears Stock Perps, Gets Sued the Same Day

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

⚖️ CFTC Clears Stock Perps, Gets Sued the Same Day

A perpetual future is a leveraged bet on a price that never expires. You hold it as long as you can meet margin and keep paying a funding rate. It’s analog product would be options in TradFi, with a few differences (primarily no expiration date). Crypto traders have used them for years, mostly on offshore venues like Binance and onchain platforms like Hyperliquid, because U.S. law had no clear home for the product.

On Tuesday the CFTC said Americans can now trade them on individual stocks.

Hours later, Wall Street sued to stop it.

The approval came as a statement from CFTC Chairman Michael Selig. Equity perpetual futures are now tradable on CFTC-registered exchanges, classified as security futures products under the Commodity Futures Modernization Act of 2000, which puts them under joint CFTC and SEC oversight.

The list of venues that can actually do this is short, and it was assembled quietly. A firm needs to be a CFTC-designated contract market and separately notice-registered with the SEC as a national securities exchange for security futures products. Seven entities hold both today: Cboe, CME, Coinbase, Kalshi, Bitnomial, Nadex, and ICE Futures U.S. Five of those seven filed inside a single 30-day window in September.

Kalshi is furthest along, with a filing covering 56 to 58 names and a launch expected within days. Coinbase has filed to list perps on Apple, Tesla and Nvidia. So those two are immediate winners. But Hyperliquid and Lighter, the top crypto perps protocols, are next in line for potential CFTC approvals and are likely future winners as well (LIT +10% on the day).

Then came the lawsuit. A new group called the Capital Markets Alliance filed in the Southern District of New York, naming Cboe, Citadel Securities and the Healthy Markets Association among its members. Its argument runs on two tracks.

The group wants the policy eliminated. The American Securities Association piled on separately, pointing out that Congress banned selling security-based swaps to retail investors at all.

A CFTC spokesman called the suit “troubling,” adding that “our markets should be governed by law, not litigation campaigns intended to block innovation.” The CME already tried this once, suing over the CFTC’s crypto perps approval on the theory that perpetuals are swaps rather than futures. That one went nowhere.

Well, remember that old phrase: “First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, then you win."

We’re at the fight stage. Next stop, winning.

#5024. Researchers Used an Old Treatment from the ‘70s to Help Alzheimer’s Patients. The Results Were Shocking.

There are a lot of treatments in development for people with Alzheimer's disease, but new research is exploring an unusual tactic that hails back to the '70s. It's called external counterpulsation (ECP), and it's a special treatment that uses squeezable cuffs to compress the lower body.

The study, which was published in the American Journal of Alzheimer’s Disease & Other Dementias, found that patients with mild cognitive impairment or early Alzheimer's disease gained several points on cognitive scales after using ECP, suggesting it may be a promising therapy worth further investigation.

The ECP contraption looks similar to compression boots that have become trendy, with a few important differences. This device extends higher to include the butt, calves, and thighs, and also inflates and deflates with the person’s cardiac cycle. Regular compression boots do not. And, while it seems random that a tool like this might help to improve memory, neurologists who treat patients with cognitive decline and Alzheimer's disease say there may be something to this news.

Meet the experts: Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, California; Liron Sinvani, MD, director of research and innovation for the Institute for Healthy Aging at Northwell Health and the Feinstein Institutes for Medical Research; and Anna Hohler, MD, regional director of Neurology for Westchester, Northwell Health, and chair of Neurology for Northern Westchester and Phelps hospitals.

For the study, researchers recruited 190 people with early Alzheimer's disease to participate in a 12-month randomized controlled trial. Participants were either given three to five weekly one-hour ECP sessions for 35 treatments or a sham treatment that exerted much less pressure. After that, they received treatments twice a week through six months. Participants went through cognitive assessments at the start of the study, and also at weeks 6, 12, 18, 24, 36, and 52.

After analyzing the data, researchers discovered that people who went through the ECP treatments had improvements in cognition, including memory, attention, language, and reasoning. They also seemed to do better at doing everyday tasks like getting dressed, eating, and doing household tasks. They continued to have cognitive benefits even after they were done receiving the treatments.

However, there are “important reasons to be cautious” about the results, says Liron Sinvani, MD, director of research and innovation for the Institute for Healthy Aging at Northwell Health and the Feinstein Institutes for Medical Research, noting that the research was funded by the company that makes the ECP device used in the study, and that the lead author works for the same company.

Doctors say that one possible explanation behind these results is the boost in blood flow that comes with this compression therapy.

"External counterpulsation is thought to improve blood flow throughout the body, including to the brain," explains Anna Hohler, MD, regional director of Neurology for Westchester, Northwell Health, and chair of Neurology for Northern Westchester and Phelps hospitals. "Because reduced cerebral blood flow has been associated with cognitive decline and Alzheimer's disease, enhancing circulation may help support brain function and potentially improve memory and attention in some patients with early-stage disease."

Dr. Sinvani agrees. "The rationale for ECP makes biological sense: By improving blood flow to the brain, enhancing vascular function, reducing inflammation, and potentially improving the brain's ability to clear toxic proteins, ECP may address a critical and often overlooked piece of the Alzheimer's puzzle," she says.

In general, "what is healthy for your heart is usually healthy for your brain, and what is unhealthy for your heart is usually unhealthy for your brain," says Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, California.

That's unclear. "I think it could, and this trial makes a compelling case for why it deserves further investigation," Dr. Sinvani says. She praises ECP's non-invasive nature and well-established safety profile as being big perks of this technology. “It is essentially a passive cardiovascular workout,” she says.

"While the early findings are intriguing, larger studies are needed before this therapy can be widely recommended for Alzheimer's disease," Dr. Hohler says. "If future research confirms meaningful cognitive benefits and long-term safety, external counterpulsation could become a useful adjunctive treatment, particularly because it is noninvasive and already has an established safety record in cardiovascular care."

There are treatment options available for people diagnosed with early Alzheimer's disease. These include FDA-approved medications to slow progression of the disease, therapies for symptoms, cognitive stimulation, physical exercise, good sleep hygiene, management of vascular risk factors, and social engagement, Dr. Hohler says.

"A comprehensive approach that combines medical treatment with healthy lifestyle interventions currently offers the best opportunity to preserve cognitive function and quality of life," she says.

Carina Hsieh, MPH, is the deputy features editor of Women’s Health. She has more than a decade’s worth of experience working in media and has covered everything from beauty, fashion, travel, lifestyle, pets, to health.

She began her career as an intern in the fashion closet at Cosmopolitan where she worked her way up to Senior Sex & Relationships Editor. While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

#5022. Justin Sun seeks $264K sanctions over World Liberty’s UAE disclosure

Sun plans to refile his token-freeze lawsuit in California state court after the sanctions motion is resolved. A hearing is set for Dec. 10.

Tron founder Justin Sun is seeking $263,821.20 in sanctions against World Liberty Financial and its lawyers, accusing the Trump family-linked crypto company of submitting a misleading court disclosure about its ownership in a legal dispute over his $45 million WLFI token investment.

In a federal court filing, Sun and two co-plaintiffs alleged that World Liberty initially listed Florida as its sole citizenship before amending its disclosure nearly three months later to identify 22 individuals and entities in its ownership chain, including those in the United Arab Emirates.

“World Liberty presumably always knew who its owners were,” Sun said. “When we asked how this happened, World Liberty refused to tell us.”

The amended disclosure came six days after a federal judge questioned World Liberty’s bid to move Sun’s claims into arbitration. World Liberty’s lawyers subsequently argued that the company’s UAE citizenship deprived the federal court of jurisdiction because foreign parties were on both sides of the lawsuit.

World Liberty Financial did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for comment.

Related: Circle shareholder sues Trump, World Liberty and Steve Witkoff over crypto deals

In April, Sun sued World Liberty, alleging fraud and the unlawful freezing of his tokens. World Liberty filed a defamation lawsuit against Sun in May, alleging false statements and violations of token-sale terms. Sun denied the allegations.

The legal back-and-forth followed a January 2025 agreement by an Abu Dhabi-backed investment vehicle to purchase a 49% stake in World Liberty for $500 million.

Sun previously backed the Trump family’s crypto ventures by investing in World Liberty and buying President Donald Trump’s TRUMP memecoin. He topped the token’s leaderboard for a May 2025 dinner with Trump, with holdings valued at about $18.5 million.

In an Aug. 26 email included in the court exhibits, World Liberty’s lawyer Sam Cleveland said his team had “recently” learned of additional citizenship information. He maintained that World Liberty already had UAE citizenship when Sun filed the lawsuit in April.

Sun’s lawyers subsequently sought corporate records and testimony about World Liberty’s ownership and when its attorneys learned of the UAE connection. They allege that World Liberty provided limited documentation but declined to explain why its original disclosure omitted that information.

According to attorney Edward Moss’s sworn declaration, the $263,821.20 request covers fees for four attorneys’ work opposing arbitration. It excludes other lawyers’ fees and costs related to jurisdictional discovery and the sanctions motion.

Sun said Tuesday he plans to refile in California state court after the sanctions motion is resolved. A hearing is scheduled for Dec. 10.

#5021. This Shell-Shaped Villa Has Mediterranean Views and a Windswept Feel

When designer Federica Gosio talks about this project in Sardinia, she describes it as “an arch over the sea.” Gosio—who leads the firm bearing her name and is also a cofounder of Studio MILO—explains, “the truly unique aspect of this home is its ability to adapt to its surroundings, the sea, and nature.” Overlooking the bay of Porto Rafael, an area on the island’s northern coast famous for its remote location and its scenic beauty, the house resembles a seashell.

The villa looks like a white shell that has emerged from the sea to embrace the surrounding landscape .

There is no single guiding principle in Gosio’s design; instead, the shell-shaped villa embodies a capacity to adapt: “The aim is to give shape to ever-changing realities, integrating it with strength and grace into a larger context,” she says. It’s an approach that characterizes many of Gosio’s projects. This one springs from the rugged land where the home is located and pays homage to its setting through its design.

A custom-made bench follows the perimeter of the portico, wrapping around the space. At its center is a set of furnishings in wood and neutral-toned fabrics.

Working with Gosio, Alessandra Radaelli, the owner of this shell-shaped villa, created an organic, sculptural retreat. “We decided to honor the design legacy of Rafael Neville,” she says, referring to the founder of Porto Rafael. That meant “favoring a harmonious approach in which architecture blends with the landscape.”

Interior designer Federica Gosio on the terrace of this unique and intriguing villa.

The villa is characterized by curved lines and pure white surfaces that also have a textured quality. Rattan and straw accents add warmth to the space.

The home’s pointed arches, organic geometries, and natural materials were inspired by the earth and the sea. “The minimalist spaces were designed to embrace the sunlight, the wind, and the slow rhythm of island life,” Gosio explains. “It’s a project that interprets the Mediterranean in a contemporary way through a refined balance of memory, nature, and design.”

Every corner of this villa blends seamlessly with its surroundings: In this outdoor space, the shapes soften and the lines curve. At the center is a white rattan lounge set.

With its soft curves, the home feels as if it has been shaped by the winds that blow across Sardinia. During the winter, the Mistral here is powerful, sweeping across the island and reshaping the landscape. It looks almost as if the same process has taken place both inside and outside the villa. The reality is that this effect was a deliberate design choice to soften the edges of the villa and smooth out its different volumes.

This semicircular arch draws one’s gaze to the sea. In the foreground are two coffee tables by Mogg. In the background, on a terrace, rattan armchairs complement the organic curves of the seaside architecture.

Both inside and out, the villa is a whitewashed structure that follows the contours of the land. Gosio and her team have done away with straight lines, opting instead for a softer silhouette. The signature element of the home is undoubtedly the pointed arch, used for both the villa’s doors and its hallways. “The soft volumes, textured surfaces, and carefully placed rocks guide the visitor toward the entrance with its hand-brushed, iron arched door, custom-designed by Daniele Mingardo,” Gosio says.

The property’s garden is an idyllic green expanse leading down to the sea with junipers and various plants native to the Mediterranean framing the villa.

Interior designer Federica Gosio (on the left) and homeowner Alessandra Radaelli.

Surrounding the home are acres of land covered in lush Mediterranean vegetation leading down to the sea, with the route including green terraces and social spaces, including the panoramic pool and an outdoor kitchen. The feeling is that of an earthly Eden planted in a corner of Sardinia.

The organic arch reveals a living space featuring an Asawa sofa by Secolo, Dune side tables by Mogg, and a vintage rattan pendant lamp purchased on the Pamono website. The Paysage II rug is by Atelier Février, the blue ceramic Anfora Anulare vase is by Walter Usai, and the cups and saucers are by LR Ceramics.

The goal of respecting and celebrating the villa’s setting is also reflected in the colors and materials used throughout the home. “The white walls, the floors in Orosei marble with a bush hammered finish and ‘broken edges,’ and the organically shaped furnishings come together to create a measured and well-proportioned design, inspired by the neutral and warm tones of the soil here,” Gosio says.

Access to the kitchen is also through a large, sweeping arch. The custom oak cabinets are paired with WA ceramic tiles by Decoratori Bassanesi on the wall. The table, which resembles a sculpture, is the Luna Elíptica by La Nena.

Orosei stone, which is found on the coast of Sardinia, not only forms the floors but also the sinks (both indoor and outdoor), a clear example of how the island was integrated into the design. Rounding out the composition is a selection of vintage rattan pieces and sculptural creations by Pretziada, a creative design practice based in Sardinia, and its artisans. Nothing is left to chance and every element contributes to the home’s exquisite design and its appealing composition.

At the center of the dining area, which overlooks the outdoor space, is a Luna Elíptica table by La Nena. Next to it is a Lia Chair by Chiara Andreatti and Pierpaolo Mandis for Pretziada, a piece that is both handcrafted and contemporary, with roots in traditional Sardinian design. The Holden Linear Pendant Uplight is from Porta Romana. On the table are several ceramic pieces by LR Ceramics.

The infinity-edge pool blends seamlessly into the villa’s architecture. Alongside it is a sundeck surrounded by lush green foliage and aromatic plants.

The outdoor spaces in this villa are extensions of its interior. Wooden furnishings with a distinct nautical flair are set amid breathtaking green spaces with ocean views. On the table is a Mini Geen-A lamp by Kartell.

A custom outdoor kitchen with views of the sea. The sink is made of Orosei marble.

The pool has views of the sea and sunsets.

The primary bedroom, with its organic lines, overlooks the pool and, beyond it, the sea. The custom headboard is upholstered in a striped fabric. Next to it is a Holden Wall Light from Porta Romana.

In the guest room, the custom headboard sets the tone. Next to it is a pendant lamp with a rattan Braided Lampshade-Bottle by Ferm Living. Atop a stone nightstand, a vase by LR Ceramics.

In this bath, Totem tiles from the Nok collection by 41zero42 add texture to the wall. The sink is by Catalano, and the faucets are from the Sirius series by Rubinetterie Stella.

This bath, with its rich textural quality, is defined by the wall-mounted sink in Orosei stone paired with a Roma faucet set by Rubinetterie Stella.

This room evokes traditional Sardinian design with a custom headboard that’s a mosaic of shapes and neutral tones, upholstered in Zinc’s Cubilete Natural fabric. The Holden Wall Light is from Porta Romana, as is the Huxley Jordan Wall Light reading lamp.

#5019. Binance hack may be ‘temporary setback’ for crypto, Grayscale’s Pandl says

Zach Pandl highlighted institutional custody safeguards as crypto markets recovered some losses following the record $6.3 billion Binance hack.

Binance’s $6.3 billion hack could temporarily hurt confidence in crypto markets but would ultimately lead to stronger security practices across exchanges, according to Grayscale.

“It also underscores the importance of institutional custody, such as offered by US-based crypto [exchange-traded products],” said Grayscale’s head of research, Zach Pandl, in a Tuesday report.

“These vehicles hold their assets at state-chartered banks and qualified custodians, typically offering offline storage, multi-signature wallets, SOC attestations and insurance policies,” he added.

Binance suffered a record $6.3 billion hack Thursday and pledged to cover all customer losses. The FBI attributed the attack to North Korea. Kim Yo Jong, sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, dismissed the allegations as “absurd rhetoric.”

The hack was followed by a market sell-off, with Bitcoin falling below $77,000 and BNB losing more than 8% over 24 hours by early Friday.

By Wednesday, total crypto market capitalization had rebounded 2% over the past 24 hours to about $2.63 trillion, after touching a weekly low of about $2.55 trillion earlier in the day. The recovery left the market about $100 billion below its Oct. 28 level.

Bitwise’s head of research, André Dragosch, told Cointelegraph that recent outflows from US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds were “related to a short-term risk-off environment rather than a structural change in institutional sentiment.”

#5016. 10 Best Airbnbs in London With Brilliant Design (2026)

If London excels at anything, it’s lodgings with soul—from the silver-service glamour of Claridge’s to the shiny new scenes of At Sloane. But these days, it’s the city’s next generation of stays—creative, design-led rentals that might include a sweet stay tucked into a mews, a contemporary warehouse with exposed brick, or a period town house on a leafy street—that capture the true spirit of living like a local. In a place where nightly rates can soar well into four figures, claiming your own corner of the city feels more like the move. Here are the London Airbnbs where you can carry on like a local—homes that are made for sinking back into after a morning finding the best coffee or after a late night in Soho.

#5014. Puking, Passing, Posting: The New Rules of Race Etiquette

More athletes than ever are showing up to starting lines of all kinds.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Race etiquette used to be pretty straightforward: Stay in your lane, don’t cut people off, and be aware of your surroundings when you stop for water. But ever since run clubs and new hybrid competitions like Hyrox have taken off, there's been an influx of athletes who have never been taught race rules. To make matters more complicated, new competition styles mean additional rules have to get ironed out in real time. Best case scenario, this might mean you bump into a few people with cameras trying to get social media content at the finish line. Worst case scenario (in rare, extreme cases), the race may turn into a literal sh*tshow.

“Racing has become much more popularized and mainstream over the years, bringing in more people who may be racing from more of a ‘fun’ standpoint rather than a competitive one,” says kettlebell coach Sarah Warshowsky, CPT, creator of the WH Build & Burn kettlebell plan who’s competed in a Hyrox race and half-marathon. “Newer racers need to respect the history, competition, and elite athletes who may have prize money on the line, while competitive racers should feel inclusive and accepting of new racers coming into their sport because in the end, it does bring more eyes to it.”

Meet the experts: Sarah Warshowsky, CPT, is a New York City–based kettlebell coach and creator of the Build & Burn kettlebell track. Kayla Jeter, CFSC, is a Lululemon athlete, wellness consultant, and veteran half-, full, and ultramarathoner. Sunny Choi, CSCS, is an Olympic breaker who’s competed in a Hyrox race.

Knowing all the ins and outs of how to act during a race isn’t exactly part of your ChatGPT training plan, but race etiquette is essential. That’s why Women’s Health tapped some of our most trusted racing experts to put together a comprehensive list of the new rules of race etiquette—here’s what to know before you head to the starting line.

Be respectful and practice spatial awareness, both when you’re maintaining a steady pace, and passing people. After all, “the point of a race is that you want to pass people and you want people to feel like they can pass you,” says Warshowsky. But you need to check your surroundings, and be strategic about when you can make those passes. For instance, if you’re about to turn a corner down a new street during a half-marathon, you might want to wait a second before you try and squeeze past a bunch of people.

Similarly, some races like Hyrox also have passing lanes for faster athletes. Do your event research ahead of time, know which lane you’re in, keep it moving, and if you need to switch, look around and try not to knock into runners, Warshowsky says.

After all, it’s your race too! “Too many times, I’ve experienced and seen others almost fall from ‘me’-centric runners moving erratically,” says Kayla Jeter, CFSC, a Lululemon athlete, wellness consultant, and veteran half-, full, and ultramarathoner. “If someone starts swerving, I’ll casually start snapping to announce my presence, I’ll also use my arms as a barrier to keep people from bumping into me.”

If you’re in desperate need of water or a walking break, stay on the lookout for an upcoming water station or less crowded area to stop by. Just like you would when riding a bike, stick out your hand to the right or left to signify that you’ll be pulling over, Warshowsky says. Then, start inching your way toward that side of the road “instead of needing to cut across an entire lane of people running,” she adds. If you need a walking break, raise your arm straight up in the air before slowing down.

“Go into every race knowing you’re not the only one on the course and are responsible for how your actions impact someone else’s race,” Jeter reminds. So, if you want to record content, do your best not to include people in the background, whether you’re at the start line, finish line, or mile 10. Want to stop and grab a couple of videos? Look around you and make sure no one is right behind you. “I’ve seen plenty of runners crash into someone crossing the finish line because someone wanted to capture their moment,” she adds.

Ultimately, just be respectful. “A creator filming themselves having their own experience does not have to bother anyone around them, as long as they are not coming in the way of anyone else's experience,” says Warshowsky.

Understand the guidelines and your race order ahead of time. At Hyrox, the stations are numbered pretty clearly, but if you get lost, don’t be afraid to ask a nearby judge for help, says Warshowsky.

After finishing up at one of the machines, participants don’t typically wipe sweat off after leaving it since that can take time off of their score. But you should make sure the station is relatively clean and the weights are in place so you’re not putting the person behind you at a major disadvantage, she adds.

PRing your race is not worth ignoring a bodily emergency. Before getting to the start line, know the nearest exits and bathrooms in each section of the race, Warshowsky says. Then, “if you have any signs of discomfort, listen to your body, because those signs are usually not there for nothing,” says Olympic breaker Sunny Choi, CSCS, who has competed in a Hyrox race. Hit the nearest bathroom, and if you don’t make it, try to find a nearby empty space to stay in until you feel better.

If you start feeling faint during Hyrox or something more serious may be going on but you still have four more stations to go, don’t power through. Tell a volunteer or someone working the course that you may need medical attention. “The only way that you can get help is if you let someone know you actually need help,” Warshowsky adds. “The safety of yourself and others around you should be number-one priority when needing to make the call whether you need to exit the race for any reason.”

Nothing New on Race Day!When it comes to clothing and sneakers, “as tempting as it can be to wear something fresh at the start line, new products are often stiff, which can cause chaffing and be distracting,” says Jeter. The same goes for food: New fuel mixed with nerves may make your stomach feel weird at an inconvenient time during the race, Choi adds. So, make sure you know what feels best in your body pre-race day by testing it ahead of time, like during a taper run.

When you’re running in the middle of the street and you have a water cup in one hand and an energy gel in the other, pulling to the side to throw them in the trash is probably not top of mind. But throwing trash anywhere on the ground can cause it to get trapped underneath athletes’ feet and potentially cause tripping. (Plus, it’s more trash for the people cleaning up afterward.) So, try your hardest to either hold onto your trash until you see a trash can, or store it in your running belt or vest for the time being, says Warshowsky.

“Without them, there is no race,” says Warshowsky. If a volunteer is giving you a directive—asking you not to step off the marathon race course to say hi to your friends, for example—don’t fight back. “It's not just about you, it's about the whole collective and everyone there,” she adds. They’re just doing their job and trying to keep everyone safe.

Remember: When you’re racing recreationally, these races are individual. “It's you versus you,” says Warshowsky. But it’s important to “be mindful of the people around you and how much time, preparation, energy, money, scheduling everyone around you had to go through as well as yourself.”

Run Your Next Race Even Faster With This 4-Week Plan

On a deeper level, remember that unless racing is your profession and there’s money on the line, “it's not always that serious,” Warshowsky adds. “The safety of yourself, your body, and the safety of everyone around you is more important” than a PR. If after a few miles—or workout stations—you know it’s not your day to beat your record, hang back, relax, and enjoy it. Remember why you race, and make it an enjoyable experience for yourself and everyone around you. There will always be another race where you can try your best.

Addison Aloian is the associate health & fitness editor at Women’s Health, where she writes and edits across the health, weight loss, and fitness verticals. She’s also a certified personal trainer through the National Academy of Sports Medicine (NASM). In her free time, you can find her lifting weights at the gym, running on the West Side Highway in New York City—she recently completed her first marathon, the 2025 New York City Marathon—and watching (and critiquing!) the latest movies that have garnered Oscars buzz. In addition to Women's Health, she’s made an appearance on the Oprah Podcast and her work has also appeared in Allure, StyleCaster, L'Officiel USA, V Magazine, VMAN, and more.

#5013. Wall Squats, Planks Once a Week May Help Lower Your Blood Pressure

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Isometric exercises, even if done only occasionally, could help improve blood pressure, according to a new study.

Researchers report that study participants who did these types of exercises once or twice a week saw reductions in blood pressure over a short period.

They added that participants who engaged in isometric exercises three times a week maintained improvements in blood pressure over a longer period.

The researchers said that previous studies had indicated that isometric exercise training reduces blood pressure more than other forms of exercise and even some drug treatments.

However, they said previous research had not established how often isometric exercise training needs to be performed to maintain these health benefits.

Isometric exercises involve a person holding a pose for a few minutes at a time. Examples include wall squats and floor planks.

The researchers said their study provides important information for people who don’t have time to exercise daily.

The researchers wrote that the study “offers evidence that lower frequency [isometric exercise training] can help maintain short-term [blood pressure] reductions, providing a pragmatic option for individuals with limited time or adherence challenges.”

They published their findings on September 29 in BMJ Open Sport and Exercise Medicine.

Experts not involved in the research said it provides helpful guidance for people who want to improve their blood pressure.

“The main takeaway is that you don’t need to exercise every day — or even several times a week — to experience significant blood pressure benefits from isometric exercise,” said Michael Fredericson, MD, a professor of orthopaedic surgery at Stanford Health Care.

“We already knew that performing isometric training for even just a few weeks can lower blood pressure,” added Amanda Paluch, PhD, an associate professor of kinesiology at the University of Massachusetts Amherst and chair of the American Heart Association’s Physical Activity Committee. “This study adds to the research by looking at how much training is needed to maintain those gains.”

“It is encouraging to see measurable improvements in blood pressure with a relatively brief and infrequent exercise routine,” said Jennifer Wong, MD, a cardiologist and medical director of Non-Invasive Cardiology at MemorialCare Heart and Vascular Institute at Orange Coast Medical Center in California. “These findings suggest that even small, consistent efforts can make a meaningful difference.”

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) estimates that nearly half of adults in the United States have high blood pressure. That’s about 120 million people.

The condition, also known as hypertension, increases the risk of heart disease and stroke.

High blood pressure occurs when the force of blood pushing through blood vessels is consistently too high.

Blood pressure readings consist of two numbers. One is the systolic pressure (top number), which measures pressure when your heart beats. The second is diastolic pressure (the bottom number), which measures the pressure between beats.

Treatment usually involves medications such as beta-blockers and ACE inhibitors, as well as lifestyle factors such as a healthy diet and exercise.

In their study, the researchers aimed to determine how much isometric exercise was needed to lower and maintain blood pressure.

The researchers recruited 100 adults with an average age of 37. Subjects included male and female participants with normal-to-high-normal systolic blood pressure (120–140 mm Hg) who engaged in less than 150 minutes per week of moderate physical activity.

The participants also had no injuries or illnesses, were not taking and had not previously taken blood pressure medication, were not smokers, and did not consume more than 14 units of alcohol per week.

The participants were divided into five groups. One group served as a control and did no isometric exercise, while the other four groups followed the same four-week home-based lower-body isometric training program.

The regimen consisted of four sessions of isometric wall squat exercise, separated by two minutes of seated rest, performed three times a week.

During the next four weeks, the participants were split into four training schedules.

One group continued to exercise three times a week. A second group exercised twice a week, while a third group exercised once a week. The remaining group stopped the exercises entirely.

Blood pressure, heart rate, and other cardiac measurements were taken at the start of the study and after four weeks and eight weeks.

The researchers reported that the four groups who completed the initial four-week exercise routine showed significant reductions in systolic and diastolic blood pressure compared to the control group.

Over the next four weeks, the researchers found no statistically significant difference in diastolic blood pressure among the groups.

However, the researchers reported that participants who continued isometric exercise training three times a week achieved the greatest reduction in systolic blood pressure compared with baseline. That group also maintained improvements in other cardiac measures.

The group that had stopped doing isometric exercises after the initial four-week period showed a reversal of their blood pressure improvements.

The researchers wrote that the study “demonstrates that while lower frequency [isometric exercise training] may be sufficient to maintain [blood pressure] reductions over the short term, higher frequencies are required to preserve these benefits and the associated vascular and cardiac autonomic adaptations.”

The researchers noted that the eight-week study period was limited and that further research is needed. They also acknowledged that the participants were from a relatively small cross-section of people.

There are several ways isometric exercises may help lower blood pressure.

“Repeat isometric exercises (contracting a muscle and holding a position) seem to change how the blood vessels regulate pressure between workouts,” said Kevin Shah, MD, a cardiologist and program director of Heart Failure Outreach at MemorialCare Heart & Vascular Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California.

“Blood pressure comes down to how hard the heart pumps and how much resistance the blood meets in the vessels,” added Paluch.

“Research suggests isometric training works mainly on that second part. It helps your blood vessels relax and open up, so blood flows through more easily.”

“Isometric exercise seems to promote cardiovascular adaptations that can lower blood pressure,” explained Fredericson. “Over time, this training can enhance the cardiovascular system’s ability to regulate blood pressure.”

Experts interviewed by Healthline say that the wall squats used in the study are one of the most beneficial isometric exercises.

They also recommend static leg extensions, handgrip exercises, planks, and exercises that involve sustained muscle contraction.

“Start slowly, with manageable goals,” Wong said. “Even one or two short sessions per week can be a starting point,” she said. “Begin with brief holds, allow adequate rest between sets and sessions, and gradually build up as strength and comfort improve. The goal is to establish a routine one can maintain consistently.”

Paluch recommended starting small and keeping it simple. She says 10 to 20 minutes per session is sufficient.

She suggested four 2-minute holds of a wall sit or handgrip squeeze with 2 minutes of rest between each hold. Those can be done two to three days a week.

“The message is that consistency matters,” she said. “If you’re able to maintain three times a week, that appears to be the best way to maintain the gain, but it’s not an all-or-nothing situation.”

“You don’t have to maintain the initial pace forever to get something out of the training, especially when life gets in the way and adherence or time becomes a challenge,” Paluch added. “Even getting in one day a week is better than nothing.”

For those just getting started with exercise, Shah recommended starting with a short walk before adding isometric exercise.

“A 10-minute walk on most days is a manageable first step,” Shah said. “Gradually increase the length of walks and then add strength training. Consistency matters more than an exhaustive routine that is not sustainable. Remember: Progress and not perfection.”

Fredericson advised starting slowly and building from there.

“For someone completely inactive, even 5 to 10 minutes of movement per day is a meaningful starting point,” he said. “Walking is an excellent option because it requires little to no equipment or preparation. From there, they can incorporate strength training two or three days a week.”

#5012. Christian Yelich’s Malibu Retreat Is on the Market for $30 Million

As Milwaukee Brewers captain Christian Yelich rides the high of the baseball team’s first World Series championship in history, the left fielder and designated hitter is angling for another win: the sale of his Malibu mansion, currently on the market for $30 million.

Six years ago, Yelich paid $5.55 million for the one-acre property, which came with architectural plans by Los Angeles–based firm KOVAC Design Studio. Yelich has decided to move his family to Wisconsin, a KOVAC representative told AD. It’s unclear if he ever actually lived in the 5,300-square-foot residence, considering it was just completed this year.

Overlooking the Pacific Coast Highway and the ocean beyond, the modern four-bedroom, six-bathroom home is lined with walls of glass that make the most of the sweeping water views and slide open to unite the indoor and outdoor spaces. Sprawling patios are connected to most of the rooms, with the main level hosting a particularly spacious deck with an infinity pool, an outdoor shower, a bathroom, and plenty of space to lounge or entertain, including multiple fire pits, a dining area, and an outdoor kitchen.

Upon entrance, guests are greeted by a sun-drenched, double-height foyer with a floating staircase leading to the upper floor bedrooms—uniquely, each one has its own refrigerator tucked in the closet. Deeper into the dwelling, a living area boasts a fireplace and a wet bar. Nearby, the blonde wood and white marble kitchen boasts a long center island with seating for five. Another highlight is the home theater, complete with a concession room, tiered recliner seating, an LED video wall, professional-grade acoustics, and surround sound.

#5011. Canton’s CC rises 4% as SEC staff view it as ‘digital commodity’

SEC staff’s no-action letter covers CC earned by running infrastructure and applications, but is not a binding ruling on its legal status.

Canton Network’s native cryptocurrency rose 4% on Wednesday after the US Securities and Exchange Commission’s (SEC) Division of Corporation Finance issued a no-action letter stating it views the token as a “digital commodity.”

Canton Coin (CC) traded at $0.098 at 10:27 am UTC, but was still down roughly 4.2% over the past week, according to CoinMarketCap.

In its Tuesday letter, SEC staff accepted the explanation that CC’s value comes from how the network operates and demand for the token, rather than a central team’s efforts to generate profits for holders.

The division also said it would not recommend enforcement action over CC’s issuance without securities registration, provided the tokens are created under the conditions described by Canton.

In Friday’s letter to the SEC, Canton developer Digital Asset said network participants earn CC by operating infrastructure and applications, with the protocol determining how many tokens they can mint based on their activity.

The SEC issued guidance in March distinguishing digital commodities, such as Bitcoin and Ether, from securities. It said these tokens are not themselves securities, although the way they are offered and sold can still be subject to securities laws.

The SEC’s response reflects its staff’s position and is not a binding ruling on CC’s legal status.

SEC staff previously issued token-related no-action letters to crypto projects, including DoubleZero (2Z) in September 2025 and Solana-native decentralized physical infrastructure network (DePIN) project Fuse in November 2025.

#5008. The 25 Best Places to Live in the US (2026-2027)

Each year U.S. News & World Report releases a list of the best places to live in the US. While typically these top-rated locales include a number of big players, like Miami and New York, in the most recent list, suburbs reign supreme and major metropolises are notably absent. To determine the best cities in the country, U.S. News considers a multitude of factors, including quality of life, the local job market, value for your money, and the place’s desirability. And while we agree that all these factors are important in determining where to live in America—which is also ranked on AD’s list of best countries to live in—we’d also throw access to art and design into the mix. That’s why we used the U.S. News list as a jumping-off point—zeroing in on the best places for design lovers among them.

Before you read on, a little more about the methodology:

Carmel, Indiana, earned the No. 1 spot on the U.S. News list thanks in large part to a healthy job market. In this suburb about 20 miles north of Indianapolis, unemployment is lower than the national average, and the median household income is higher: $144,615, compared with $83,181. Plus, Carmel packs on the Midwestern charm, with friendly residents, a walkable downtown, and safe neighborhoods. The schools are excellent here as well. Carmel High School landed on the U.S. News Best High Schools list. And though homes in Carmel are pricier than the national average ($477,625 compared with $359,870), you get plenty for the money.

U.S. News determines quality of life based on how satisfied residents are with their daily lives, considering factors such as crime rates, quality of education, quality and availability of health care, and commute times. Tamarac, Florida, ranked No. 1 for quality of life, with Palo Alto, California; Doral, Florida; and Bethesda, Maryland (included on our list below), right behind it. For those who want quality of life outside of the US, consider our list of the best countries for Americans to move to.

On a separate U.S. News list of The Most Affordable Places to Live in the US, Eagle Pass, Texas, took top honors. Broken Arrow, Oklahoma, landed in the top 20 (at spot 12) and is included on our list below. But within the Best Places to Live list, U.S. News examined each city’s median annual household income compared with home prices in the area. And as you’ll see below, good design and affordable real estate aren’t mutually exclusive. Average housing costs in Ankeny, Iowa; Pearland, Texas; Broken Arrow, Oklahoma; Sioux Falls, South Dakota; El Paso, Texas; Appleton, Wisconsin; Farmington Hills, Michigan; Oklahoma City, Oklahoma; and Cedar Rapids, Iowa, are below the national average, and all of these places have landed on our design-centric list.

We chose our top 25 from the U.S. News list based on access to museums, art, cultural institutions, and top-notch architecture. We also considered which cities best fuel creative energy and make the best places to live for designers, artists, and innovators.

Indiana is a highly celebrated state, claiming the first two spots on the U.S. News list with Carmel and Fishers. Texas also makes a number of appearances in the top 10, thanks to Flower Mound, Leander, Frisco, and Sugar Land. But in a separate U.S. News list of the Best States, the top honor goes to Utah, which earns high marks for its fiscal stability, infrastructure, and excellent education.

The Carmel Arts and Design District is at the heart of the suburb.

Anyone who regularly reads best-places-to-live lists will instantly recognize Carmel—this Indianapolis suburb is always a favorite. It has superb schools, low crime, and a thriving job market. It also has a buzzing Arts and Design District, with galleries, studios, locally owned shops, and public art and sculptures. Plus, the district is a major hub on the Indiana Glass Trail, a collection of artists and studios dedicated to the region’s rich history in glassmaking that stretches across eight counties in the Hoosier State.

A train station in Fishers that was later turned into a pedestrian walkway

Coming in just behind Carmel, Fishers ranks second for places to live in Indiana and second in the national ranking, according to U.S. News. And back in 2017, Money Magazine named it the best place to live in the country. Located about 30 minutes north of Indianapolis, Fishers consistently earns accolades for its safety, top-notch education system, and excellent outdoor attractions, including 100 miles of nature trails, 24 parks, and even an outdoor music venue—the Nickel Plate District Amphitheater. The city also packs a punch in terms of cultural offerings, most notably through The Fishers Art Center, where locals can take pottery, painting, and drawing classes, and explore contemporary art exhibitions.

As the name suggests, Flower Mound is rich in natural beauty, with nearly 1,000 acres of parkland; over 75 miles of trails for hiking, biking, and horseback riding; as well as proximity to two lakes: Lake Grapevine and Lake Lewisville. The town gets its name from the wildflowers—including Texas bluebonnets, pansies, and wild foxglove—that blanket its elevated town center. Flower Mound also has top-tier schools and easy access to major metropolitan areas. Dallas is just 28 miles southeast of town, and Fort Worth is 33 miles southwest. All of the above, plus high ranks in health care and a strong economy, helped Flower Mound earn the No. 1 spot on Livability’s list of Best Places to Live in 2025.

Ankeny is a fast-growing suburb of Des Moines.

The top spot to live in the Hawkeye State, according to U.S. News, is Ankeny, a suburb of Des Moines that began as a farming community—it’s even home to a 455-acre John Deere factory that’s been in operation since 1947. But these days, there’s more to Ankeny than just soybeans, corn, and tractors. The rapidly growing suburb has its fair share of cultural attractions too, including museums such as the Ankeny Area Historical Society Museum, the Iowa Aviation Heritage Museum, and the Ankeny Art Center. Both kids and adults can take classes in pottery, drawing, watercolors, and acrylics at the art center. That is, of course, when they’re not busy cycling the town’s 25-mile High Trestle Trail, which is an art attraction in itself. At night, Blue LED fixtures light up a bridge on the trail, creating a mesmerizing portal-like display. The glowing bridge was made possible by a public art grant, illustrating Ankeny’s commitment to culture in addition to first-rate schools, public parks, and a high standard of living.

Johns Creek, Georgia, is a suburb of Atlanta.

This Atlanta suburb landed in the top five on the U.S. News list (and in the top spot in last year’s ranking) thanks in large part to a healthy job market and a median household income of $163,653 (compared with the national median household income of $79,466). Additionally, the average commute time in Johns Creek is only 26 minutes. That proximity to the city also means access to Atlanta museums such as the Museum of Contemporary Art of Georgia and the High Museum of Art—the Southeast’s largest museum for visual art and features architecture by both Richard Meier and Renzo Piano. In Johns Creek itself, The Art Center is home to exhibits, classes, and workshops. Plus, public art—including various murals and sculptures—can be found in parks and along pedestrian trails around town. The town also has its own orchestra, the Johns Creek Symphony Orchestra.

Hoover Metropolitan Stadium in Hoover, Alabama

Alabama’s top place to live earns high marks for its affordability, with a lower cost of living compared with other similarly sized metro areas. The town, just 20 minutes south of downtown Birmingham, also has a great appreciation for preserving its architecture and parkland. In 1997, the city purchased the historic Aldridge estate, turning it into the Aldridge Gardens, a botanical garden that’s open to the public and offers yoga classes, art exhibits, and group tours of its remarkable hydrangea beds. The city also bestowed a Hoover Historical Society Marker upon its Park Avenue Historical District, a neighborhood with architecture that spans from the late 19th century to just after World War II. The district is home to the 1923 Bluff Park Elementary School, the 1889 Overseer’s House, and the 1910 Hale-Joseph House.

Like Hoover, Alabama, Rochester Hills has a low cost of living when you compare housing costs (an average of $388,231 vs. a national average of $359,870) and the median household income ($119,938 vs $83,181 nationally). This family-friendly town, located about 25 miles north of downtown Detroit, is also celebrated for its low crime rates, top-notch schools, and accessible health care—Henry Ford Rochester Hospital is here. The town also offers plenty to inspire art and design fans, including galleries like Rochester Fine Art Market, Subjectively Objective, and the Oakland University Art Gallery, which is in the neighboring city of Rochester.

This Austin suburb offers proximity to the state capital at a more affordable price. Here, the average housing cost is $495,712, compared with $581,402 in Austin, which is only a 30- to 45-minute drive away. Plus, the CapMetro Rail gives commuters a direct route to downtown on public transportation. Design-wise, Leander offers Texas Hill Country charm, more modern architecture, and even a master-planned community of midcentury-inspired homes. Starlight Village, where construction began about 10 years ago, is a community of homes that look like they could’ve been plucked straight from 1950s Palm Springs—butterfly roofs, orange doors, wood-paneled walls and all.

Located within North Carolina’s Research Triangle—the region that quite literally forms a triangle between Raleigh, Durham, and Chapel Hill, and their research-heavy universities (Duke, UNC-Chapel Hill, and NC State)—Cary is a desirable home for anyone interested in science and tech. The city is also ripe for residents wanting to live somewhere that embraces progressive urban design and planning. The town has even adopted a long-term plan called “Imagine Cary,” a comprehensive vision to guide growth through 2040 and create a sustainable urban-suburban lifestyle. Areas of focus under the plan include transportation, housing, shopping, and environmental initiatives. In addition to looking toward the future, the people of Cary actively preserve the past through notable restoration projects including the Page-Walker Arts & History Center, a former 19th-century hotel that’s now a hub for the arts, and the Nancy Jones House, a residence that dates back to 1803.

The Kresge Foundation headquarters in Troy, Michigan

If it was good enough for legendary architect Minoru Yamasaki, who designed the original World Trade Center, the city of Troy deserves a spot on our list. Yamasaki and his family lived in Troy for 25 years, and he operated his architecture firm, Yamasaki and Associates, out of the Detroit suburb. Locally, his firm designed their office building, the Columbia Center, and the Temple Beth El, a synagogue in nearby Bloomfield Hills. Beyond its architectural history, Troy is celebrated for its strong economy (global consumer intelligence company JD Power is headquartered here), excellent education system, and outdoor recreational offerings—the town has more than 1,000 acres of parkland.

Buffalo Creek Forest Preserve in Buffalo Grove, Illinois

The No. 1 place to live in the Land of Lincoln offers all the benefits of the ’burbs—great schools, low crime rates, and plenty of green space—with proximity to one of the country’s top cities for architecture: Chicago. Buffalo Grove is only about 30 miles northwest of the Windy City, giving its residents easy access to architectural marvels like the Frank Lloyd Wright–designed Robie House and the Wrigley Building, as well as world-class institutions such as the Art Institute of Chicago and the National Museum of Mexican Art. In Buffalo Grove itself, The Raupp Museum tells the history of the city, including an exhibit about Potawatomi life in the 1830s and a replica of an early-20th-century general store.

The Delores Fenwick Nature Center in Pearland, Texas

Affordable housing (the median housing cost is $319,753 compared to a national median of $370,489) and a low unemployment rate (3.6% compared to a national average of 4.5%) helped Pearland nab a spot in the top five on the U.S. News list. The city of 123,986 earns a place on our list because of its dedication to creative living. In 2024, it rolled out a Cultural Arts Master Plan aimed at fostering more cultural events and activities, developing a community arts and cultural center, providing more opportunities for children to participate in the arts, and creating a more vibrant downtown. Add to that, Pearland is only minutes from Houston, home to an Arts District with over 300 studio spaces for creatives of all kinds.

We like Broken Arrow—the fast-growing Tulsa suburb with a history tied to the Muscogee Creek Nation—for its buzzy arts and entertainment center, the Rose District (the name is a nod to the rosebushes that dotted the area’s original landscaping). The district is home to multiple art galleries, a performing arts center, a pavilion for a weekly farmers market, and The Museum Broken Arrow. A hub for local history, the museum gives visitors the chance to experience the town’s Main Street as it would have looked in the early 20th century, plus step inside a Civil War–era cabin. Meanwhile, U.S. News appreciates Broken Arrow for its job market, which is better than the national average, and its affordable housing—the median home value here is $251,667.

Lake Sammamish is a highlight of the Seattle suburb.

The ’burbs often get a bad rap for being nothing more than a series of strip malls, but that couldn’t be further from the truth in Sammamish—one of Seattle’s most desirable suburbs. Located about 30 minutes east of the city, Sammamish offers the best of both worlds: access to the metropolis’s tech-driven job market and jaw-dropping natural beauty. Lush forests, tranquil lakes, and the peaks of the Cascade Mountain Range are all baked into the scenery here. As such, boating, fishing, hiking, and cycling are popular activities among residents, as are visits to nearby Lake Sammamish State Park. Add to that, the city is celebrated for its excellent public schools, low crime rate, quality health care facilities, and culturally diverse community.

Called the “Technology City of the South,” Alpharetta is a hub for major corporations like ADP, Bakkt, and Aptean, and offers plenty of employment opportunities—some even say it has twice as many jobs as residents. The average household income is also higher here: $153,928 compared with $83,181 nationally. On the design front, Alpharetta is celebrated for its diverse residential styles, including Greek Revival, Queen Anne, and Craftsman homes in its historic downtown, as well as French country and European-inspired homes around Lake Windward.

Arlington Heights is located just northwest of Chicago.

Rumors abound that this northwest Windy City suburb might be the future home of the Chicago Bears’ stadium, a major perk for the area’s football fans. Other advantages of living in this city (technically an incorporated village) with a population of roughly 75,000 include access to public transportation (it’s only about 30 minutes to downtown Chicago by train), excellent public schools, a median housing cost that’s only slightly higher than the national average, and a super charming, walkable downtown. Arlington Heights is also home to the Metropolis Performing Arts Centre, where locals can take improv or acting classes as well as catch a show. And of course, with easy access to Chicago, fans of the performing arts are only minutes from the Chicago Theatre, the Auditorium Theatre, and the Lyric Opera House.

Like many of the top cities on this year’s U.S. News list, Bethesda offers the comforts of suburban living with access to a major city—in this case, Washington, DC. But that doesn’t mean locals have to commute for culture. Bethesda has plenty going on in terms of arts and entertainment. For music lovers, there’s the Strathmore, a 1,976-seat theater that regularly hosts the National Philharmonic Orchestra; and the Bethesda Theater, home to concerts as well as comedy shows. Meanwhile theater enthusiasts flock to Round House, and fans of the visual arts find plenty of eye candy in the city’s Arts & Entertainment District, with its galleries and public art.

Outdoor recreation is a strong attraction in Sioux Falls.

Catch the Big Sioux River cascading over pink quartzite rock at the 123-acre Falls Park; explore the world’s largest annual exhibit of public sculptures at SculptureWalk; and hit the powder at Great Bear Ski Valley—it’s all part of the lifestyle in Sioux Falls, South Dakota’s largest city. Living here also means affordable housing—with the median home cost at $315,767, compared to the national average of $370,489—plus no state income tax. The city’s low unemployment rate and excellent public safety are other reasons Sioux City landed in the top 50 on the U.S. News list. But, of course, the real draw here is the area’s natural beauty. In addition to the waterfalls mentioned above, the city is home to a 45-mile system of biking trails that connects various parks; this includes a 19-mile loop along the Big Sioux River.

Round Rock is within easy grasp of Austin but also boasts its own thriving cultural scene.

For those itching to live in Austin (ranked #164 on the U.S. News list) but not thrilled about the capital city’s high costs, Round Rock may be the place to be. Only about 30 minutes north of Austin, Round Rock has a median home value of $446,967, compared to $581,104 in the big city, according to U.S. News. But planting roots in this suburb doesn’t mean you have to totally sacrifice culture. Round Rock has a spirited arts scene, including public murals and sculptures in its downtown, as well as a brand-new creative hub called the City Arts Center. Opened in January 2026, the center is located in downtown’s historic Griffith Building, providing 44,000 square feet and two-story-high cathedral ceilings for art exhibitions, film screenings, and live music.

An aerial view of El Paso, Texas, and Juarez, Mexico

Affordable housing isn’t El Paso’s only draw, but it’s a big one. The average house costs $196,914 here, compared with a national average of $359,870. And for roughly 45% less, you get nearly 300 days of sunshine and the largest state park in an urban setting. Franklin Mountains State Park is almost 27,000 acres of desert terrain, including over 100 miles of trails for hiking and biking. Architecturally, you’ll find everything from adobe to Art Deco styles. The city’s Mission of Guadalupe dates all the way back to the 1650s, and the Socorro Mission was built in 1840. Prolific architect Henry Trost rolled into town in 1903, and he and his firm would eventually design more than 300 buildings in El Paso, adding Prairie, Spanish Colonial, Art Deco, Gothic, and Byzantine styles to the area.

For a luxury apartment in a historic high-rise, you might think you have to head to a major city, like New York or Chicago. But Appleton, about 30 miles southwest of Green Bay, offers architecturally unique apartment living. Its neo-Gothic Zuelke Building, constructed in 1931 and the city’s first high-rise, has been converted into luxury apartments, with the building’s original brass elevators and pink marble floors restored. Nearby, the Historic Fox River Mills Apartments are spread across three restored paper mills. The brick buildings, all of which are on the National Register of Historic Places, date from 1883 through 1915. And it’s not just the apartments that are appealing to design lovers—downtown Appleton is a hub for creativity. In addition to live music, the Trout Museum of Art, and the Fox Cities Performing Arts Center, the area is home to Creative Downtown Appleton, Inc., an organization that actively funds public art and urban design improvements.

Snowcapped Mount Rainier in Bellevue, Washington

Located directly across Lake Washington from Seattle, Bellevue earns high praise for its booming economy. Major corporations, including Microsoft, Amazon, T-Mobile, and Expedia Group, have offices here, and the average household income is $172,076, compared with a national average of $83,181. Of course it’s a good thing that wages run higher here, as the average home value is a staggering $1,304,230. But if you can swing the high cost, you’ll also get top-tier schools, an exceptionally safe and clean city, and extraordinary outdoor experiences. Take, for example, Bellevue Downtown Park, a 21-acre oasis in the heart of the city that features a 240-foot-wide waterfall and views of Mount Rainier in the distance.

Farmington Hills is about 20 miles northwest of downtown Detroit.

This Detroit suburb has Michigan’s largest dedicated municipal Cultural Arts Division, which coordinates year-round programs, including classes, concerts, and exhibitions. Farmington Hills is also home to The Hawk—an award-winning 270,000-square-foot recreation and arts venue that includes the Hawk Theatre, where Grammy-celebrated musicians have performed; several 2D and 3D art studios; gallery spaces that showcase local, regional, and national talent; and the Hawk Makerspace, an immersive shared creative and technical studio. The city’s Cultural Art Division also plans and juries Art on the Grand, an annual fair held in neighboring Farmington each June. The event features paintings, ceramics, and photography for sale, plus art demos and live music.

Though the median household income here is just under the national average, both the unemployment rate and average housing cost are also below the national average. In terms of cultural appeal, Oklahoma’s largest city and its capital has a number of museums, including the Oklahoma City Museum of Art and performing arts venues that host ballets, operas, and musical theater productions. Plus, OKC’s Paseo Arts District, a neighborhood just north of downtown, has 80 working artists and 20 studios that take part in an art walk the first Friday of every month, sharing their latest collections.

In the Hawkeye State, Cedar Rapids has a reputation as an artistic hub. In the 1920s and ’30s, American Regionalist painter Grant Wood, best known for his work American Gothic, lived here. Residents can enjoy his creations at various locations around town, including at the Cedar Rapids Museum of Art, home to the world’s largest collection of work by Wood; at the Veterans Memorial Building, which has a Wood-designed 24-foot-tall stained-glass window; and at the Grant Wood Studio, the artist’s former home and workspace, now owned and operated by the Cedar Rapids Museum of Art. Add to that, a number of Wood’s paintings can be found hanging in the town’s elementary and high schools, giving local kids regular access to celebrated art.

Job opportunities in tech, education, and health care abound in Austin, as do activities for the design-loving crowd. Museums in town include The Contemporary Austin, the Blanton Museum of Art, and the Mexic-Arte Museum. The last in that list is located in Austin’s bustling Warehouse District, where old warehouses have been ingeniously redesigned to host restaurants, bars, and shops. Twice a year, the arts nonprofit Big Medium coordinates studio tours, offering a peek behind the creative curtains of nearly 500 local artists and makers.

#5007. France backs reporting for self-custodied crypto worth 100K euros or more

The amendment would require taxpayers to report holdings worth at least $112,000, but the measure still needs to survive the budget process.

The French National Assembly approved an amendment that would require taxpayers to report self-custodied crypto holdings totaling at least 100,000 euros ($112,000) to tax authorities.

Amendment I-2701 was adopted on Tuesday in a 150–44 vote, with eight abstentions, according to the Assembly’s official voting record. The threshold would be assessed on Dec. 31 of the relevant tax year.

Separately, the Assembly rejected an amendment that would have exempted gains on crypto payments for goods and services from tax if the payments totaled no more than 1,000 euros ($1,120) during the tax year. The proposal was defeated 69–14, according to the Assembly’s transcript.

The reporting requirement forms part of the 2027 Finance Bill and would need to be retained in the final legislation to become law. The Assembly faces a Nov. 17 deadline to complete its first reading of the bill. Senate debate is scheduled to begin Nov. 20, according to its schedule.

The National Assembly postponed Tuesday’s scheduled vote on the budget’s revenue section for the second time as lawmakers continued debating amendments. The vote was originally scheduled for Oct. 20.

The vote’s postponement itself does not introduce a significant change, but the “likely rejection” of the budget’s revenue section could “result in the rejection of the entire bill in the National Assembly, including provisions adopted by way of amendments,” Faustine Fleuret, global head of public affairs at lending protocol Morpho, told Cointelegraph.

On Friday, the Assembly rejected two proposals that would have applied exit taxes to unrealized crypto gains when taxpayers transferred their tax residence abroad.

Lawmakers also approved an amendment on Oct. 16 that would tax gains from converting crypto into stablecoins. The proposal covers stablecoins classified as electronic money tokens under EU rules and would still need to be retained in the final law.

Related: French Senate adopts bill for automatic sharing of crypto tax data
