#5005. Low-Energy Diet Plus Structured Exercise Reverses Early Onset Diabetes

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A new study has found that a combination of a low-energy diet and structured exercise reversed early onset type 2 diabetes in about half of participants.

The research focuses on adults under 45 years old who developed type 2 diabetes early in life. The authors presented their findings at the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting, held September 28 to October 2. The study was published on October 1 in The Lancet Regional Health Americas.

Type 2 diabetes is a condition in which the body becomes less able to respond to insulin or has difficulty producing it, leading to elevated blood sugar and low energy. If untreated or not properly managed, it can lead to serious complications such as vision problems, kidney disease, and nerve damage.

These findings offer hope for better treatment options for people with this condition, which the authors say is known for causing faster health decline when compared to later-onset diabetes.

The study was conducted at three centers in England and Canada, linked with the University of Leicester, McGill University, and the University of Alberta.

Researchers selected adults ages 18 to 45 who had early onset type 2 diabetes and obesity but were not using insulin therapy.

A total of 96 participants were randomly divided into two groups: one received usual care, and the other took part in a program combining a low-energy diet with supervised and independent exercise.

The intervention group followed a strict diet of 800-900 calories per day, consisting of 30% protein, 50% carbohydrates, and 20% fat. Participants were encouraged to drink at least 2 liters of calorie-free fluids daily and were given a fiber-based laxative if needed.

For the first two weeks, the diet was entirely based on meal replacements (Optifast). From week 3 to week 12, the same calorie limit was maintained, with greater food variety, including one meal per day made from regular foods such as protein sources, one portion of fruit, and two portions of non-starchy vegetables.

The exercise program involved supervised aerobic and resistance training twice a week and one session of independent exercise per week during the first 12 weeks. After this, from weeks 13 to 24, participants followed a weight-maintenance diet and exercised independently.

Participants stopped all glucose– and blood pressure-lowering medications at the start of the diet unless they had a specific kidney condition called albuminuria (high levels of a protein called albumin in the urine).

The main goal of the study was to determine how many participants achieved remission of their diabetes after 24 weeks. Remission was defined as having a blood sugar level below the diabetes threshold (measured by glycated hemoglobin or HbA1c less than 6.5%) and not taking any diabetes medication for at least 12 weeks.

The study also examined secondary outcomes such as fitness levels, body composition, and heart health.

After 24 weeks, 54% of participants in the diet and exercise group achieved remission, compared with only 4% in the usual care group.

They also lost weight. The diet and exercise group lost an average of 8.4 kilograms (about 18.5 pounds), while the usual care group lost only 1.2 kilograms (about 2.6 pounds).

After adjusting for various factors, the diet-and-exercise group lost about 7.5 kilograms more than the control group.

Additionally, most of this weight loss was fat, with about 6.7 kilograms of fat lost at 24 weeks, which the researchers note is important for improving diabetes.

According to the researchers, the intervention helped preserve muscle mass, which is usually lost during weight loss. This is significant because maintaining muscle is important for long-term health, strength, and metabolism.

They further highlighted that early onset type 2 diabetes can cause serious health problems at a young age.

However, their study shows that a combined approach of diet and exercise can not only help people lose weight and improve health but can also lead to real remission of diabetes, without needing medication.

This is important because early onset type 2 diabetes usually worsens quickly and is harder to manage than diabetes that starts later in life.

The researchers additionally believe their approach could be especially helpful for younger adults who may be planning to have a child, as managing diabetes early is critical for health during and after pregnancy.

The study’s success in a multi-ethnic population also adds to its significance, showing the program could work for diverse groups.

The researchers noted that most people in the diet-and-exercise group experienced side effects early in the program, including constipation, headaches, dizziness, and fatigue. However, these were mostly mild and resolved over time.

Avery Zenker, a registered dietitian and medical writer, explained the importance of this work. “We have evidence that type 2 diabetes doesn’t have to be an inevitably progressive disease,” Zenker told Healthline.

“This can give hope for people with diabetes and help empower them to make impactful lifestyle changes.”

Zenker, who was not involved in the study, added that type 2 diabetes remission is not only possible but more likely in younger people who tend to have a shorter disease duration and a less complicated health history.

Jennifer Pallian, BSc, a registered dietitian and founder of Foodess, who wasn’t involved in the research, said that you can put these findings into action right now.

First, you can start a lower-calorie diet. “A reasonable target used in the research was to reduce daily calorie intake by about 500–750 calories, with the goal of losing at least 5% of starting body weight,” she told Healthline.

Zenker noted there are other factors to consider besides the number of calories. “There’s a big difference between a low calorie diet that’s predominantly whole foods compared to one that’s mostly ultra-processed and nutrient-poor,” she said.

“It’s important to consider other recommendations like whole foods, high fiber, and reduced intake of ultra-processed foods.”

Along with making dietary changes, Pallian advised working toward getting at least 150 minutes of moderate-intensity activity per week. “It also helps to make the plan measurable,” she said. “Set specific nutrition and activity goals, keep track of what and how much you eat, and record your physical activity too.”

Pallian suggested taking note of situations where it’s harder to stay on track so you can spot patterns and make adjustments.

Zenker further noted the importance of starting small and not feeling as if going to the gym is the only way to get exercise.

“Some simple ways one might start to increase daily activity could include adding a short body weight routine in the morning, taking a short walk after a meal, or dancing to music at home,” she said. “Even activities like yard work or cleaning the house can count as physical activity.”

Finally, Pallian advised regular visits with a healthcare professional to monitor blood sugar and blood pressure. These values may improve with weight loss, which may require your doctor to adjust your medication regimen.

#5004. The 6 Best Undereye Patches, Tested and Reviewed by Dermatologists and Beauty Editors

The entire Women's Health team swears by them.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

I’m always up late doomscrolling TikTok and The White Lotus subreddit for season 4 theories. (Natasha Rothwell hive, stand up!) The only problem? Those late nights mean I have dark circles and puffiness in the morning (boo!). While I'm a huge fan of undereye creams, sometimes I get bored and need to switch things up. Enter undereye patches. They're a fast fix for dryness, puffiness, and dark circles—and even help me fake eight hours of sleep. "The skin under the eyes is prone to changes related to aging skin, such as fine lines, and conditions like dryness and discoloration," says dermatologist Brendan Camp, MD. In just 10 to 20 minutes, undereye patches temporarily hydrate the delicate skin and help me look refreshed. Plus, they're super cute and add a fun touch to my selfies, even if they're shared to just my Close Friends circle on Instagram Stories.

Like any other skin care product, eye masks really get their power from their ingredients, some of which are typically hyaluronic acid, vitamin C, niacinamide, and caffeine.

“Eye patches incorporate key ingredients like hyaluronic acid to boost moisture, peptides to firm, ceramides to strengthen the skin barrier, caffeine to help reduce puffiness, and niacinamide to reduce discoloration,” says dermatologist Marisa Garshick, MD.

As with any beauty product, there are always a few things to consider before making the investment. Since these masks are mostly advertised for their ability to soothe and hydrate, you'll want to check the hydrating and firming ingredients, and keep your expectations realistic since results are usually temporary. Here's what to look out for.

For starters, look for key ingredients such as aloe, chamomile, cucumber, green tea, glycerin, or hyaluronic acid. You're less likely to come across vitamin C and retinol, but it's a plus if you do. Patches with a cellulose gel material can also prevent them from sliding off your face (a con I often deal with and try to avoid as much as possible).

Not only is the skin under your eyes thinner and more delicate than in other areas of your face, but it’s also where lymphatic fluid drainage occurs, says dermatologist Lauren Penzi, MD. It can also show more blood vessels, and in turn, make dark circles appear more visible, says Dr. Garshick. Most eye masks are created to reduce fine lines, undereye darkness, and wrinkles while giving your skin the boost of moisture it needs. The overall effect of these patches is to revive and hydrate, which is why so many people use them to get a more refreshed look.

Many people complain about looking tired, and eye patches are a nice, quick fix. Still, it's important to be realistic about the kind of results you'll get in just one or two uses. So, you’ll want to zero in on the reason you’re buying patches, says Dr. Camp. For example, you can target puffiness by rocking undereye patches infused with caffeine or green tea while getting ready for work, says Dr. Camp.

As a member of the beauty team at Women’s Health, I’m constantly on the hunt for the best products and spend a lot of my time testing and reviewing the latest drops. I frequently consult skin experts, which is exactly what I did to kick off my selection process here. I consulted five New York-based dermatologists and had them give their recommendations with insight on why these undereye masks are so splurge-worthy. Then, I tested several of their picks and compared them to some of the other undereye masks available in our beauty closet.To narrow down the list even further, I got a few other members of team WH, including Addison Aloian, Lily Wohlner, Brian Underwood, and Danielle Jackson, to get in on the testing process. Ingredients, price, and overall efficacy were taken into account to determine which would be best for a wide range of needs and preferences.Here are the fruits of our labor.

After testing tons of undereye masks, this one takes the cake for best overall. Why? It's the most hydrating option on this list and it doesn't slip off your face, which is super hard to find in an undereye mask.

ICYMI, Wander Beauty basically started the undereye mask trend. This OG mask is made with peptides, amino acids, and hyaluronic acid to improve your skin's texture, tone, and hydration. It's also expert-approved by Dr. Garshick and dermatologist Dendy Engelman, MD. "I love that this mask is made with three different layers that allow the formula to penetrate the skin deeply and prevent it from sliding off the face, so you can multitask while you wear it," says Dr. Engelman.

Wander Beauty patches are as effective as they are Instagrammable. I’ve been a fan of the rose gold and yellow gold patches for five years, and all of my beauty editor friends gush over them. They brighten, hydrate, and plump up my fine lines. They also contain calendula extract and aloe vera to help reduce puffiness. Ahem, hi holiday parties and upcoming travel—I’m looking at you.

These pretty patches have three unique layers: the surface's foil retains heat and prevents evaporation, the middle grips to prevent slippage, and the base delivers moisturizing glycerin and depuffing calendula extract. For an extra soothing experience, I like to leave mine in the fridge. Cool temperatures help shrink blood vessels under the eyes, so dark circles appear lighter and puffiness minimizes, dermatologist Marisa Garshick, MD, said in our guide to undereye skin care.

That said, the construction of this mask consists of super light and flexible material versus the conventional plastic material found in a lot of other eye patches that were tested, giving this product a high-quality feel.

I would be remiss if I didn't mention my personal fave patch from Patchology. It contains hyaluronic acid (hi, hydrated, plump skin) and feels super cool under the eyes to de-puff and smooth out that area.

Patchology is fantastic for targeting lines, says dermatologist Anar Mikailov, MD. He mentions that there's resveratrol, a natural antioxidant found in grapes that helps improve barrier function, and other antioxidants that provide protection against oxidizing agents that can visibly age the skin by causing wrinkles. There’s also antioxidant-rich strawberry extract, which helps brighten dark circles.

After using this patch, I also noticed my undereye area was less puffy and more luminous. My only con was that the mask slides out of place after a while, but it’s still epic to use and totally worth incorporating into your routine. My hack is to use these patches while lying down in bed to prevent any slipping.

This undereye mask is basically an expert at reducing puffy, tired-looking eyes thanks to its cooling effects. It also temporarily plumps up my fine lines with hydrating hyaluronic acid.

This option comes with a lot of serum, so it may slip down your face over time. But, feel free to go ahead and massage that extra serum into your undereye area—that little bit of massaging can help assist with lymphatic drainage and further reduce puffiness. Need even more convincing? Celebs like Jessica Alba post about this mask on her Instagram story time and time again, noting that she's a major fan.

Most masks on this list only need 10 minutes on the skin, but this one should stay on for up to 20. Although, 20 minutes fly by when you have a good book in hand (pro tip!).

These eye masks from Live Tinted are packed with copper peptides and banana extract (hi, Ashton Hall!) to reduce puffiness and smooth fine lines. They also reduce puffiness after just a few uses and leave skin looking glowy and feeling hydrated.

And because these patches help soften and smooth your delicate undereye area, Aloian relies on them for makeup prep. "Not only is the patch super cute—it's shaped like a sun—but it also genuinely hydrates and brightens my undereye area," she says. These patches make Aloian feel confident going makeup free because of the glow they leave behind, but it's just as helpful on days when she wants a dewy base before applying concealer.

Along with a mix of peptides to help stimulate collagen production, this eye mask contains niacinamide, bakuchiol, caffeine, and vitamin C. Bakuchiol is a great retinol alternative that helps stimulate collagen production, says Dr. Mikailov.

Wohlner also gave this patch a try and loves how flexible it is under the eye. However, she did note that it does tend to slip down the face because it's super thin.

This vitamin C-packed eye patch is perfect for helping brighten up the undereye area while reducing signs of aging and puffiness with the help of caffeine.

Yeah, it's a little pricier, but trust that it's totally worth it if you have the budget. The gel patches are super easy to apply. Plus, they can reduce discoloration and fine lines within just a few weeks. Sometimes I'm wary of products with vitamin C in them because the smell can be a bit aggressive, but these are just right.

Derms also deem this patch worth the money. "The key ingredient in this mask is THD Ascorbate Vitamin C, which is 50 times more powerful than other forms of vitamin C," says Dr. Engelman. THD is especially good for surface dark spots that need brightening rather than deeper dark circles, says Dr. Mikailov.

It's worth noting that these dry up around the 10-minute mark, and while they stay on really well, the edges break off easily if you try to move the masks around a few minutes after the initial application.

As the name suggests, this patch is meant to soothe tired-looking eyes. So, for those of you who have a lot of sleepless nights or are just night owls at heart, this is your winner. Infused with caffeine, niacinamide, and hyaluronic acid, these work overtime to reduce puffiness and dark circles.

"I often recommend Peace Out’s acne patch for the face, but I also love the brand’s eye patches," says Dr. Engelman. "It's so effective at quickly bringing down puffiness and brightening and smoothing the under-eyes." The patch is biodegradable and made with biocellulose, which helps the formula deeply penetrate the skin, says Dr. Engelman.

These patches fit snugly under my eyes, so I could wear them while doing my eye makeup. They also did a great job of catching eyeshadow fallout that would normally ruin my undereye concealer. Another pro? Unlike most gel patches, they weren’t completely soaked in serum, which can make the process a bit messy. Instead, they had just the right amount for ample hydration. After the initial use, I noticed my undereyes were softer and brighter, and I’d slowly reach for these patches for special occasions.

I’m not the only fan on WH’s beauty team. “I have very sensitive skin, so I'm always cautious when it comes to trying things out, but this didn't give me any issues,” says tester Sabrina Talbert. “It also doesn't have much of a scent, which put my mind at ease since fragrance tends to be an early indicator that my skin might not like a product.”

These patches are on the larger side, so they feel a bit bulky. They also need to be slightly adjusted because they slide down a little bit over 15 minutes, but honestly, they’re such minor cons that Talbert continues using them.

#5003. 9 Decorative Plates for When You’re Sick of Boring Dishes (2026)

The great thing about decorative plates is that they don’t have to disappear into storage once dinner is over. Hang a few on a display wall, prop one up alongside your favorite cookbooks, or work it into a shelf vignette. So what makes one worth showing off? It’s the details that spark curiosity: A scalloped edge, an unexpected color combination, or a hand-painted pattern can be enough to make you look twice. “Any plate with a fun decorative motif, bright colors, or an interesting shape makes it more than just simply functional,” says Olympia Irving of Carolina Irving and Daughters.

Of course, good looks are only part of the appeal. The most memorable pieces often have a story to tell, whether it's a vintage find or a handmade design with charming, artisanal characteristics. Even the finish can make a statement. “I often find myself drawn to surfaces that look like they’re in flux—glazes that pool unpredictably or finishes that suggest movement or fragility, even in solid materials," says Anna Jewsbury, founder and artistic director of Completedworks.

There's no need to play by the rules of a perfectly coordinated table setting. A little contrast, a playful border, or a beautifully imperfect finish makes for a more interesting table—one that looks like it can only belong to you. Ahead, nine decorative plates that deserve a spot in your regular rotation and perhaps a prime position in your china cabinet.

For a plate that has lived a completely different life before landing on your table—try this Completedworks dinner plate made from recycled glass. Sculptural and subversive, it’s made from broken windows, melted glass bottles, and is repurposed using old engine oil as fuel. “We often play with the illusion of softness in materials that are anything but. Those moments where form and material are at odds are the ones I find most compelling,” Jewsbury shares.

Transport yourself to the French countryside with this Villeroy & Boch dinner plate. Part of the brand's beloved French Garden collection, its fruit-and-floral motif brings to mind sun-drenched afternoons and leisurely lunches in the garden. Even fruit and pastries straight from the market become a proper breakfast spread.

Does anything scream summer more than seashell-shaped splatterware? These Concha plates by editor-favorite brand Carolina Irving & Daughters bring together a seaside-inspired shape along with a creative, painterly glaze. “It is easy to make a table setting pop by only adding a few decorative touches, whether that is a dessert plate or a salt dish,” Irving says. “Truthfully, we love to use decorative plates as our everyday dishware!”

Designed by Cabana and made by heritage Italian porcelain house Ginori 1735, the painterly motif on this plate adds an element of rustic Italian charm to the table. Whether you stick to a cohesive set or play around with different colors and patterns for a more collected vibe, these plates can make even the simplest meals feel storied.

Score a vintage-inspired find without the hours spent scouring antique shops. Olive Ateliers did the legwork with its Vincent Plate, featuring a hand-painted floral motif, an aged blue rim, and an ivory ceramic base. “A plate becomes more when it holds history,” shares Laura Sotelo, cofounder of Olive Ateliers. “The wow isn’t about perfection—it’s about the story it carries. It should feel like it belonged somewhere long before it ended up in your hands.”

Each plate is finished by hand, giving it the character of a one-of-a-kind discovery. One important distinction: This plate isn't food-safe, so save it for a spot on your wall or shelf instead.

The soft pale yellow tones and the scalloped edge are some of our favorite details about these affordable, vintage-inspired dinner plates by West Elm. They are perfect for those who want to dip their toes into a decorative tabletop but aren’t quite ready to splurge. Sold in sets of four or eight, they strike the right balance between charm and accessibility and are ideal for daily use.

Your next dinner party can channel a little Italian holiday spirit with these hand-painted plates, crafted in a family-run workshop on the Amalfi Coast. They're available in two sizes, so they're equally charming piled high with antipasti or passed around the table with the main course.

A collaboration between L’Objet and South African artist Ruan Hoffmann, this plate brings a bold edge to the table with its painterly design and hand-applied 24k gold accents. While it’s smaller in dimension (6.5 inches), it makes a subtle statement and can complement your existing dinnerware.

If you're a fan of British artist Tess Newall's painterly work, you'll be happy to know her designs have made their way to the dinner table, courtesy of Porta. The “Ottoline” plates take inspiration from English country houses, Bloomsbury interiors, and traditional European ceramics. These hand-painted pieces are adorned with scrolling motifs, dotted edges, and wave detailing in contrasting shades of slate blue and rust. Sold in sets of four, they're a lovely way to bring a little art history to the table.

#5000. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Democrats won control of Congress as a CertiK executive warned the CLARITY bill was “likely dead.” In Europe, ZachXBT identified French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the suspected victim of a crypto theft exceeding $133 million.

Democrats have won control of both chambers of the US Congress in the midterm elections, giving the party greater influence over crypto legislation and oversight of President Donald Trump’s administration.

At the time of writing, Democrats had secured 228 House seats to Republicans’ 139, with 68 races still uncalled, according to the Associated Press. The Senate tally stood at 54 seats for Democrats and 44 for Republicans.

The result could derail the CLARITY crypto market structure bill, according to Stefan Muehlbauer, head of US government affairs at CertiK.

“The immediate read on this election result is that CLARITY bill is likely dead,” he said in comments shared with Cointelegraph, referring to both the upcoming lame-duck session and the next Congress.

Muehlbauer said he expected Sen. Elizabeth Warren and Rep. Maxine Waters, both outspoken crypto critics, to chair the Senate Banking Committee and House Financial Services Committee, respectively. Democratic control could also bring greater congressional scrutiny of Trump-linked digital asset businesses.

Onchain investigator ZachXBT has identified French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the alleged victim of a crypto theft exceeding $133 million, warning that total losses could be higher.

On Wednesday, the pseudonymous crypto sleuth said the institution, which he described as having $235 billion in assets, was allegedly exploited over multiple days. He also suggested that additional losses may have occurred through over-the-counter (OTC) transactions that were not included in earlier estimates.

“I suspect the amount stolen is larger when accounting for OTC activity,” ZachXBT said.

The bank’s alleged involvement has not been independently verified, and ZachXBT has not disclosed how the attackers gained access to the funds.

The disclosure follows ZachXBT’s initial report on Monday, when he identified over $106 million in suspicious withdrawals from centralized exchanges.

On Tuesday, he raised the estimated losses to over $133 million after discovering another $27 million in withdrawals, some of which occurred after his initial warning.

#4998. Where Does Mark Ruffalo Live? Exploring the Avengers Star’s East Coast Real Estate Portfolio

He’s currently booked and busy, but where does Mark Ruffalo live when he’s not filming? The Oscar-nominated actor appears in five projects released in 2026 alone. With that kind of schedule, the Marvel star must maintain somewhat of a nomadic lifestyle. It’s no surprise that, for years, he and his family juggled properties on both coasts. These days, however, Ruffalo is all in on New York—when he isn’t traveling to movie sets around the world.

Read on to learn more about the Emmy winner’s homes in the Big Apple and beyond.

Ruffalo first visited the small town of Callicoon in New York’s Catskills region in the mid-’90s, according to a December 2017 interview with Men’s Journal. Soon after visiting Callicoon, the 28-year-old Ruffalo paid $63,000 for his first home there: a 27-acre property with a one-room cabin. Around 2001, he and his wife, Sunrise Coigney, purchased an estate there, on a former dairy farm, complete with a 19th-century post-and-beam barn. “Smartest thing I ever did,” the Avengers star told the outlet. “It ended up having a huge influence on my life in many ways.”

At first, Ruffalo and his family split their time between their Los Angeles and New York homes. But that soon began to wear on the couple, spurring the decision to sell their Hollywood Hills property in 2009 and move to Callicoon full-time. “The whole guiding idea for living upstate was to simplify,” Ruffalo said in 2011. “It’s the best thing I could have done for the family as a whole and for the kids in particular.… We’re letting them grow a little wild, and we step in when it’s essential, but in a lot of ways they figure stuff out on their own and they do it in a way that’s organic and lasts. We live in a small space and we’re really influenced by the natural world around us. Eventually that is reflected in one’s behavior and how you get along with other people. There’s a lot of quiet in the country and there’s a lot of quietness in my children.”

On the farm, the Now You See Me actor built a woodworking shop, a chicken coop, and a garden where he grows “strawberries, rhubarb, tomatoes, basil, corn, a beautiful asparagus bed that’s five years old, and watermelon, which is hard to grow up here,” Ruffalo said. “Now I’m doing a little orchard—raspberries, Honeycrisp apples, blueberries, a pear tree.”

In the mid-2010s, Ruffalo moved his primary residence back to NYC for his children’s schooling. “We try to get to our place in Callicoon as much as we can on weekends and holidays and of course spend the summer there,” he said in 2016. “It’s a place I will always hold dear to my heart, filled with lifetime friends and lots of positive sentiment. It’s the best, most-interlocked community I have ever lived in save for my early years in Los Angeles when I was scraping it out to become an actor.” Ruffalo also compared the region to his upbringing in Wisconsin: “Just outside my town there were vast dairy farms, where I would spend my days as a boy fishing in creeks and ponds or climbing around in trees. Everything about the Catskills reminds me of my childhood. I don’t know what to say other than it’s my home.”

Ruffalo and Coigney were married in 2000 and welcomed the first of three children the following year. In December 2004, a few months before their second child was born, the growing family moved into a three-bedroom Hollywood Hills home that they bought for about $1.7 million. It was the cherry on top of a banner year for Ruffalo, who appeared in four movies that year: We Don’t Live Here Anymore, Eternal Sunshine of the Spotless Mind, 13 Going on 30, and Collateral.

According to The Los Angeles Times, the gated single-story residence dated to 1933 and was built in the style of a Connecticut farmhouse. Across 3,000 square feet, it hosted three bedrooms, four bathrooms, and a detached guesthouse. Highlights included a fireplace-warmed living room with beamed ceilings, and a breakfast room.

Ruffalo and Coigney welcomed their third child in 2007, then sold the Los Angeles house for $1.65 million two years later to New Girl star Zooey Deschanel and her then husband, Death Cab for Cutie frontman Ben Gibbard. Deschanel and Gibbard divorced in 2012, and Deschanel sold the property in 2015, the Los Angeles Times reported.

Ruffalo’s next real estate purchase was a $3.5 million town house in Brooklyn’s Carroll Gardens neighborhood in 2015. Spanning 3,000 square feet, the 1899-built residence had four bedrooms, three bathrooms, a backyard garden, and dreamy period details: cathedral ceilings, marble fireplaces, decorative arches, and wide-plank wood flooring. There was also a large kitchen with a high-end range, a sunroom with a balcony, and a curved wooden staircase leading to the upstairs bedrooms.

The actor sold the Brooklyn home in 2017 for $3.2 million—a slight loss.

Mark Ruffalo at a rally for the Landmark West Park Presbyterian Church in 2025

Though he left Brooklyn, Ruffalo was not leaving NYC altogether—just moving across the river to Manhattan. In January 2017, he and Coigney were seen touring a $9.99 million six-bedroom town house on the Upper West Side. While it doesn’t appear that they signed that deed, the couple did pick up a home in the neighborhood at some point thereafter. The Poor Things star has been a vocal supporter of upholding the landmark status of the neighborhood’s historic West Park Presbyterian Church since 2023. In December 2025, he said that he lived “a block away from the church.” While the details of this real estate transaction are unknown, Ruffalo’s Upper West Side residence is reportedly an early-20th-century four-story town house.

#4995. Letter From the Editor-in-Chief: Is Worrying About Sleep Keeping Us Awake?

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

As a busy, sleep-deprived, middle-aged father of two young kids, including an irrepressible 8 year old ball of energy who is, without fail, up and ready to take on the world at 6 o’clock every morning (usually with a steady stream of questions about dinosaurs, the natural world and the origins of the universe in his back pocket), do I really need another set of headlines informing me that not only am I falling short in getting the requisite 40 winks a night but I’m also at risk of 83 diseases into the bargain?

Another day, another headline-grabbing sleep study lands, this time linking a greater amount of sleep – specifically sleep of the rapid eye movement (REM) variety, also known as dream sleep – to a lower risk of dozens of diseases.

The recent study published in PLOS Medicine found that REM sleep is linked to a lower risk of 83 diseases, including dementia, Parkinson’s, and multiple sclerosis (MS). As an added bonus, the study also found that a higher amount of deep sleep was associated with a lower risk of seven health conditions, including depression and type 2 diabetes.

So far, so alarming for a self-confessed “bad sleeper”.

Yet also fairly unsurprising. The accepted science, combined with anecdotal experience, has long told us that the right amount of good quality sleep corresponds directly with improved short and long-term health outcomes, both mental and physical. Evidence suggests that 7-9 hours of sleep per night is typically the sweet spot for a healthier life. Where this particular study differs from those that have come before is that scientists used wrist trackers to gather much more detailed information over a multi-year timeline about participants’ sleep stages, including irregular sleep patterns and variations in sleep duration. The findings were drawn from real-world data from more than 95,000 participants in the UK Biobank database.

Now, as the aforementioned and self-proclaimed VBP (very busy person – if you know, you know), who works long hours across UK and US timezones for the day job whilst serving as a sub-par encyclopedia for the real boss (the aforementioned 8 year old) outside of work, the general demands and pressures of daily life are more than sufficient to make consistently good sleep something that is all too often out of reach.

I know this to be true because the wearable I use reminds me of the fact with cold, hard data on a daily, if not hourly, basis. One of the first things my wife and I do each morning (after fending off existential questions from a third grader) is hawkishly plug into our Oura app to trade sleep scores and discuss what did or didn’t contribute to our invariably bad or, very rarely, good night’s sleep. Deep sleep, REM sleep, resting heart rate, heart rate variability, latency, even sleep debt – a laundry list of data points that we pore over in a futile attempt to make our sleep consistency optimal whilst living a life that doesn’t readily allow for it. Scientists have even given this phenomenon a name – Orthosomnia – where the anxiety of analyzing and perfecting sleep scores actually disrupts rest and intensifies poor sleep, and in the most extreme cases, insomnia.

It turns out we’re not alone. Results of a Gallup poll from 2024 – with the rather on-the-nose headline “Americans sleeping less, more stressed” – showed Americans report averaging 6.5 hours of sleep per night, comfortably below the recommended baseline of 7+ hours. Perhaps more tellingly, in a survey from 2024, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine found that nearly 70% of Americans report losing sleep due to anxiety.

Which brings me back to my original point. Anxiety-induced stress makes for the perfect bedfellow (pun intended) for a bad night’s sleep. The kind of anxiety-induced stress that can be both caused and amplified by obsessing over wearables data and a steady torrent of sleep-related content that aims to scaremonger rather than inform.

In fairness to the UK Biobank study, it is unequivocally important and unique research steeped in actionable real-world data and peer-reviewed medical science. And yet we live in a world increasingly awash with AI-generated content, sporting sensationalist headlines and powered by algorithms designed with an inherent imbalance to engage rather than empower. In that environment, gold-standard scientific work like this is all too often reduced to the lowest common denominator.

If you’ll indulge a minor tooting of our own horn here, this is precisely the inverse of how the team of wonderfully talented editorial misfits at Healthline Media thinks about and creates health information. We take research-backed science and our embedded clinical rigor and apply it to every aspect of our work. But we never lose sight of the fact that behind every study, there’s someone lying awake in the early hours running the math on their own risk factors, and that what they need in that moment is recognition that we are all at different stages on that journey, and a helping hand along the path to better health.

Life isn’t linear, sleep isn’t linear, and health is certainly not linear. Some nights I’ll get my seven hours. Plenty of others I won’t. And that is ok. Because either way, my alarm clock has a favorite dinosaur and a nonnegotiable 6 a.m. start time, and no peer-reviewed study or scary headline is going to change that. So I’ll take the data and the empathy, leave the anxiety, and see you all again at dawn.

If there’s something keeping you awake at night, or you just want to learn more about the wonderful world of sleep, we have a wealth of science-backed information at our dedicated Sleep Health hub. There you can also find Healthline’s Better Sleep Challenge – a three-week series designed to help you sleep better, feel more rested, and wake up ready to thrive each day.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#4990. FDA Issues Class II Blood Pressure Medication Recall, Over 13,000 Bottles Affected

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Some people taking a common blood pressure medication may have pills that don’t dissolve the way they should.More than 13,500 bottles of 25 mg chlorthalidone tablets are under a nationwide recall after failing lab tests, according to the Food and Drug Administration (FDA).Inventia Healthcare Limited voluntarily recalled the tablets on September 3, 2026, according to the FDA’s enforcement report.

The report lists the reason for the recall as “Failed Dissolution Specifications.” That means the tablets failed a test that measures how the medicine is released from the pill, according to the FDA.“Chlorthalidone is a diuretic, or ‘water pill,’ commonly used to treat high blood pressure and reduce excess fluid retention in the body,” Cheng-Han Chen, MD, a board certified interventional cardiologist at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, told Healthline.This is the second time this year Inventia has recalled its 25 mg chlorthalidone tablets.

The first recall was initiated on June 5 and covered 11,460 bottles. Both were for the same reason: failed dissolution specifications, according to FDA enforcement reports.

Together, the June and September recalls cover 25,027 bottles.

The FDA classified both recalls as Class II.

The two recalls cover three batches of the chlorthalidone tablets, USP, 25 mg, according to the FDA enforcement reports. All three expire in April 2027.

The 25 mg tablets are light yellow, round and flat, with “103” stamped on one side and nothing on the other, according to the drug label.

Recall initiated on September 3, 2026; 13,567 bottles affected.

Recall initiated on June 5, 2026; 11,460 bottles affected.

Patients whose supply is part of the recall “should contact their pharmacy to discuss obtaining a replacement and ask whether they should continue taking the medication in the meantime,” Chen said.

For Class II recalls, people can generally keep taking the medicine unless the recalling company gives other instructions, according to the FDA.

In some cases, stopping a medication may be more harmful than continuing to take the recalled medicine, the agency said.

If you’re concerned that your medication has been recalled, here’s what you can do:

A Class II recall means using the drug may cause temporary health problems, but the chance of serious harm is unlikely, according to the FDA.

“The voluntary recalls cited are classified as Class II, at the retail level,” an FDA Spokesperson told Healthline.

A retail-level recall means the company is “removing the product from retail stores and pharmacy shelves,” Ileana Elder, a branch chief in the FDA’s Office of Compliance, said in an FDA podcast. When a recall doesn’t extend to consumers, stopping a medication unnecessarily can be more harmful, and patients should contact their pharmacy with questions, Elder said.Healthline reached out to the companies for more information about the September recall, but had not heard back by the time of publication. The FDA referred further questions to the companies

However, based on a preliminary assessment of the batches recalled in June, Rising Pharma Holdings, a distributor of one of the recalled lots, stated that “no impact on patient health and safety is anticipated.”

“If a medication does not dissolve at the expected rate, it may not be absorbed by the body as intended,” Chen said. “This can reduce the amount of medication that reaches the bloodstream and potentially decrease its effectiveness.”

In a dissolution test, a lab places a tablet in a controlled liquid medium to determine whether and how quickly the active ingredient is released, according to FDA guidance on dissolution testing.

A failed result means the batch didn’t meet the release standard set for that product, according to FDA guidance on drug quality standards.Manufacturers propose and justify these standards, and regulators approve them as part of drug approval, according to the same guidance.

In the United States, the official standards stay the same from the time a drug is released until it expires, the guidance said.The September FDA enforcement report doesn’t say how the recalled tablets failed. A notice from Rising Pharma Holdings about the June recall provides more details.

Rising found two problems at the 18-month mark of ongoing quality testing of its 25 mg tablets, the company said in a notice posted by the California State Board of Pharmacy:

According to the drug label, chlorthalidone treats:

The label recommends that most people with high blood pressure start with one 25 mg dose per day. That’s the strength involved in both 2026 recalls.

#4989. ‘Scromiting’ and Other Super Scary Symptoms of Taking High-THC Weed

Yes, that’s vomiting and screaming at the same time.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

HIGH-CONCENTRATION SYNTHETIC THC products are producing wild reactions in some people. Kevin Gray, MD, a practicing physician and director of addiction sciences at Medical University of South Carolina, helps break them down.

“You experience hallucinations, paranoia, delusions,” says Dr. Gray. “It’s become undeniable looking at the association between teen cannabis use and psychosis that if somebody has some kind of already innate risk for developing a psychotic disorder, there’s something about use of cannabis during this stage of development that [may trigger the onset of] a lifelong disorder.” (The risk of someone developing the condition is less than 1 percent.)

“Paranoia is a symptom of psychosis. Instead of feeling the usual sort of high and calming feeling with cannabis, people feel more detached and more suspicious. Sometimes there will be a very high concentration or some unusual strain that may be more likely to lead to paranoia. The effect can be intense, but it’s transient and wears off with time.”

That’s screaming and vomiting at the same time. “Cyclic vomiting, cannabis-related emesis—I don’t see it that often among my clients, but it happens,” says Dr. Gray. “THC can reduce nausea, but there’s something about the interaction between THC and the systems that control our nausea that goes haywire in some people. We don’t know even how to treat it symptomatically, other than taking showers.” So if you totally misread a gummy label, just call 911—no judgment.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

#4988. 7 Best Lumbar Pillows Bolster Your Back and Bedscape (2026)

Though a lumbar pillow is typically the last detail in making a bed, it’s often the first thing you or a guest will notice. Because it’s long and low, its best suited to the space in front of loftier throw pillows, making a horizon line across your bedscape, and it can bring in a color or texture the rest of the room is vying for. The shape turns up all over AD’s archives, including in supermodel Paloma Elsesser’s Brooklyn home and this classically decorated Southern house.

A lumbar pillow can mean two things: a firm or cushioned gel pillow designed to support the lower back, or an elegant decorative detail among your bedding. While we like both, we’ll leave the Cushion Lab’s best-of for another day. Here, we’re focused on a more inspirational lineup, between linen, wool, velvet, and cotton lumbar pillows that pull a bed and sofa together (and still give your lower back something to lean on in an accent chair). Below are the seven we like best.

Sometimes one pillow is all a bed really needs. West Elm’s oversized linen pillow cover has the scale to anchor a queen or king bed on its own, creating a clean, pulled-together look without a full stack of throw pillows. The linen keeps it loose, making it especially well-suited to coastal, minimalist, modern farmhouse, and casual contemporary spaces.

This pillow is all about texture. Its dimensional surface gives a sofa or bed a more layered, considered look without relying on a loud print or saturated color. In other words, its style will speak volumes even when paired with quieter neutrals.

At 48 inches, Quince’s velvet lumbar is long enough to span a queen bed with nothing else in front of it, and the cotton pile catches light in a way linen won’t. The glacier blue reads cool and a little silvery against white or oatmeal bedding. It’s dry clean only, and the insert is sold separately.

Color is the point with this Athena cushion. Rather than fading quietly into the background, this lumbar pillow is designed to bring energy to a room, whether it’s breaking up the monotony of a neutral living room or adding contrast to a more restrained bedding scheme. It’s the kind of accent that makes a pillow arrangement feel more collected, playful, and intentional.

A little color can go a long way, especially in the form of Pendleton’s Turquoise Crosses Lumbar Pillow. Its vivid palette and geometric pattern give neutral furniture or a plain accent chair an instant lift. For anyone drawn to Southwestern-inspired textiles, this pick brings just enough graphic punch without crowding the rest of the room.

The Mendocino cover is made up of hand-dyed washed linen, so the color shifts slightly across the surface instead of sitting flat. What separates it from the other linen picks here is the fringe: three-inch knotted tassels run along two sides, which give a simple lumbar some movement at the ends of a sofa or bed. Nota bene: the insert is sold separately.

Washed linen has a way of making a room feel more relaxed and breezy, which is exactly the appeal of Brooklinen’s pillow cover. The simple design is easy to fold into an existing pillow arrangement, while the softly textured fabric and choice of vibrant hues keep it from feeling too plain.

A lumbar pillow is a long, rectangular cushion designed to support the lower back. The term describes two different products: one is the ergonomic back support pillow, often memory foam, made for an office chair or car seat. The other is the decorative version on this list, which works as an accent piece on beds, sofas, benches, and accent chairs. Their shape makes them especially useful for layering with standard square throw pillows, adding dimension without making a space feel cluttered.

One of the easiest ways to style a lumbar pillow is to place it in front of larger pillows or shams on a bed. On a sofa or accent chair, a lumbar pillow can serve as a single focal point or be paired with smaller throw pillows in complementary colors, patterns, or textures. For a more polished look, choose a lumbar pillow that either picks up a color already in the room or brings in a contrasting texture, like linen, wool, velvet, or tassels.

For lower back support, a lumbar pillow goes in the curve between your back and the back of a sofa, reading chair, or recliner chair. Keep in mind a firmer, fuller back pillow will likely do more for your posture than a soft, decorative one. For styling, it can also be centered across a bed, layered in front of sleeping pillows, or placed on a bench for extra comfort and visual interest. Oversized lumbar pillows work especially well on queen and king beds because they can anchor the entire pillow arrangement with one clean horizontal line.

#4987. 30 Years of Legal Weed: Has It Made Us Any Healthier?

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The very highs and bizarre lows of America on medicinal marijuana.

EARLIER THIS YEAR, during a brief and mercifully abandoned period when I was considering doing stand-up as a hobby, I went to a comedy show in downtown Austin. I was legitimately shocked to find, as part of the two-drink minimum bill of fare, the option for several different flavors of $5 THC seltzers. This wasn’t “take a pull off your vape or smoke a roach in the alley before the show” territory. No, marijuana drinks were on the menu.

In the past few years, my only social activities have been poker, competitive trivia, and going to the movies, all activities people do stoned, but not activities where you can literally order drugs from the bar alongside your chicken wings. The comedy club, on the other hand, considered marijuana products as a profit center.

This was one of those lightly attended weeknight shows where the comedians banter with the audience because they haven’t prepared much material. So, during a Q&A, I brought up the fact that there were THC sodas available to order, in laminate. “Sir, this is 2026,” the host said.

This is where we are now, a full three decades after California legalized medical weed. But is that good for us?

As I write this, it’s been eight-and-a-half years since I quit marijuana, a drug that at one point made pretty much anything—listening to music, shopping for groceries, cleaning the house—fun for me.

But really, I’ve been sober since I stopped using THC, since I was hardly just smoking there at the end. I was vaping, eating, drinking, dabbing, and otherwise gorging myself on what has become an extremely potent, psychosis-inducing drug. And I wasn’t having fun.

My breaking point, which I recounted in my 2020 memoir Pothead, came at Game 2 of the 2017 World Series at Dodger Stadium, where I was so wasted on weed that I had a screaming meltdown in front of security when I found out the ticket I’d bought was fraudulent.

It was a low point, a bottom so deep and so pathetic that I should have quit right there. But I went on for another three weeks before I finally crawled to a Marijuana Anonymous meeting and asked for help.

Now, nearly a decade later, I’ve emerged from my cocoon to find that THC is everywhere. I live in a state where it’s still technically illegal, but you can find some sort of THC variant in most convenience stores and in most bars and nightclubs.

My friend is an attorney who represents the Texas Alcoholic Beverage Commission. He says his client is in a near panic. Sales are down for every beverage except for nonalcoholic mixers and those THC seltzers. THC is the beer, wine, and vodka of the present day, combined.

And so we find ourselves in a bizarre situation where people are drinking way less alcohol because it’s unhealthy for you, but they are instead ingesting THC products—either through soda, gummies, or any number of other concoctions—from unknown manufacturers, with unknown formulas and malleable dosages.

They’re also vaping, which emerging research is showing is kind of awful for our lungs. A University of Michigan study recently showed that teens who vaped cannabis were twice as likely to experience respiratory symptoms like “wheezing or whistling” in the chest than those who vaped nicotine or smoked traditional cigarettes.

Unlike traditional marijuana, this stuff isn’t “from the earth.” Who knows where it’s really from—or where it’s going?

When I quit weed in 2017, and somewhat loudly and publicly announced myself as an addict, I got the requisite amount of sympathy, but also a tremendous amount of pushback. Marijuana isn’t dangerous, people said. It isn’t addictive. It’s actually good for you.

In 2016, Gavin Newsom, then California’s lieutenant governor, spearheaded the legalization push for recreational marijuana. Cannabis sales have produced more than $8 billion in tax revenue for California since retail sales began in 2018, with a decent proportion of that funneled into teen substance-abuse prevention programs. But the state has also seen an increase in children suffering from cannabis poisoning.

It’s been a massive shift, moving from medically administered weed for veterans and cancer patients to powerful THC beverages you can buy at your bodega.

Legalization has created a new frontier of intoxicants without a centralized warning system for users. Data from a 2025 national study of U.S. adults stated that 15 percent of respondents reported daily or near-daily cannabis use.

Among those daily users, 18 percent reported experiencing symptoms consistent with cannabinoid hyperemesis syndrome, a disorder characterized by cyclic, severe nausea, intractable vomiting, and gut pain alleviated temporarily by hot baths or showers.

This translates to an estimated 7.2 million people in the United States alone, many of them teenagers, experiencing these symptoms. That number is probably vastly underestimated, given that fewer than 12 percent of sufferers have received a formal diagnosis.

Welcome to the darker side of legalized weed, a world of cannabis-induced psychosis, “scromiting” (yes, screaming while vomiting), and concoctions like “shatter” or “wax,” which contain up to 90 percent THC, as opposed to the 3 to 5 percent in joints from previous decades.

So I find myself in a somewhat odd situation. I was a relatively early victim of modern weed’s potency, as well as a relatively early unadopter, and a very early voice in the wilderness warning the world that marijuana might not be the answer to all our health concerns. But now I find myself, like a patient waking up from a long coma, in a world where THC is as prevalent as, and cheaper than, whiskey. Yet still, I find myself thinking it should be legal. So which is it? Maybe we still don’t have enough data.

Thirty years since the start of legalization might feel like a long time, but it’s really not. In terms of scientific analysis, we’re still in relatively undiscovered country. If you’re suffering from chronic pain, or dying of cancer, marijuana is a healthier option than, say, oxycodone. But for the rest of us? No one really knows.

The only thing I can be truly sure of is that it’s no longer for me.

#4986. Skeleton Architecture: 7 Buildings That Prove Why Bones Are Some Designer’s Favorite Muse

“It should be harsh and cruel, as if made of bones,” Antoni Gaudí wrote next to his sketch of the Passion Façade, the west-facing entrance of his magnum opus, the Sagrada Família. In stark contrast to the organic, pictorial design of the Nativity Façade on the opposite side of the basilica, which depicts the birth of Jesus, Gaudí employed skeletal forms to evoke human suffering and Christ’s final days.

While the Catalan architect drew inspiration from skeletons for multiple projects—most notably Barcelona’s Casa Batlló, a residential building known as the House of Bones for its dragon-vertebrae-inspired design, and Casa Milà, with its undulating, rib cage–like arches—he was not the first to do so. Skeletons have long served as sacred ornamentation across cultures, representing the transience of life. As early as 7,200 BCE, Middle Eastern communities displayed human skulls in domestic spaces to honor their ancestors, while in Mesoamerica, the Aztecs erected elaborate skull racks known as tzompantli to display sacrificial captives’ remains as a demonstration of power. Following the Black Death in medieval Europe, memento mori, symbolic reminders of death’s inevitability, further ubiquitized skulls in art and architecture. Meanwhile, as European cemeteries became increasingly inundated from the Middle Ages into the early modern period, carcasses were exhumed and relocated to crypts featuring skeletal decorative displays, such as Portugal’s 16th-century Capela dos Ossos and the Czech Republic’s Sedlec Ossuary.

By the late 19th and early 20th centuries, architects such as Antoni Gaudí began studying skeletal anatomy not merely for adornment, but as the actual framework for buildings. After all, much like the human body, a building relies on its skeleton as the foundation for life within. From an avant-garde temple to to contemporary buildings constructed to resemble skeletal forms, below are architectural marvels that reveal why skeletons remain an enduring muse for architects.

While historic ossuaries incorporated actual human remains into their architecture, contemporary designer and artist Chalermchai Kositpipat incorporated ornamental skeletal architecture into Wat Rong Khun, or the White Temple, an unconventional Buddhist temple and art installation that depicts the stages of Nirvana. To enter the temple, visitors must cross a bridge over a sea of sculpted hands and skulls, symbolizing human desire and the cycle of suffering on the path to enlightenment. Started in 1997 and still evolving, the complex features intricate white ornamentation and mirrored glass symbolizing purity and wisdom.

Moving beyond skeletal ornamentation, Antoni Gaudí helped pioneer Catalan Modernism, a late 19th- and early 20th-century architectural movement that challenged traditional academicism, rejecting rigid geometry in favor of nature’s organic forms and structural principles, including those of skeletal anatomy. Nicknamed the House of Bones, Gaudí’s Casa Batlló, which was created from 1904–1906, is among the most prominent examples of this movement. Its undulating façade features balconies resembling skulls, slender bone-like columns, and a roof covered in iridescent scales that evokes a dragon’s spine. Originally designed as a private residence for textile industrialist Josep Batlló, the UNESCO-listed building is now a museum open to visitors, its skeletal framework inspiring interpretations ranging from human anatomy to the legend of Saint George slaying the dragon.

Built between 1906 and 1912, another of Gaudí’s architectural masterpieces, Casa Milà, also known as La Pedrera, draws inspiration from natural forms, most notably in what’s known as the Whale-Bone Attic, where 270 repeating brick arches expand and contract in size to resemble the rib cage of a giant whale. These catenary arches support the building’s rippling roof, demonstrating Gaudí’s fascination with the relationship between anatomy and engineering. More broadly, Gaudí conceived the building as an animal body, with internal supporting columns serving as its bones and the sculptural stone façade as its skin. Originally constructed as a residential apartment building, the UNESCO World Heritage Site remains partly residential, while its attic, rooftop with surreal sculptural chimneys, and other historic spaces are open to visitors.

Designed by Spanish architect Santiago Calatrava and completed in 1994, the Lyon-Saint Exupéry Airport Railway Station combines skeletal engineering with the imagery of a bird taking flight. “Sometimes I say [I’m inspired by] the human skeleton, because it’s true that there’s something essential in the construction of our body,” Calatrava once told Fast Company in 2010. (He’s also inspired by nature more broadly, he added.)

This idea is on full display at the Lyon-Saint Exupéry Airport Railway Station, where the expansive central hall features a soaring steel framework of intersecting arches, while the adjoining railway platforms are sheltered beneath a vaulted concrete roof supported by rib-like structures. The building’s curved, tapering spine and wing-shaped silhouette evoke avian anatomy.

Another Calatrava design, the Quadracci Pavilion at the Milwaukee Art Museum, completed in 2001, emulates a mythical winged skeletal form. Its soaring Windhover Hall features repeating white concrete ribs, pointed arches, and a 90-foot-high glass ceiling, evoking a Gothic cathedral with the anatomy of a fantastical creature. Outside, the building’s most recognizable feature, the Burke Brise Soleil, consists of 72 steel fins that open and close like enormous wings, spanning 217 feet when fully extended.

The World Trade Center Oculus resembles an enormous skeletal structure through its repeating white steel ribs, which extend outward to form a pair of wing-like canopies. Another Calatrava project, the architect conceived it as a symbol of hope and renewal following the September 11 attacks—the transportation hub was inspired by the image of a bird being released from a child’s hands. Inside, its soaring, cathedral-like hall features an elliptical framework of steel and glass, with a central skylight that floods the space with natural light. The structure’s combination of skeletal anatomy and avian symbolism is one of the most renowned projects embodying Calatrava’s exploration of biological forms in architecture.

The 61,000 meter headquarters for Chinese fashion company Xinhee, designed by Beijing-based MAD Architects and opened in 2017, conjures the architecture of the human body. Conceived of as a structure of “skin and bones,” the building features a diagonal steel skeleton enveloped in translucent PTFE membrane resembling fabric draped over the human body, while also filtering natural light and facilitating natural ventilation in the subtropical climate. Its flower-like layout consists of six radiating wings, each housing a different atrium, which are connected by a suspended footbridge that doubles as a fashion runway. With its elevated gardens and multistory green spaces, the structure integrates nature into the building’s skeletal framework. Known as one of the most innovative buildings in contemporary China, the design personifies the relationship between fashion and architecture.
